\theta_{N-3}

(b)

Figure 3
.
(A) The directed, colored (and labeled) graph
G
associated to half-space log-gamma
\Theta
-Gibbs measures. A few of the vertices of
G
have their
\phi
-induced index (i.e., the coordinates above the vertex), and a few of the blue edges are labeled by the
\vartheta_{i,j}
parameters. A generic bounded connected domain
\Lambda
is shown in the figure which contains all vertices in the shaded region.
\partial\Lambda
consists of white vertices in the figure. (B) The domain
K_{N}
considered in Theorem
1.3
.
\Lambda_{N}^{*}
consists of vertices in the shaded region. The assignment
\vartheta_{i,j}=\theta_{N-i+1}
of
\Theta
parameters from Theorem
1.3
as shown here over the blue edges.

We next define a bijection
\phi:V(G)\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{2}
by
\phi((m,n))=(-\lfloor n\rfloor,m)
. This pushes the directed/colored edges in
G
onto directed/colored edges on
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{2}
which we denote by
E(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{2})
. We will always view
G
as in Figure
3
and will use the
\phi
-induced indexing when describing this graph. As in Figure
3
(B), set
\Lambda_{N}^{*}:=\{(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{2}:i\in[1,N-1],j\in[1,2N-2i+1]\}
.

We associate to each
e\in E(\mathbb{Z}^{2})
a weight function based on the color of edge defined as follows:

(1.5)

\displaystyle W_{e}(x):=\begin{cases}\exp(\vartheta x-e^{x})&\mbox{ if $e$ is {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{blue}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{$(\vartheta)$}},\\
\exp(-e^{x})&\mbox{ if $e$ is {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{black}}},\\
\exp(-\alpha x)&\mbox{ if $e$ is {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{red}},}\end{cases}

Definition 1.2

(Half-space log-gamma
\Theta
-Gibbs measure)
.

Fix any
\Theta:=\{\vartheta_{m,n}>0\mid(m,n)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{2}\}
. Consider the graph
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{2}
endowed with directed/colored edges
E(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{2})
as above. Let
\Lambda
be a bounded connected subset of
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{2}
. Set

\partial\Lambda:=\big\{v\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{2}\cap\Lambda^{c}:\{v^{\prime}\to v\}\in E(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{2})\mbox{ or }\{v\to v^{\prime}\}\in E(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{2}),\mbox{ for some }v^{\prime}\in\Lambda\big\}.

The half-space log-gamma (
\mathpzc{HSLG}
)
\Theta
-Gibbs measure for the domain
\Lambda
, with boundary condition
\big(u_{i,j}\in\mathbb{R}:(i,j)\in\partial\Lambda\big)
, is a measure on
\mathbb{R}^{|\Lambda|}
with density at
(u_{i,j})_{(i,j)\in\Lambda}
proportional to

(1.6)

\displaystyle\prod_{e=\{v_{1}\to v_{2}\}\in E(\Lambda\cup\partial\Lambda)}W_{e}(u_{v_{1}}-u_{v_{2}}).

Lemma
B.2
shows that the
\mathpzc{HSLG}

\Theta
-Gibbs measure is well-defined. When all
\vartheta_{m,n}
are equal to a generic parameter
\theta>0
, we shall simply call the corresponding measure as
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs measure.

Notationally, we will generally use
u_{v}
for vertices
v=(i,j)\in\Lambda
as dummy-variables when discussing the density of
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs measures. When discussing multivariate random variables distributed jointly according to a
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs measure we will typically write
L_{i}(j)
, or sometimes
L(v)
for
v=(i,j)
, for the
(i,j)
coordinate of these multivariate random variables.

An event
A
, i.e., elements of the Borel
\sigma
-algebra for
\mathbb{R}^{|\Lambda|}
, is
increasing
if it satisfies the condition that
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\in A
implies
\mathbf{u}\in A
provided
\mathbf{u}\preceq\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
. Here
\mathbf{u}=\big(u_{i,j}\big)_{(i,j)\in\Lambda}
,
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}=\big(u^{\prime}_{i,j}\big)_{(i,j)\in\Lambda}
and
\mathbf{u}\preceq\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
if
u_{i,j}\leq u_{i,j}^{\prime}
for all
(i,j)\in\Lambda
. An event is
decreasing
if
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\in A
implies
\mathbf{u}\in A
provided
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\preceq\mathbf{u}

The following shows how the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
free energy process can be embedded in a
\mathpzc{HSLG}

\Theta
-Gibbs measure. Its proof in Section
2.2
relies on results of
[
BW22
]
that build on the analysis of the log-gamma polymer via the geometric RSK correspondence
[
COSZ14
]
on symmetrized domains
[
OSZ14
,
NZ17
,
BZ19
]
. In Section
2.2
, for each
N>0
, we will define explicitly such a choice for
\big(\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}(j):(i,j)\in\mathcal{K}_{N}\big)
that will satisfy the two criterion of the theorem. We will call this the half-space log-gamma (
\mathpzc{HSLG}
) line ensemble. We will use
\mathcal{L}
when discussing the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
line ensemble, while
L
will be used when discussing general line ensembles that enjoy the
\mathpzc{HSLG}

\Theta
-Gibbs property.

Theorem 1.3

(Half-space log-gamma line ensemble)
.

Let
\vec{\theta}=(\theta_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}}
be a sequence of positive parameters. Fix
\alpha>-\theta
where
\theta:=\min\{\theta_{i}:i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\}
, and
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. As in Figure
3
(B), set
\mathcal{K}_{N}:=\{(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{2}:i\in[1,N],j\in[1,2N-2i+2]\}
. There exists random variables
\big(\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}(j):(i,j)\in\mathcal{K}_{N}\big)
, called here the
\mathpzc{HSLG}

\Theta
-line ensemble, on a common probability space such:

(i)

We have the following equality in distribution

(1.7)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(2j+1))_{j\in\llbracket 0,N-1\rrbracket}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}\big(\log Z_{(\alpha,\vec{\theta})}(N+j,N-j)+2N\Psi(\theta)\big)_{j\in\llbracket 0,N-1\rrbracket}.

(ii)

The law of
\big(\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}(j):(i,j)\in\Lambda_{N}^{*}\big)
conditioned on
\big(\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}(j):(i,j)\in(\Lambda_{N}^{*})^{c}\big)
is given by the
\mathpzc{HSLG}

\Theta
-Gibbs measure for the domain
\Lambda_{N}^{*}
with boundary condition
\big(\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}(j):(i,j)\in\partial\Lambda_{N}^{*}\big).
Here the parameters in
\Theta
are chosen as
\vartheta_{i,j}:=\theta_{N-i+1}
, see the blue edge labeling in Figure
3
(B).

In the homogeneous case we set all
\theta_{i}\equiv\theta
.

Remark 1.4
.

Theorem
1.3
is stated for the polymer model using the inhomogeneous weights in (
1.2
). In the homogeneous case (which we will focus upon here) where
\theta_{i}\equiv\theta
(and hence
\vartheta_{i,j}\equiv\theta
) the
2N\Psi(\theta)
centering term in (
1.7
) is chosen to be adapted to our ultimate goal of taking scaling limits. However, this terms inclusion is ultimately inconsequential since it constitutes a constant shift of the Gibbs measures which does not impact the Gibbs property (see Lemma
2.1
(a)
).

We assume below that we are dealing with the homogeneous case of the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
line ensemble.

It is useful to view
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs measures (in particular we focus here on the Gibbs measures from Theorem
1.3
) in terms of the language of Gibbsian line ensembles. Consider
k
and
T
fixed and
N
sufficiently large so that all of the random variables
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}\llbracket 1,T\rrbracket,\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}\llbracket 1,T\rrbracket,\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{2k}^{N}\llbracket 1,T\rrbracket
are defined. We will think of
\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}
as the label
i
‘line’ (rather, a piecewise linearly interpolated curve) in the ensemble. The values of
\big(\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}(2T+1):i\in\llbracket 1,2k\rrbracket\big)
and
\mathcal{L}_{2k+1}^{N}(\cdot)
constitute boundary data which, once known, uniquely identify (via the Gibbs description) the laws of
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}\llbracket 1,T\rrbracket,\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}\llbracket 1,T\rrbracket,\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{2k}^{N}\llbracket 1,T\rrbracket
.

Let us consider the three types of weights in the Gibbs measure. The weights corresponding to
black
edges
v_{1}\to v_{2}
contribute a factor of
e^{-e^{u_{v_{1}}-u_{v_{2}}}}
(here
u_{v}
is the dummy variable in the Gibbs density corresponding to a vertex
v
) in the Gibbs measure. Whenever
u_{v_{1}}\gg u_{v_{2}}
, this weight is very close to 0, whereas when
u_{v_{1}}\ll u_{v_{2}}
the weight is close to 1 (between, there is a smooth monotone transition from
0
to
1
). Thus, this weight produces a soft version of conditioning on the event that
\mathcal{L}^{N}(v_{2})\geq\mathcal{L}^{N}(v_{1})
(recall the notational convention for a line ensemble that
L(v)=L_{i}(j)
where
v=(i,j)
).
Black
edges arise between consecutive lines thus we expect that our measure will strongly favor configurations where
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(\cdot)\gtrsim\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(\cdot)\gtrsim\mathcal{L}_{3}^{N}(\cdot)\gtrsim\cdots
, i.e., the curves are non-intersecting up to some error (Theorem
3.1
provides a precise statement substantiating this). Of course, the soft nature of this conditioning will not rule out crossing, but a heavy penalty will be incurred so at a heuristic level it is useful to think in terms of non-intersecting lines.

The

red
edges are
(2i-1,1)\to(2i,1)
and come with a weight
e^{-\alpha(u_{2i-1,1}-u_{2i,1})}
. This weight is close to 0 when
u_{2i-1,1}\gg u_{2i,1}
(since
\alpha>0
). This creates an attractive force between
\mathcal{L}_{2i-1}^{N}(1)
and
\mathcal{L}_{2i}^{N}(1)
which tries to establish the ordering
\mathcal{L}_{2i-1}^{N}(1)\leq\mathcal{L}_{2i}^{N}(1)
. Of course, this is in opposition to the soft non-intersecting influence already discussed. Combined, these forces ultimately (through our analysis of weighted paired random walks) result in the difference
\mathcal{L}_{2i-1}^{N}(1)-\mathcal{L}_{2i}^{N}(1)=O(1)
as
N\to\infty
. In contrast, in the critical regime, when
\alpha_{N}=N^{-1/3}\mu
, the attraction weakens with
N
and the forces result in
\mathcal{L}_{2i-1}^{N}(1)-\mathcal{L}_{2i}^{N}(1)=O(N^{1/3})
. It is the
O(1)
distance between
\mathcal{L}_{2i-1}^{N}(1)
and
\mathcal{L}_{2i}^{N}(1)
that makes the supercritical case harder than the critical case.

Finally, consider the

blue
edges that encode the Gibbs weights between consecutive values of a given line, i.e. between
\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}(j)
and
\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}(j+1)
. Alone, these weights define log-gamma increment random walks (with two-step periodicity in the law of the increments). Thus, putting these three factors together one arrives at the picture illustrated in Figure
2
(A) – an ensemble of softly non-intersecting log-gamma random walks with starting points
O(1)
distance apart between the curves labeled
2i-1
and
2i
for each relevant
i
.
In order to prove Theorem
1.1
we essentially need to justify the distance scales in Figure
2
(A). To do that, we use the Gibbs property for the line ensemble described above along with some one-point control over
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}
that we describe now.

1.3.2.
Point-to-line free energy fluctuations

The
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs measures machinery gives us access to the behavior of the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
line ensemble conditioned on the boundary data. However, we still need to understand the behavior of the boundary data. The theory of (full-space) Gibbsian line ensembles that has been developed over the last decade has become proficient at taking very minimal seed information, such as the scale in which tightness occurs for the one-point fluctuations of the top curve of a Gibbsian line ensemble, and outputting the scaling and tightness for the entire edge of the line ensemble. We take the first step in developing such a half-space theory.

There are currently only two fluctuation results about the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
polymer. The first (chronologically) is a result of
[
BW22
]
that we will recall below and appeal to, while the second is the work of
[
IMS22
]
that proves a limit theorem for
N^{-1/3}\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(1)
(i.e.
\mathcal{F}_{N}^{\alpha}(0)
). Our work began prior to the release of
[
IMS22
]
and thus we rely only on the work of
[
BW22
]
. The control
[
IMS22
]
provides is for
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(1)
only and since we need some information away from the boundary too, most of the work herein is unavoidable and not significantly simplified by using
[
IMS22
]
. It is natural to wonder if
[
IMS22
]
could have been used alone, in place of
[
BW22
]
, at the seed for our analysis. While we do not rule this out, it would certainly require a very different type of argument since we rely heavily on the fact that
[
BW22
]
provides some information about
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(j)
as
j
varies.

We recall the result of
[
BW22
]
. For each
k>0
, define the point-to-(partial)line partition function

(1.8)

\displaystyle Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}(k):=\sum_{j=\lceil k\rceil}^{N}Z_{(\alpha,\theta)}(N+j,N-j).

This sum is restricted to endpoints at least distance
2k
from the boundary.
Set
p=\frac{N+k}{N-k}
, Let
\theta_{c}
be the unique solution to
\Psi^{\prime}(\theta_{c})=p\Psi^{\prime}(2\theta-\theta_{c})
and set (recall the digamma function
\Psi
from (
1.4
))

\displaystyle f_{\theta,p}:=-\Psi(\theta_{c})-p\Psi(2\theta-\theta_{c}),\quad\sigma_{\theta,p}:=\bigg(\tfrac{1}{2}(-\Psi^{\prime\prime}(\theta_{c})-p\psi^{\prime\prime}(2\theta-\theta_{c})\bigg)^{1/3}.

Theorem 1.5

(Theorem 1.10 in
[
BW22
]
)
.

Suppose
(k_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}
is such that for some
y\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
,
\lim_{N\to\infty}(N-k_{N})^{1/3}\sigma_{\theta,p}(\alpha+\theta-\theta_{c})=y
. Then, as
N\to\infty

\displaystyle\frac{\log Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}(k_{N})-(N-k_{N})f_{\theta,p}}{(N-k_{N})^{1/3}\sigma_{\theta,p}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{\Longrightarrow}}U_{-y}.

where for
y\in\mathbb{R}
,
U_{-y}
is distributed as the Baik-Ben Arous-Péché distribution with parameter
y
(see Eq. (5.2) in
[
BW22
]
). When
y=\infty
,
U_{-\infty}
is distributed as the GUE Tracy-Widom distribution.

The crucial deduction from Theorem
1.5
is that there exists
\nu>0
such that for each
M>0
,

(1.9)

\displaystyle V_{N}(M)+M^{2}\xRightarrow[N\to\infty]{(d)}X_{M},\quad\mbox{where}\quad V_{N}(M):=\frac{\log Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}(MN^{2/3})+2\Psi(\theta)N}{N^{1/3}\nu}.

Here the BBP distributions of the limiting random variables
(X_{M})_{M>0}
form a tight sequence in
M
, in particular they converge in law to the GUE Tracy-Widom distribution as
M\to\infty
. A precise version of this deduction in given later in Lemma
3.7
.
Essentially, the rescaled point-to-(partial)line free energy process
V_{N}(M)
looks like an inverted parabola
-M^{2}
with tight fluctuations around it.

1.3.3.
Using the Gibbs line ensemble structure to prove Theorem
1.1

We now give a brief overview of the steps of our proof and how it relies on combining the seed information from
[
BW22
]
, i.e. (
1.9
), and the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
line ensemble Gibbs property. Fixing a bit of notation, we will say that a sequence of random variables
X_{N}
is
upper-tight
if
\max(X_{N},0)
is tight, and
lower-tight
if
\min(X_{N},0)
is tight. Recall that
X_{N}
is tight if for all
\varepsilon>0
there exists
K=K(\varepsilon)>0
such that
\mathbb{P}(|X_{N}|\geq K)<\varepsilon
for all
N\geq N_{0}
. If
X_{N}
is both upper and lower tight, then it is tight.

We sketch the proof of the main theorem for
r=1
(
r
is as in the statement of the theorem). Fix any
N_{0}
large enough so everything below is well-defined for
N\geq N_{0}
. We consider a time

(1.10)

T=8\lfloor N^{2/3}\rfloor

(the key point is that time window
[0,T]
scales like
N^{2/3}
).
By virtue of the relation (
1.7
) in Theorem
1.3

(i)
, to prove our main theorem for
r=1
it suffices to establish tightness of the top curve of the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
line ensemble
\mathcal{L}^{N}
(after appropriate scaling) on the time window
[0,T/4]
.
The broad steps used in establishing our main theorem can be summarized as
(i)
-
(iv)
below.

(i)

Given any
M_{1}>0
, prove that there exists
M_{2}>M_{1}
such that
N^{-\frac{1}{3}}\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(2p^{*}-1)
and
N^{-\frac{1}{3}}\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(2p^{*})
are tight for some random
p^{*}\in[M_{1}N^{\frac{2}{3}},M_{2}N^{\frac{2}{3}}]
.

Here and below we consider staggered (i.e., even and odd) arguments for
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}
(and
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
) due to the diagonal Gibbs interaction. This is a technical point which can be ignored currently.

Owing to the Gibbs property, Theorem
1.3

(ii)
, enjoyed by the line ensemble
\mathcal{L}^{N}
, the joint law of
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(\llbracket 1,2p^{*}-1\rrbracket)
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(\llbracket 1,2p^{*}\rrbracket)
given the knowledge of
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(2p^{*}-1),\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(2p^{*})
and
\mathcal{L}_{3}^{N}(\llbracket 1,2p^{*}\rrbracket)
(where
p^{*}
comes from
(i)
) is that of a two-curve
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs line ensemble
(L_{1},L_{2})
with a bottom boundary data given by
L_{3}=\mathcal{L}^{N}_{3}
and right-boundary data determined by
L_{1}(2p^{*}-1)=\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(2p^{*}-1)
and
L_{2}^{N}(2p^{*})=\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(2p^{*})
. The point of this reduction is that we can now make use of a tool known as
stochastic monotonicity
(see Proposition
2.6
and discussion later in the introduction). This implies that if we instead condition on lower boundary data (i.e., lower
L_{3}
,
L_{1}^{N}(2p^{*}-1)
or
L_{2}^{N}(2p^{*})
), the resulting measure is stochastically dominated by the original measure, i.e. the law of
(\mathcal{L}^{N}_{1},\mathcal{L}^{N}_{2})
.

By using stochastic monotonicity we see that conditioned on the values of
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(2p^{*}-1),\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(2p^{*})
, it is possible to couple on the same probability space
\big(\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(\llbracket 1,2p^{*}-1\rrbracket),\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(\llbracket 1,2p^{*}\rrbracket)\big)
along with
(L_{1},L_{2})
distributed according to a
bottom-free

\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs measure specified by
L_{3}\equiv-\infty
,
L_{1}(2p^{*}-1)=\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(2p^{*}-1)
and
L_{2}^{N}(2p^{*})=\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(2p^{*})
(see also (
1.12
) below, or Definition
2.4
for a precise definition) in such a way that
\mathcal{L}^{N}_{1}(\llbracket 1,2p^{*}-1\rrbracket)\geq L_{1}(\llbracket 1,2p^{*}-1\rrbracket)
and
\mathcal{L}^{N}_{2}(\llbracket 1,2p^{*}\rrbracket)\geq L_{2}(\llbracket 1,2p^{*}\rrbracket)
point-wise. In particular, this means that any increasing event (recall from Definition
1.2
) will have a larger probability under the bottom-free measure than under the original measure. This is an important tool in establishing lower-tightness as well as control over the modulus of continuity.

For the below three items we will assume that
(L_{1},L_{2})
is specified in this bottom-free manner and that the right-boundary data given by
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(2p^{*}-1)
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(2p^{*})
satisfies
(i)
above.

(ii)

Prove that
N^{-\frac{1}{3}}L_{1}(1)
and
N^{-\frac{1}{3}}L_{2}(2)
are lower-tight.

(iii)

Prove that for any
M^{*}>0
, with positive probability (depending on
M^{*}
and
r
but not of
N
)

L_{1}(p)\geq M^{*}N^{1/3},\quad\textrm{and}\quad L_{2}(p)\geq M^{*}N^{1/3},\quad\mbox{for all }p\in\llbracket 1,T\rrbracket.

(iv)

Prove process-level tightness of
(N^{-1/3}L_{1}(xN^{2/3}))_{x\in[0,2]}.

We shall describe how we establish the above broad steps in a moment. Let us first conclude how the above steps work together to yield our main theorem, Theorem
1.1
.

We first argue that
N^{-\frac{1}{3}}\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(1)
and
N^{-\frac{1}{3}}\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(2)
are tight. Indeed, since the point-to-line free energy is an upper bound for the point-to-point free energy process, utilizing (
1.9
) it follows immediately that
N^{-\frac{1}{3}}\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(1)
and
N^{-\frac{1}{3}}\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(2)
are upper-tight. To show that they are also lower-tight we utilize the above mentioned stochastic monotonicity of the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs line ensembles (Proposition
2.6
) and instead show lower-tightness for the two-curve bottom-free line ensemble
(L_{1},L_{2})
(i.e., under the condition
L_{3}\equiv\infty
), which is what we established in item
(ii)
.

The next step to proving Theorem
1.1
is to argue that with strictly positive probability (i.e., not going to zero with
N\to\infty
) there is a uniform separation of length
cN^{1/3}
(for sufficient small
c
) between the first two curves
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}
and the third curve
\mathcal{L}_{3}^{N}
. The argument to show this (Proposition
4.1
in the text) proceeds as follows. Once we have tightness at the left boundary, it is straight-forward to show that
N^{-\frac{1}{3}}\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(2v-1)
and
N^{-\frac{1}{3}}\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(2v)
are tight for any choice of
v\in\llbracket 1,p^{*}\rrbracket
. Combining this with the soft non-intersection property of the line ensembles and
(ii)
, we deduce in Theorem
3.8
that
\sup_{p\in\llbracket 1,2T\rrbracket}N^{-\frac{1}{3}}\mathcal{L}_{3}^{N}(p)
is upper tight. The result in
(iii)
shows that the bottom-free line ensemble
(L_{1},L_{2}
) has a strictly positive probability of being uniformly high on
[0,T]
and thus by stochastic monotonicity so too does
(\mathcal{L}^{N}_{1},\mathcal{L}^{N}_{2})
. Together with upper-tightness of
\sup_{p\in\llbracket 1,2T\rrbracket}N^{-\frac{1}{3}}\mathcal{L}_{3}^{N}(p)
, this shows that the probability that
(\mathcal{L}^{N}_{1},\mathcal{L}^{N}_{2})
stay separate from
\mathcal{L}^{N}_{3}
stays bounded from 0 as
N\to\infty
.

Finally, we prove the process-level tightness of the top curve of our ensemble. Size biasing plays a key role in this deduction (see around equation (
5.25
)). Indeed, once we know that there is a positive probability of uniform separation (as deduced above), we can use the fact that the Radon-Nikodym derivative defining our Gibbs measures highly penalize configurations where the top two curves are close to the third curve. Thus, the positive probability event of separation becomes a high probability event. Finally, we are able to establish process-level tightness (i.e., control on the modulus of continuity) by leveraging the separation and the process-level tightness of the first two curves with the third curve moved to
-\infty
that was shown in item
(iv)
. This establishes tightness of the first curve which, through identification with the free energy process, yields Theorem
1.3
.

Remark 1.6
.

The result of
[
IMS22
]
immediately implies the tightness of
N^{-1/3}\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(1)
. However, to carry our proof outlined above we need tightness of
\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(1)
, and other fine information about
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}
away from the boundary, as described in item
(ii)
and
(iv)
, which to our best understanding is beyond the scope of
[
IMS22
]
.

We return to steps
(i)
-
(iv)
stated above and describe the main ideas in achieving them.

Proof idea for
(i)
:

We start by proving (Theorem
3.1
) that the curves
\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}
are typically non-intersecting (or at least do not overlap by much). Combining this with the fact that the point-to-line partition function (controlled in
[
BW22
]
) dominates the point-to-point partition function for any point along the line, it follows that
\sup_{i,j}N^{-1/3}\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}(j)
is upper-tight. Lower-tightness is trickier.

From the parabolic decay of the point-to-(partial)line free energy (
1.9
), we deduce that the point-to-point free energy process has to be in the
N^{1/3}
fluctuation scale at some random
p_{1}^{*}
in a
O(N^{2/3})
window. We essentially (see Proposition
3.4
) show that for
M_{0}
large enough

(1.11)

\displaystyle\sup_{p\in\llbracket QN^{2/3},(M_{0}+2Q)N^{2/3}\rrbracket}\frac{\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(2p+1)}{N^{1/3}\nu}+Q^{2}

is tight as
N\to\infty
, uniformly over all
Q>0
. The parameter
\nu
is an explicit function of
\theta
, see (
3.13
).
The crucial point here is the uniformity, i.e., the
K(\varepsilon)
in the definition of tightness can be chosen independent of
Q>0
. Thus, in
N^{1/3}
and
N^{2/3}
scaling
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}
follows an inverted parabola.

We next essentially show (see Proposition
3.3
) that there exists
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
large enough so that

\sup_{p_{2}\in\llbracket M_{1}N^{2/3},M_{2}N^{2/3}\rrbracket}N^{-1/3}\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(2p_{2})

is tight. The idea is if
\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}
is uniformly low in
[M_{1}N^{2/3},M_{2}N^{2/3}]
, then, due to the Gibbs property of the line ensemble, the first curve
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}
behaves like a random bridge, i.e., linearly, in that interval. However, as we show in proving Proposition
3.3
, this violates the inverted parabolic trajectory (
1.11
) for some
Q
thus leaving us with a random
p_{2}^{*}\in[M_{1}N^{2/3},M_{2}N^{2/3}]
so that
N^{-1/3}\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(2p_{2}^{*})
is tight. Owing to typical non-intersection (Proposition
3.1
) we have that
N^{-1/3}\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(2p_{2}^{*}-1)
is tight.

\mathsf{WPRW}
machinery:
The remaining proofs of
(ii)
,
(iii)
and
(iv)
rely heavily on understanding the

(1.12)

\displaystyle\mbox{$\mathpzc{HSLG}$ Gibbs measure on }(L_{1},L_{2})\mbox{ with }L_{1}(2n-1)=x_{n},\,\,L_{2}(2n)=y_{n},\,\,\textrm{ and }L_{3}\equiv-\infty,

for
L_{1}
with domain
\llbracket 1,2n-1\rrbracket
,
L_{2}
with domain
\llbracket 1,2n\rrbracket
,
n
of order
N^{2/3}
and
x_{n},y_{n}
of order
N^{1/3}
(i.e. order
\sqrt{n}
). We referred above to this as the bottom-free measure.

Set
M_{1}=16
(so that
M_{1}N^{2/3}\geq 2T
, where
T
is defined in (
1.10
)) in
(i)
and determine a random point
p^{*}
from the same item. Essentially, we want to take
n
in (
1.12
) to be this
p^{*}
. However, one caveat in taking
p^{*}
as a choice for
n
is that it is random. So, instead we analyze (
1.12
) for all fixed
n\in[M_{1}N^{2/3},M_{2}N^{2/3}]
. We shall show
(ii)
,
(iii′)
, and
(iv)
under the law in (
1.12
) with estimates uniform over all possible choices of
n\in[M_{1}N^{2/3},M_{2}N^{2/3}]
. Here
(iii′)
is given by

(iii
′
)

Prove that any
M^{*}>0
, with strictly positive probability (depending only on
M^{*}
and
M_{1},M_{2}
)

\mbox{under \eqref{law}}\quad L_{1}(p)\geq M^{*}N^{1/3},\quad\textrm{and}\quad L_{2}(p)\geq M^{*}N^{1/3}\quad\mbox{for all }p\in\llbracket 1,n-1\rrbracket.

Note that as
n\geq 2T
,
(iii′)
implies
(iii)
.

The law in (
1.12
) is closely related (see (
1.16
)) to the
weighted paired random walk
(
\mathsf{WPRW}
) law.

Definition 1.7

(Paired Random Walk and Weighted Paired Random Walk)
.

Let
\Omega^{2}_{n}=\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\mathcal{F}^{2}_{n}
be the Borel
\sigma
-algebra associated to
\Omega^{2}_{n}
. Write
\omega\in\Omega^{2}_{n}
as
\omega=(\omega_{1}(1),\ldots,\omega_{1}(N),\omega_{2}(1),\ldots,\omega_{2}(N))
. (For later purposes write
\Omega^{1}_{n}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, let
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{n}
be its Borel
\sigma
-algebra and write
\omega=(\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{n})
for
\omega\in\Omega^{1}_{n}
.)
Let
\mathsf{f}_{\theta}(x)
denote the density at
x\in\mathbb{R}
of
\log Y_{1}-\log Y_{2}
were
Y_{1},Y_{2}
are independent
\operatorname{Gamma}(\theta)
random variables and
\mathsf{g}_{\zeta}(x)=\Gamma(\alpha)^{-1}e^{\alpha x-e^{x}}
(see also (
2.3
) below). For
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}

The
paired random walk
(
\mathsf{PRW}
) law on
(\Omega^{2}_{n},\mathcal{F}^{2}_{n})
is the probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(x,y)}
proportional to the product of two Dirac delta functions
\delta_{\omega_{1}(n)=x}\delta_{\omega_{2}(n)=y}
and a density (against Lebesgue on
\mathbb{R}^{2(n-1)}
) is given by

(1.13)

\displaystyle\mathsf{g}_{\zeta}\big(\omega_{2}(1)-\omega_{1}(1)\big)\prod_{k=2}^{n}\mathsf{f}_{\theta}\big(\omega_{1}(k)-\omega_{1}(k-1)\big)\mathsf{f}_{\theta}\big(\omega_{2}(k)-\omega_{2}(k-1)\big)\,d\omega_{1}(k)\,d\omega_{2}(k).

As a slight abuse of notation we will say that the coordinate functions (i.e., random variables)

S_{i}(k)(\omega):=\omega_{i}(k),\quad k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket,i\in\{1,2\}

under this measure
\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(a,b)}
are
paired random walks
.
See Figure
4
for an illustration of the
\mathsf{PRW}
.

The weighted paired random walk (
\mathsf{WPRW}
) law
\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{WPRW}}^{n;(x,y)}
on
(\Omega^{2}_{n},\mathcal{F}^{2}_{n})
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(x,y)}
and defined through a Radon-Nikodym derivative so that for all
\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{F}_{n}
,

(1.14)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{WPRW}}^{n;(x,y)}(\mathsf{A})=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(x,y)}[W_{\operatorname{sc}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}]}{\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(x,y)}[W_{\operatorname{sc}}]},

where
W_{\operatorname{sc}}=W_{\operatorname{sc}}(\omega)
is given by

(1.15)

\displaystyle W_{\operatorname{sc}}:=\exp\bigg(-e^{S_{2}(1)-S_{1}(2)}-\sum_{k=2}^{n-1}\left(e^{S_{2}(k)-S_{1}(k+1)}+e^{S_{2}(k)-S_{1}(k)}\right)\bigg).

The ‘sc’ here refers to ‘super-critical’ as we will use a different representation of the bottom-free law in the critical case. For the purpose of this introduction we will just write
W
in place of
W_{\operatorname{sc}}
below.
The
\mathsf{WPRW}
law can be seen as a ‘soft’ version of the law that would result from conditioning on non-crossing. Crossing is now allowed but subject to substantial energetic penalization.

a_{n}=0
b_{n}=-\sqrt{n}

Figure 4
.
A paired random walk (
\mathsf{PRW}
) with top curve
(S_{1}(k))_{k=1}^{n}
and the bottom curve
(S_{2}(k))_{k=1}^{n}
specified by the condition that
S_{1}(n)=x_{n}=0
and
S_{2}(n)=y_{n}=-\sqrt{n}
. The
\mathsf{PRW}
law is given in (
1.13
) and should be seen as a reweighting of independent random walks by
\mathsf{g}_{\zeta}(S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1))
. This explains why the paths approach each other on the left of this figure. Also illustrated here is the situation where the two random walks happen to also be non-intersecting.

It follows from the Gibbs property for the
\mathsf{WPRW}
law (see Lemma
4.4
) that the

(1.16)

law of
(L_{1}(2k-1),L_{2}(2k))_{k=1}^{n}
in (
1.12
) equals law of
(S_{1}(k),S_{2}(k))_{k=1}^{n}
under
\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{WPRW}}^{n;(x_{n},y_{n})}

where the latter depends only on
n
and not
N
. Thus, hereon out we study the
\mathsf{WPRW}
law.

Remark 1.8
.

The
\mathsf{WPRW}
law described above only describes the behavior of points
(L_{1}(2k-1),L_{2}(2k))_{k=1}^{n}
under the law in (
1.12
). This leaves half of the points unaccounted for – even indexed points in
L_{1}\llbracket 1,2n\rrbracket
and odd indexed points in
L_{2}\llbracket 1,2n\rrbracket
. However, once we have controlled the behavior of the points
(L_{1}(2k-1),L_{2}(2k))_{k=1}^{n}
, the complementary points can easily be controlled by use of the Gibbs property as explained in Lemma
4.4
.

Proof idea for
(ii)
and
(iii)
:
We now illustrate the proof idea of
(ii)
. The proof idea for
(iii′)
is quite similar and done in parallel in Section
4
.
To establish
(ii)
, it suffices to show that for
\mathsf{A}=\{S_{1}(1)\leq-M\sqrt{n}\}
or
\mathsf{A}=\{S_{2}(1)\leq-M\sqrt{n}\}
,
\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{WPRW}}^{n;(x_{n},y_{n})}(\mathsf{A})
can be made arbitrarily small by choosing
M
large enough in a manner that is uniform as
n\to\infty
. Let us consider the case
\mathsf{A}=\{S_{1}(1)\leq-M\sqrt{n}\}
as the argument for the other case is completely analogous. The event
\mathsf{A}
is increasing (recall from Definition
1.2
). Thus, by stochastic monotonicity of
\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{WPRW}}^{n;(x_{n},y_{n})}
(Proposition
2.6
), decreasing the values of the endpoints
(x_{n},y_{n})
can only increase the probability of
\mathsf{A}
. Thus

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{WPRW}}^{n;(x_{n},y_{n})}(S_{1}(1)\leq-M\sqrt{n})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{WPRW}}^{n;(\min\{x_{n},y_{n}\},\min\{x_{n},y_{n}\}-\sqrt{n})}(S_{1}(1)\leq-M\sqrt{n})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{WPRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}(S_{1}(1)\leq-M\sqrt{n}-\min\{x_{n},y_{n}\}),

where the last inequality follows from shift invariance of the Gibbs measures (Lemma
2.1

(a)
). Recall that by the tightness afforded to us from
(i)
we were able to assume that
|\min\{x_{n},y_{n}\}|
is of order
\sqrt{n}
. Since in
(ii)
and
(iii)
we are likewise trying to prove tightness or that certain events occur with positive probability, it suffices to show that for all
C>0
, those results hold under the assumption
|\min\{x_{n},y_{n}\}|<C\sqrt{n}
. We do not need uniformity in
C
and the argument is the same for any such value, so we will currently assume
C=1
. Thus we aim now to bound
\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{WPRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}(S_{1}(1)\leq-M\sqrt{n})
(really
M+1
, but since
M
is arbitrary we just write
M
here) for large enough
M
, uniformly in
n
. To summarize, we have currently reduced our consideration to the boundary data
x_{n}=0,y_{n}=-\sqrt{n}
. This type of reduction is also possible while dealing with corresponding events in
(iii′)
but not for the event
(iv)
.

We next claim that there exists a constant
\mathrm{C}>0
so that uniformly over all
M
and
n

\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{WPRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}(\mathsf{A})\leq\mathrm{C}^{2}\,\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}(\mathsf{A})\qquad\textrm{for }\mathsf{A}=\{S_{1}(1)\leq-M\sqrt{n}\}.

The probability
\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}(\mathsf{A})
can be readily shown to go to zero uniformly in
n
as
M
grows. Thus, it suffices to prove the above comparison.
In light of (
1.14
), it suffices to show such a comparison for both the numerator and denominator. In particular, we show that

(1.17)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}[W\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}]\leq\mathrm{C}\!\cdot\!n^{-1/2}\!\cdot\!\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}],\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}[W]\geq\tfrac{1}{\mathrm{C}}\!\cdot\!n^{-1/2},

where
\mathrm{C}>0
is a universal constant that does not depend on
M
or
n
. Notice that while both numerator and denominator terms in (
1.14
) go to zero with
n\to\infty
, they do so at the same rate
n^{-1/2}
which cancels and yields the desired control on
\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{WPRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}(\mathsf{A})
. Note also that the
n^{-1/2}
decay behavior here is particular to
x_{n},y_{n}
of order
\sqrt{n}
and that for general boundary values of
x_{n},y_{n}
, the estimates in (
1.17
) may not be true.
The inequalities in (
1.17
) are established in the proofs of Lemmas
4.1
and
4.11
, and Corollary
4.12
. We will describe their main ideas here.

Proof idea for upper bound in (
1.17
).

We briefly explain the proof idea for the upper bound on
\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}[W\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}]
and, along the way, we explain why the
n^{-1/2}
factor shows up. The starting point of our proof is to compare the soft non-intersection conditioning by
W
to hard non-intersection conditioning in the following manner. Define

(1.18)

\displaystyle\mathsf{NI}_{p}:=\left\{S_{1}(k)-S_{2}(k)\geq-p,\mbox{ for all }k\in\llbracket 2,n-1\rrbracket\right\},\qquad\textrm{with}\quad\mathsf{NI}:=\mathsf{NI}_{0}.

Under the complement event
\mathsf{NI}_{p}^{c}
, we have
W\leq e^{-e^{p}}
and thus the following deterministic inequality:

(1.19)

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{NI}}+\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}e^{-e^{p}}\!\cdot\!\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{NI}_{p+1}\cap\mathsf{NI}_{p}^{c}}\leq\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{NI}}+\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}e^{-e^{p}}\!\cdot\!\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{NI}_{p+1}}.

Note that if we condition on
(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1))
, the
\mathsf{PRW}
law can be viewed as two independent random bridges from
(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1))
to
(0,-\sqrt{n})
. We denote this law as
\mathbb{P}^{n;(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1)),(0,-\sqrt{n})}
. In Lemma
C.8
, we show that there is an absolute constant
\mathrm{C}>0
, such that

\mathbb{P}^{n;(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1)),(0,-\sqrt{n})}(\mathsf{NI}_{p})\leq e^{\mathrm{C}p}\cdot\mathbb{P}^{n;(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1)),(0,-\sqrt{n})}(\mathsf{NI})\quad\textrm{for all }p\geq 0.

By this inequality and (
1.19
) along with the tower property of conditional expectations we find

\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}[W\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}]\leq\mathrm{C}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}\cdot\mathbb{P}^{n;(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1)),(0,-\sqrt{n})}(\mathsf{NI})\right]

for some
\mathrm{C}>0
. Thus, to upper bound
\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}[W\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}]
it suffices to do so to
\mathbb{P}^{n;(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1)),(0,-\sqrt{n})}(\mathsf{NI})
.

Due to the presence of the
g
factor in (
1.13
), under the
\mathsf{PRW}
law we expect a pinning effect in the left boundary, i.e.,
S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)=O(1)
. Thus we expect the large scale behavior under the
\mathsf{PRW}
law should be comparable to that of two independent random walks started close to each other. It is well known (see for example
[
Spi60
,
Koz77
]
) that when
S_{1}(k),S_{2}(k)
are independent random walks with
S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)=0
, the non-intersection probability over a time horizon of
n
step is of the order
n^{-1/2}
. This is why we expect the
n^{-1/2}
behavior of the non-intersection probability under the
\mathsf{PRW}
law as well. We confirm this expectation with two lemmas. The first, Lemma
4.7
, show that
|S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)|
,
S_{1}(1)/\sqrt{n}
, and
S_{2}(1)/\sqrt{n}
all have exponential tails under the
\mathsf{PRW}
law. The second, Lemma
C.9
, bounds the non-intersection probability as

(1.20)

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{|S_{1}(1)|+|S_{2}(1)|\leq\sqrt{n}(\log n)^{3/2}}\cdot\mathbb{P}^{n;(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1)),(0,-\sqrt{n})}\big(\mathsf{NI}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{\mathrm{C}}{\sqrt{n}}\!\cdot\!{\max\{S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1),1\}\!\cdot\!\max\left\{\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}|S_{1}(1)|,2\right\}^{\frac{3}{2}}}\!.

This lemma allows us to control the probability when
|S_{1}(1)|+|S_{2}(1)|\leq\sqrt{n}(\log n)^{3/2}
(the complementary case probability is controlled by the exponential tails). Lemma
4.7
follows from the description of the
\mathsf{PRW}
law in (
1.13
) and the exponential tails for the densities
\mathsf{f}_{\theta}
and
\mathsf{g}_{\zeta}
. Lemma
C.9
is more subtle and requires various estimates under the random bridge law that are uniform over a specified set of starting and ending points. Let us briefly explain here why we have such a bound in (
1.20
). Intuitively, the non-intersection probability should increase as the difference in starting points,
S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)
, increases. Thus we see a term of the form
\max\{S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1),1\}
on the right-hand side of (
1.20
). The term involving
\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}|S_{1}(1)|
, on the other hand, arises due to the nature of our proof. In the course of proving Lemma
C.9
, we proceed by bounding the ratio of density of the random bridge and density of a (pure) random walk. Such bound naturally depends on the slope of the random bridge and gets worse as the slope
|S_{1}(1)|/\sqrt{n}
increases. This is why we encounter
\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}|S_{1}(1)|
term on the right-hand side of (
1.20
). The details of the proof are presented in Appendix
C
. From the above two lemmas the upper bound in (
1.17
) follows readily.

Proof idea for lower bound in (
1.17
).

Lower bounding
\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}[W]
is more involved. The first step is to find a lower bound for
W
in terms of the indicator function for an event which we call
\mathsf{Gap}
. For simplicity we do this in a simpler setting to lower bound
\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}[W^{\prime}]
where

(1.21)

\displaystyle W^{\prime}:=\exp\bigg(-\sum_{k=2}^{n-1}e^{S_{2}(k)-S_{1}(k)}\bigg)

is obtain by deleting various terms in the exponential defining of
W
(see (
1.15
)). Note that
W^{\prime}\geq W
and thus the argument for
W
is necessarily more involved. It is known from
[
Rit81
]
that when a random walk
(S(k))_{k=1}^{n}
is conditioned to stay positive, with high probability
S(k)
has growth at least of the order
k^{\frac{1}{2}-\delta}
for any
\delta>0
. Taking
\delta=\frac{1}{4}
this implies that if we condition a random bridge
(S(k))_{k=1}^{n}
of length
n
starting and ending at zero to stay positive, then
S(k)
should be at least of the order
\min\{k,n-k+1\}^{\frac{1}{4}}
with very high probability. Treating
S_{1}(k)-S_{2}(k)
as a random bridge, this inspires us to define

\displaystyle\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta}^{\prime}:=\{S_{1}(k)-S_{2}(k)\geq\beta\cdot\min\{k,n-k+1\}^{\frac{1}{4}}\mbox{ for all }k\in\llbracket 2,n\rrbracket\}

We note that when
\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta^{\prime}}\cap\{S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)\in[0,1]\}
occurs, the sum in the exponent of
W^{\prime}
is bounded uniformly in
n
and hence
W^{\prime}
is bounded below by a strictly positive constant, say
a_{\beta}^{\prime}
. Thus we have
W^{\prime}\geq a_{\beta}^{\prime}\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta}^{\prime}\cap\{S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)\in[0,1]\}}
.
For
W
, we define a similar, albeit more complicated, event
\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta}
(see (
4.22
) for definition) that captures the above idea and we show in Lemma
4.8
that
W\geq a_{\beta}\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta}\cap\{S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)\in[0,1]\}}
for some deterministic constant
a_{\beta}>0
. Thus to lower bound
\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}[W]
we lower bound
\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}(\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta})
and
\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}(S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)\in[0,1])
.

A
D
B
C
n/4
n/2
n/4

Figure 5
.
The modified random bridge is constructed by starting two random walks of length
n/4
, one from
A
to
B
and another (run backwards) from
D
to
C
. The path between
B
and
C
is then chosen given the values there according to a random bridge of length
n/2
.

Recall that by the Gibbs property, conditioned on
(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1))
, the law of
(S_{1}(k),S_{2}(k))_{k=1}^{n}
under
\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}

is that of two independent random bridges (with increment law
\mathsf{f}_{\theta}
as in all of our discussion above) started from
(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1))
and ended at
(0,-\sqrt{n})
. In estimating the
\mathsf{Gap}
event probability under this law we found it easier to work with the law of two independent
modified random bridges
. These are described in Figure
5
and composed of random walks (with increment law
\mathsf{f}_{\theta}
) in the first and last
n/4
portion of its domain, and then a bridge to connect the resulting values. Lemma
4.10
shows that the density of the random bridge and modified random bridge are comparable provided the values at
k=1,n/4,3n/4,n
are all of order
\sqrt{n}
.
In particular, if we set

\mathsf{E}:=\{|S_{1}(n/4)-S_{1}(3n/4)|+|S_{2}(n/4)-S_{2}(3n/4)|\leq\sqrt{n}\},\quad\mathsf{F}:=\{|S_{1}(1)|+|S_{2}(1)|\leq\sqrt{n}\},

the results from Lemma
4.10
allows us to conclude that

(1.22)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}(\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta}\cap\{S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)\in[0,1]\})\geq\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}(\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta}\cap\{S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)\in[0,1]\}\cap\mathsf{E}\cap\mathsf{F})

\displaystyle\geq\tfrac{1}{\mathrm{C}}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\{S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)\in[0,1]\}\cap\mathsf{F}}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n;(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1)),(0,-\sqrt{n})}(\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta}\cap\mathsf{E})\right]

where
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n;(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1)),(0,-\sqrt{n})}
is the law of two independent modified random bridges started from
(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1))
and ended at
(0,-\sqrt{n})
. Using shorthand
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}
for
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n;(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1)),(0,-\sqrt{n})}
and Bayes rule,

\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta}\cap\mathsf{E})=\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(\mathsf{NI})\cdot\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta}\cap\mathsf{E}\mid\mathsf{NI}).

Since the modified random bridge has two true random walk portion (first and last quarter) we can now rely on standard non-intersecting random walk techniques to eventually obtain a lower bound on the probability
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(\mathsf{NI})
above. In Appendix
C
we establish various uniform estimates and in particular (combining Lemma
C.3
and Corollary
C.10
) show that for
x_{n},y_{n}
of order
n^{1/2}
,

(1.23)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(\mathsf{NI})\geq\tfrac{1}{\mathrm{C}}\cdot n^{-1/2}\cdot\mathbb{P}^{n/4;(x_{n},y_{n})}(\widetilde{\mathsf{NI}}),

and
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta}\cap\mathsf{E}\mid\mathsf{NI})\geq\frac{1}{\mathrm{C}}
(for small enough
\beta
) uniformly over
S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1)\leq M\sqrt{n}
and
S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)\in[0,1]
. In (
1.23
),
\mathbb{P}^{n/4;(x_{n},y_{n})}(\widetilde{\mathsf{NI}})
denotes the probability of non-intersection of two random walks of length
n/4
started from
x_{n}
and
y_{n}
. As
x_{n}=0,y_{n}=-\sqrt{n}
, we can show that
\mathbb{P}^{n/4;(x_{n},y_{n})}(\widetilde{\mathsf{NI}})
is bounded below. Finally, Lemma
4.7
establishes that
\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\{S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)\in[0,1]\}\cap\mathsf{F}}\right]
is bounded below. Thus combining all the estimates leads to an
n^{-1/2}
order lower bound for the right hand side of (
1.22
). Putting together the various bounds described above now yields the desired lower bound on

\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}[W]
in (
1.17
).
Since in Section
5.3
, we prove
(ii)
and
(iii′)
in parallel, some parts of the argument presented here in the introduction appear in a more general or slightly different flavor later. However, the core idea and features remain the same.

Non-intersecting random walks and random bridges that are pinned at the starting and/or ending points have been studied extensively (e.g.
[
BS07
,
EK08
,
DW10
]
and the reference therein) and are known to converge under diffusive scaling to Dyson Brownian motion and non-intersecting Brownian bridges. As demanded by our technical arguments, our work establishes
uniform
(over starting and ending points) estimates for non-intersection probabilities of pairs of random walks and random bridges in such scaling, i.e.
uniform
over
all possible

O(1)
starting points that potentially can vary in a diffusive
O(\sqrt{n})
window (precisely how
(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1))
behaves). Appendix
C
develops the machinery to establish such uniform estimates under general assumptions on increments of the random bridges.

Proof idea for
(iv)
:

The argument to prove
(iv)
also uses the machinery developed in the proof of
(ii)
and
(iii)
and the reduction from (
1.16
) to the study of the weighted paired random walks
(S_{1}(k),S_{2}(k))_{k=1}^{n}
. For
\gamma,\delta,M>0
, consider the events

\mathsf{B}=\mathsf{B}(\delta,\gamma):=\bigg\{\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},i_{2}\in\llbracket 1,n/4\rrbracket\\
|i_{1}-i_{2}|\leq\delta n\end{subarray}}\!\!\!|S_{1}(i_{1})-S_{1}(i_{2})|\geq\gamma\sqrt{n}\bigg\},\quad\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{G}(M):=\big\{|S_{1}(1)|+|S_{2}(1)|\geq M\sqrt{n}\big\}.

To prove tightness we will show that for each
\gamma>0
, as
\delta\to 0
, we have
\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{WPRW}}^{n;(x_{n},y_{n})}(\mathsf{B})\to 0
. Recall that as an input we know that
x_{n},y_{n}
are of order
n^{1/2}
.
In
(ii)
, we observed that
\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{WPRW}}^{n;(x_{n},y_{n})}(\mathsf{G})\to 0
as
M\to\infty
uniformly in
n
. Thus it suffices to provide an upper bound for
\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{WPRW}}^{n;(x_{n},y_{n})}(\mathsf{B}\cap\mathsf{G}^{c})
for each
M>0
. Thanks to (
1.14
), it suffices to give a upper bound for
\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(x_{n},y_{n})}[W\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{B}\cap\mathsf{G}^{c}}]
and a lower bound for
\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(x_{n},y_{n})}[W]
.
An important difference from the discussion regarding
(ii)
is that now the event
\mathsf{B}
in question deals with two point differences of
S_{1}(\cdot)
which is not an increasing event. Thus, the monotonicity of the Gibbs measure with respect to the boundary data does not help here and, unlike in
(ii)
, we cannot use monotonicity to reduce consideration to
x_{n}=0,y_{n}=-\sqrt{n}
.

Instead, using the soft non-intersection property of our Gibbsian line ensemble we can deduce control on the difference of the exit points. We show in Theorem
3.1
that for all large enough
N

\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(2n-1)\geq\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(2n)-(\log N)^{7/6}\textrm{ for all }n\in\llbracket M_{1}N^{2/3},M_{2}N^{2/3}\rrbracket\Big)\geq 1-2^{-N}.

Note that in (
1.12
) we conditioned upon
L_{1}(2n-1)=x_{n}
and
L_{1}(2n)=y_{n}
. In view of the above high probability event, we may thus assume
x_{n}-y_{n}\geq-(\log n)^{7/6}
.
Under these boundary conditions (
x_{n},y_{n}
of order
n^{1/2}
and
x_{n}-y_{n}\geq-(\log n)^{7/6}
) the estimates in (
1.17
) may not hold.

Nonetheless, for the lower bound of
\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(x_{n},y_{n})}[W]
, all the arguments up to and including (
1.23
) hold under the present assumptions on
(x_{n},y_{n})
. We show in Lemma
5.6
that

(1.24)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(x_{n},y_{n})}[W]\geq\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}{\mathrm{C}_{1}^{-1}}\cdot\mathbb{P}^{n/4;(x_{n},y_{n})}(\widetilde{\mathsf{NI}})\geq{\mathrm{C}_{2}^{-1}}e^{-\mathrm{C}_{2}(\log n)^{5/4}},

where the second bound above is true under our assumption
x_{n}-y_{n}\geq(\log n)^{7/6}
. For the upper bound on
\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(x_{n},y_{n})}[W\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{B}\cap\mathsf{G}^{c}}]
we first obtain a deterministic bound for
W
(similar to (
1.19
))

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{C}\left(e^{-(\log n)^{2}}+\widetilde{W}\right),\qquad\widetilde{W}:=\sum_{p=0}^{\lfloor 2\log\log(n)\rfloor}e^{-e^{p}}\!\cdot\!\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{NI}_{p+1}}.

Due to the
e^{-\mathrm{C}_{2}(\log n)^{5/4}}
lower bound in (
1.24
), the
e^{-(\log n)^{2}}
factor above can be ignored and we instead focus on upper bounding
\widetilde{W}
. Using the Gibbs property and conditional expectations

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(x_{n},y_{n})}[\widetilde{W}\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{B}\cap\mathsf{G}^{c}}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=0}^{\lfloor 2\log\log(n)\rfloor-1}e^{-e^{p}}\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(x_{n},y_{n})}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{G}^{c}}\mathbb{P}^{n;(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1)),(x_{n},y_{n})}(\mathsf{B}\cap\mathsf{NI}_{p+1})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=0}^{\lfloor 2\log\log(n)\rfloor-1}e^{-e^{p}}\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(x_{n},y_{n})}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{G}^{c}}\mathbb{P}^{n;(S_{1}(1)+p+1,S_{2}(1)),(x_{n}+p+1,y_{n})}(\mathsf{B}\cap\mathsf{NI})\right].

where
\mathbb{P}^{n;(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1)),(x_{n},y_{n})}
is the law of two independent random bridges started from
(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1))
and ending at
(x_{n},y_{n})
. The last equality above follows by lifting the
S_{1}(\cdot)
random walk by
p+1
units. We then apply the density comparison (Lemma
4.10
) to modified random bridges to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{G}^{c}}\cdot\mathbb{P}^{n;(S_{1}(1)+p,S_{2}(1)),(x_{n}+p,y_{n})}(\mathsf{B}\cap\mathsf{NI})\leq\mathrm{C}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{G}^{c}}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n;(S_{1}(1)+p,S_{2}(1)),(x_{n}+p,y_{n})}(\mathsf{B}\cap\mathsf{NI}).

where
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n;(S_{1}(1)+p,S_{2}(1)),(x_{n}+p,y_{n})}
is the law of two independent modified random bridges started from
(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1))
and ending at
(x_{n},y_{n})
. The above comparison is only possible when we have a control on the slopes of the random bridges. This slope control is precisely furnished by
\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{G}^{c}}
.

Let us write
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{p}
for
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{n;(S_{1}(1)+p+1,S_{2}(1)),(x_{n}+p+1,y_{n})}
. Using uniform estimates for non-intersection probability for random walks and bridges from Appendix
C
(combining Lemma
C.3
, Lemma
C.8
and Corollary
C.10
) we obtain that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{p}(\mathsf{NI})\leq\tfrac{\mathrm{C}}{\sqrt{n}}e^{\mathrm{C}p}\cdot\max\{S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1),1\}\cdot\mathbb{P}^{n/4;(x_{n},y_{n})}(\widetilde{\mathsf{NI}}).

Since
\mathsf{B}
depends only of the first quarter points,
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{p}(\mathsf{B}\mid\mathsf{NI})
can be controlled by modulus of continuity estimates for (pure) random walks under non-intersection which we deduce in Lemma
C.12
. In particular, we obtain that
\sup_{p\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor 2\log\log(n)\rfloor-1\rrbracket}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{p}(\mathsf{B}\mid\mathsf{NI})\to 0
as
\delta\to 0
. Combining all the above estimates, in view of the exponential tail bounds for
S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)
under the
\mathsf{PRW}
law from Lemma
4.7
, this leads to the desired estimate.

1.4.
Related works on half-space polymers

Half-space polymers are a particular variant of full-space polymers that are well-studied in the literature (introduced in
[
HH85
,
IS88
,
Bol89
]
). Full-space polymers are widely believed to be in the KPZ universality class in the sense that they are expected to have
1/3
fluctuation exponent and
2/3
transversal exponent. However, besides a few solvable models, these exponents are not proven rigorously for general polymers. We refer to
[
Com17
,
Sep12
,
BC20
,
BCD23
,
DZ22a
,
DZ22b
]
and references therein for more details.

Half-space polymer models have been studied in the physics literature since the work of Kardar
[
Kar85
]
. They arise naturally in the context of modeling wetting phenomena
[
PSW82
,
Abr80
,
BHL83
]
where one studies directed polymers in the presence of a wall. They have been of great interest due to the presence of phase transition (called the ‘depinning transition’) and a rich phase diagram for limiting distributions based on the diagonal strength. This phase diagram was first rigorously proven for geometric last passage percolation (LPP), i.e., polymers with zero temperature, in a series of works by Baik and Rains
[
BR01a
,
BR01b
,
BR01c
]
. Multi-point fluctuations were studied then in
[
SI04
]
and similar results were later proven for exponential LPP in
[
BBCS18a
,
BBCS18b
]
using Pfaffian Schur processes. For further recents works on half-space LPP, we refer to
[
BBNV18
,
BFO20
,
BFO22
,
FO22
]
.

Positive temperature models such as polymers resisted rigorous treatment for longer compared to LPP since they are no longer directly related to Pfaffian point processes. For such class of models in the half-space geometry, the first rigorous proof of depinning transition appeared in
[
BW22
]
where the authors proved precise fluctuation results including the BBP phase transition
[
BAP05
]
for the point-to-line log-gamma free energy. For the point-to-point log-gamma free energy, the limit theorem along with Baik-Rains phase transition was conjectured in
[
BBC20
]
based on an uncontrolled steepest descent analysis of certain formulas coming from half-space Macdonald processes. This result was proved recently in
[
IMS22
]
using a new set of ideas, relating the half-space model to a free boundary version of the Schur process. In fact,
[
IMS22
]
also proves analogous results for the half-space KPZ equation which is the free energy of the continuum directed random polymer in half-space.
The half-space KPZ equation arises as a limit of free energy of
\mathpzc{HSLG}
polymer
[
Wu20
,
BC23
]
. Since the early work by Kardar
[
Kar85
]
, the half-space KPZ equation has received significant attention, with a flurry of new results recently in
in both mathematics
[
CS18
,
BBCW18
,
BBC20
,
Par19
,
Par22
,
BC23
,
IMS22
]
and physics literature
[
GLD12
,
BBC16
,
IT18
,
DNKLDT20
,
KLD18
,
BKLD20
,
BLD21
,
BKLD22
]
. Apart from log-gamma and continuum polymer, a half-space version of the beta polymer was recently introduced and studied in
[
BR22
]
.

Organization

In Section
2
, we study several properties of
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs measures and Gibbsian line ensemble, and prove Theorem
1.3
. Section
3
is divided into three subsections that discuss three important probabilistic results for the line ensemble. In Section
3.1
, we show a certain ordering of points on the line ensemble (Theorem
3.1
). This is the precise technical form of the typical non-intersection property discussed at the end of Section
1.3.1
. In Section
3.2
, we show that there is a high point on the second curve (Theorem
3.3
) as discussed at the end of item
(i)
from Section
1.3.3
. In Section
3.2
, we provide high probability uniform upper bounds for the second and third curves (Theorem
3.8
). These bounds are used later in proving item
(ii)
from Section
1.3.3
. In Section
4
, we prove one-point tightness on the left boundary and study the probability of a certain ‘region pass event’. The study of the region pass event is utilized in proving the lower bound on the uniform separation between the first two curves and the third curve (described earlier in
(ii)
from Section
1.3.3
). Finally, in Section
5
, we study the modulus of continuity under the
\mathsf{WPRW}
law and prove Theorem
1.1
.
Appendix
A
includes the proof of stochastic monotonicity for
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbsian line ensembles. Appendix
B
collects several basic facts about log-gamma random variables and related measures. Appendix
C
is devoted to proving several technical estimates related to non-intersecting random bridges which are required in studying the
\mathsf{WPRW}
law.

Notations and Conventions

For
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote
\llbracket a,b\rrbracket:=[a,b]\cap\mathbb{Z}
,
a\wedge b=\min(a,b)
, and
a\vee b=\max(a,b)
. Throughout this paper we work with three fixed parameters:
\theta>0
(bulk parameter),
\zeta>0
(supercritical boundary parameter), and
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
(critical boundary parameter). All our constants appearing in the rest of the paper may depend on
\theta,\zeta,\mu
and possibly other specified variables. We will only specify the dependency of the constants on the variables besides
\theta,\zeta,\mu
by writing
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}(a,b,c,\cdots)>0
to denote a generic deterministic positive finite constant that may change from line to line, but is dependent on the designated variables
a,b,c,\cdots
. We write l.h.s. or r.h.s. to denote the left- or right-hand side of an equation.
Given a density
f
,
X\sim f
denotes a random variable
X
whose distribution function has density
f
. We also write
X_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}f
if
\{X_{i}\}
are i.i.d. with some common density
f
. We sometimes also use the notation
X\sim\bullet
where
\bullet
is the name of a distribution (e.g.
\operatorname{Gamma}^{-1}(\beta)
) to mean
X
has distribution
\bullet
and is independent of all other random variables being considered. For two densities
f
and
g
, we write
f\ast g(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(z)g(x-z)dz
for the convolution density. We use the notation
\sigma(\bullet)
for denote the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the random variables
\bullet
. We write
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{\Longrightarrow}}
and
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}
for convergence and equality in distribution.
There is a glossary at the end of this text that recalls and points to the definitions of much of the notation introduced elsewhere.

2.
Half-space log-gamma objects and proof of Theorem
1.3

In Section
2.1
, we gather several useful properties of
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs measures from Definition
1.2
including stochastic monotonicity (Proposition
2.6
). The
\mathpzc{HSLG}
line ensemble is defined in Section
2.2
which includes the proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Here, we introduce few important functions that will come up often in our arguments. We define

(2.1)

\displaystyle W(a;b,c)=\exp(-e^{a-b}-e^{a-c}),\quad a,b,c\in\mathbb{R},

For
\theta>0,m\in\mathbb{Z}
we set

(2.2)

\displaystyle G_{\theta,(-1)^{m}}(y):=e^{\theta(-1)^{m}y-e^{(-1)^{m}y}}/\Gamma(\theta),\quad y\in\mathbb{R}.

One can check that
G_{\theta,(-1)^{m}}
is a density (i.e., it integrates to
1
). Using
G
, we define two more probability density functions:

(2.3)

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}_{\theta}(x):=G_{\theta,+1}\ast G_{\theta,-1}(x),\qquad\mathsf{g}_{\zeta}(x):=G_{\zeta,1}(x),\qquad\theta,\zeta>0,x\in\mathbb{R},

where
\ast
denotes the convolution operation, i.e.,
p\ast q(x):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}p(z)q(x-z)dz
.

2.1.
Properties of
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs measures

We start by writing down several lemmas that all follow directly from the definition of
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs measures (recall from Definition
1.2
).

Lemma 2.1
.

Consider the graph
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{2}
endowed with directed/colored edges
E(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{2})
as above. Let
\Lambda
be a bounded connected subset of
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{2}
. For each
(i,j)\in\partial\Lambda
fix some
u_{i,j}\in\mathbb{R}
. Fix any
c\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
\big(L(v):v\in\Lambda\big)
be a collection of random variables that are distributed as the
\mathpzc{HSLG}

\Theta
-Gibbs measure on the domain
\Lambda
with boundary condition
\big(u_{i,j}:(i,j)\in\partial\Lambda\big)
.

(a)

(Translation invariance) The law of
\big(L(v)+c:v\in\Lambda\big)
is given by the
\mathpzc{HSLG}

\Theta
-Gibbs measure on the domain
\Lambda
with boundary condition
\big(u_{i,j}+c:(i,j)\in\partial\Lambda\big)
.

(b)

(Gibbs property on smaller domain) Take a bounded connected subset
\Lambda^{\prime}\subset\Lambda
. The law of
\big(L(v):v\in\Lambda^{\prime}\big)
conditioned on
\big(L(v):v\in\Lambda\setminus\Lambda^{\prime}\big)
is given by the
\mathpzc{HSLG}

\Theta
-Gibbs measure on the domain
\Lambda^{\prime}
with the boundary condition
\big(L(v):v\in\partial\Lambda^{\prime}\big)
where we set
L(v)=u_{v}
for
v\in\partial\Lambda
.

Proof.

Note that the density of a
\mathpzc{HSLG}

\Theta
-Gibbs measure given in (
1.6
) only involve terms of the form
u_{v_{1}}-u_{v_{2}}
. Thus adding a constant
c
to every term does not change the law. The fact that Gibbs property carries to smaller domains follows from the explicit form of the density as well. ∎

Although
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs measures are defined for any bounded connected subset
\Lambda
, we will be mainly concerned with two kinds of domains
\Lambda
. Given
k\geq 1
and
T\geq 2
, we define

(2.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{k,T}:=\left\{(i,j):i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,j\in\llbracket 1,2T-1-\mathbf{1}_{i=1}\rrbracket\right\},\quad\mathcal{K}_{k,T}^{\prime}:=\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\times\llbracket 1,2T-2\rrbracket.

The domains
\mathcal{K}_{k,T}
and
\mathcal{K}_{k,T}^{\prime}
are shown as shaded regions in Figure
6
. We state these results for the homogeneous Gibbs measures, though they could easily be adapted to
\Theta
-Gibbs measures.

y_{2}
y_{3}
{z_{0}}

\mathcal{K}_{k,T}
