\mathcal{K}_{k,T}^{\prime}

Figure 6
.
Two domains
\mathcal{K}_{k,T}
and
\mathcal{K}_{k,T}^{\prime}
are shown in (A) and (B) with
k=3
,
T=4
and boundary conditions
(\vec{y},\vec{z})
and
(\vec{y},\vec{w})
respectively. They include all the vertices within the gray dashed box as well some labels for the points. The directed edges with lighter colors are edges connecting vertices from
\Lambda
to
\partial\Lambda
or viceversa (
\Lambda=\mathcal{K}_{k,T}
or
\Lambda=\mathcal{K}_{k,T}^{\prime}
).
The boundary variable
z_{0}
does not actually play any role in the density of the corresponding
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs measure after normalizing it to be a probability density. This point is explained in the proof of Lemma
2.2
.

Lemma 2.2

(One-sided boundary Gibbs property)
.

Fix
k,T\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
and
\alpha>-\theta
. Fix
\vec{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{T}
, and
\vec{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{T-1}
.

(a)

The
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs measure on the domain
\mathcal{K}_{k,T}
with boundary condition
(\vec{y},\vec{z})
is a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{K}_{k,T}|}
whose density at
\mathbf{u}=(u_{i,j})_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{K}_{k,T}}
is proportional to

(2.5)

\displaystyle f_{k,T}^{\vec{y},\vec{z}}(\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle:=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left(e^{(-1)^{i}\alpha u_{i,1}}\prod_{j=1}^{T-\mathbf{1}_{i=1}}W(u_{i+1,2j};u_{i,2j+1},u_{i,2j-1})\hskip-5.69046pt\prod_{j=1}^{2T-1-\mathbf{1}_{i=1}}\hskip-8.5359ptG_{\theta,(-1)^{j+1}}(u_{i,j}-u_{i,j+1})\right)

where
W(a;b,c)
and
G_{\theta,(-1)^{m}}(y)
are defined in (
2.1
) and (
2.2
) respectively.
Here
u_{k+1,2j}=z_{j}
for each
j\in\llbracket 1,T\rrbracket
,
u_{1,2T-1}=y_{1}
, and
u_{i,2T}=y_{i}
,
u_{i,2T+1}:=+\infty
(so that the factor
\exp(-e^{u_{i+1,2T}-u_{i,2T+1}})=1
) for each
i\in\llbracket 2,k\rrbracket
.

(b)

The
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs measure on the domain
\mathcal{K}_{k,T}^{\prime}
with boundary condition
(\vec{y},\vec{w})
is a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{K}_{k,T}^{\prime}|}
whose density at
\mathbf{u}=(u_{i,j})_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{K}_{k,T}^{\prime}}
is proportional to

(2.6)

\displaystyle Q_{k,T}^{\vec{y}^{\prime},\vec{z}}(\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle:=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left(e^{(-1)^{i}\alpha u_{i,1}}\prod_{j=1}^{T-1}W(u_{i+1,2j};u_{i,2j+1},u_{i,2j-1})\prod_{j=1}^{2T-2}G_{\theta,(-1)^{j+1}}(u_{i,j}-u_{i,j+1})\right).

Here
u_{k+1,2j}=w_{j}
for each
j\in\llbracket 1,T-1\rrbracket
, and
u_{i,2T-1}=y_{i}
for each
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
.

Proof.

We refer to Figure
6
for a visual representation of the above measures. Recall the edge weights from (
1.5
). The blue edges in the figure corresponds to
G_{\theta,(-1)^{j+1}}(\cdot)
factors that appear in (
2.5
) and (
2.6
). The
(-1)^{j+1}
factor is due to the alternate switching of the direction of blue weights as we read off from left to right. Here we have obtained the
G
function from the blue edge weights by multiplying by a constant. This is done so that the
G
function becomes density (i.e., integrates to
1
), a fact that will be useful in the later analysis. The black edge weights from (
1.5
) corresponds to the
W
factor in (
2.5
) and (
2.6
). Finally the red edge weights are of type
e^{-\alpha u_{2i-1,1}-u_{2i,1}}=e^{-\alpha u_{2i-1,1}}\cdot e^{\alpha u_{2i,1}}
. Note that only for odd
k
we have
(k+1,1)\in\partial\mathcal{K}_{k,T},\partial\mathcal{K}_{k,T}^{\prime}
. In that case, the factor
e^{-\alpha u_{k+1,1}}
can be absorbed into the proportionality constant. Thus, overall, the red weights contributes the factor
\prod_{i=1}^{k}e^{(-1)^{i}\alpha_{i,1}}
in the above densities. This also explains why the
z_{0}
value does not play any role in the definition of these densities.
∎

Definition 2.3
.

We will mostly be concerned with the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs measure on
\mathcal{K}_{k,T}
with boundary condition
(\vec{y},\vec{z})
(see Lemma
2.2
(a)
for the probability density of this measure).
We will denote the probability and the expectation operator under this law as
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}^{\vec{y},\vec{z};k,T}
and
\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}^{\vec{y},\vec{z};k,T}
respectively and a random variable with this law by
L:=\big(L(i,j):=L_{i}(j):(i,j)\in\mathcal{K}_{k,T}\big)
. We may drop
\alpha
and write
\mathbb{P}^{\vec{y},\vec{z};k,T}
and
\mathbb{E}^{\vec{y},\vec{z};k,T}
when clear from the context.

We now define the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs measure on
\mathcal{K}_{k,T}
with boundary condition
\vec{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\vec{z}:=(-\infty)^{T}
.

Definition 2.4

(Bottom-free Gibbs measure)
.

The
bottom-free
measure on the domain
\mathcal{K}_{k,T}
with boundary condition
\vec{y}
is the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs measure on the domain
\mathcal{K}_{k,T}
with boundary condition
(\vec{y},(-\infty)^{T})
. By Lemma
4.2
this the corresponding density
f_{k,T}^{\vec{y},(-\infty)^{T}}
is integrable when
k
is even and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
(in that case the measure does not even depend on
\alpha
) or when
k
is odd and
\alpha\in(-\theta,\theta)
.
In this case the bottom-free measure can be normalized to a probability measure so that for
\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{T}

(2.7)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}^{\vec{y},\vec{z};k,T}(\mathsf{A})=\frac{1}{V_{k}^{T}(\vec{y},\vec{z})}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}^{\vec{y};(-\infty)^{T};k,T}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{T}W(z_{2j};L_{k}(2j+1),L_{k}(2j-1))\right],

for any event
\mathsf{A}
, where we set
L_{k}(2T+1)=+\infty
and the normalization is given by

(2.8)

\displaystyle V_{k}^{T}(\vec{y},\vec{z}):=\mathbb{E}_{\alpha}^{\vec{y};(-\infty)^{T};k,T}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{T}W(z_{2j};L_{k}(2j+1),L_{k}(2j-1))\right].

In other words, we can build the full Gibbs measure
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}^{\vec{y},\vec{z};k,T}
by reweighting the bottom-free measure by a Radon-Nikodym derivative given by the expression (except
\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}
) inside the expectation in (
2.7
), normalized by dividing by
V_{k}^{T}(\vec{y},\vec{z})
.

Besides one-sided conditioning as in Lemma
2.1
, we can also use the Gibbs property when conditioning on boundary data on both sides as is standard in full-space discrete line ensembles
[
DFF+21
,
BCD23
,
DW21b
]
. We record here one such result that will be useful in our later proofs.

Lemma 2.5

(Two-sided boundary Gibbs property)
.

Fix
1\leq T_{1}<T_{2}-1
. Suppose
L
is distributed as
\mathbb{P}^{\vec{y},\vec{z};1,T_{2}}
. Let
(X(j))_{j=T_{1}-1}^{T_{2}-1}
be a random bridge from
X(T_{1}-1)=a
to
X(T_{2}-1)=b
with i.i.d. increments from the density
\mathsf{f}_{\theta}
defined in (
2.3
). The law of
\big(L_{1}(2j+1):T_{1}\leq j\leq T_{2}-2\big)
conditioned on
\{L_{1}(2T_{1}-1)=a,L_{1}(2T_{2}-1)=b\}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the law of
(X(j))_{j=T_{1}}^{T_{2}-2}
with Radon-Nikodym derivative proportional to

\widetilde{W}:=\exp\bigg(-\sum_{j=T_{1}}^{T_{2}-1}(e^{z_{j}-X(j)}+e^{z_{j}-X(j-1)})\bigg).

Proof.

We utilize the form of the Gibbs measure density given in (
2.5
). The
G_{\theta,1}\ast G_{\theta,-1}
function appears in the statement of Lemma
2.5
as we focus on the marginal distribution of the odd points only and hence we integrate out the dummy variables on the even points (see Figure
7
).
∎

b
a
z_{1}
z_{2}
z_{3}
z_{4}
z_{5}

Figure 7
.
The marginal distribution of the odd (black) points of the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs measure shown above with
T_{1}=1,T_{2}=6
is described in Lemma
2.5
.

As with full-space line ensemble Gibbs measures
[
CH14
,
CH16
,
Wu20
,
BCD23
,
DW21b
]
, the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs measures satisfy
stochastic monotonicity
with respect to the boundary data. The following, stated for the inhomogeneous
\Theta
-Gibbs measures provides a grand monotone coupling over all boundary data.

Proposition 2.6

(Stochastic monotonicity)
.

Fix
k_{1}\leq k_{2}
,
a_{i}\leq b_{i}
for
k_{1}\leq i\leq k_{2}
. Fix
\Theta:=\{\vartheta_{i,j}>0:(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{2}\}
, and
\alpha>-\min\{\theta_{i,j}:(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{2}\}
. Let

\displaystyle\Lambda:=\{(i,j):k_{1}\leq i\leq k_{2},a_{i}\leq j\leq b_{i}\}.

There exists a probability space that supports a collection of random variables

\displaystyle\big(L(v;(u_{w})_{w\in\partial\Lambda}):v\in\Lambda,(u_{w})_{w\in\partial\Lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\partial\Lambda|}\big)

such that

(1)

For each
(u_{w})_{w\in\partial\Lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\partial\Lambda|}
, the marginal law of
\big(L(v;(u_{w})_{w\in\partial\Lambda}):v\in\Lambda\big)
is given by the
\mathpzc{HSLG}

\Theta
-Gibbs measure for the domain
\Lambda
with boundary condition
(u_{w})_{w\in\partial\Lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\partial\Lambda|}
.

(2)

With probability
1
, for all
v\in\Lambda
we have

L\big(v;(u_{w})_{w\in\partial\Lambda}\big)\leq L\big(v;(u_{w}^{\prime})_{w\in\partial\Lambda}\big)\mbox{ whenever }u_{w}\leq u_{w}^{\prime}\mbox{ for all }w\in\partial\Lambda.

Consequently, the probability of increasing events (defined in Definition
1.2
) increase with respect to decreasing the boundary conditions.

The above proposition is stated for the general
\mathpzc{HSLG}

\Theta
-Gibbs measure introduced in Definition
1.2
. In light of the second part of the above proposition, we will sometimes say that an increasing event is ‘increasing with respect to the boundary data’. The proof of the above proposition follows a similar strategy as in
[
BCD23
,
DW21b
]
and is provided in Appendix
A
for completeness.

2.2.
The
\mathpzc{HSLG}
line ensemble and Proof of Theorem
1.3

In this section we define the half-space log-gamma (
\mathpzc{HSLG}
) line ensemble and prove Theorem
1.3
. We work with the inhomogeneous polymer model determined by parameters
\vec{\theta}:=(\theta_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}}
. The construction of the line ensemble is based on the multi-path point-to-point partition functions. These are defined in (
2.10
) as sums over multiple non-intersecting paths on the full quadrant
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{2}
(not just half-quadrant) of products of the symmetrized versions of the weights from (
1.1
):

(2.9)

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}_{i,j}\sim\begin{cases}\tfrac{1}{2}W_{i,j}&\mbox{when }i=j,\\
W_{j,i}&\mbox{when }j>i,\\
W_{i,j}&\mbox{when }j<i.\end{cases}

For
m,n,r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
with
n\geq r
, let
\Pi_{m,n}^{(r)}
be the set of
r
-tuples of non-intersecting upright paths in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{2}
starting from
(1,r),(1,r-1),\cdots,(1,1)
and going to
(m,n),(m,n-1),\ldots,(m,n-r+1)
respectively. We define the multipath point-to-point symmetrized partition function as

(2.10)

\displaystyle Z_{\operatorname{sym}}^{(r)}(m,n):=\sum_{(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{r})\in\Pi_{m,n}^{(r)}}\prod_{(i,j)\in\pi_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\pi_{r}}\widetilde{W}_{i,j},

with the convention that
Z_{\operatorname{sym}}^{(0)}(m,n)\equiv 1
for all
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. The dependence on the
\vec{\theta}:=(\theta_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}}
parameters that determine the weights through (
1.1
) is suppressed here and below.

1
2N
\mathcal{L}_{1}(\cdot)
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\cdot)
\vdots
\mathcal{L}_{N}(\cdot)

Figure 8
.
The half-space log gamma line ensemble
\mathcal{L}=(\mathcal{L}_{i}(\cdot))_{i=1}^{N}
(
N=6
in above figure). Each curve
\mathcal{L}_{i}(\cdot)
has
2N-2i+2
many coordinates.
\Lambda_{N}^{*}
in Theorem
1.3
is the set of all black points in the above figure. Theorem
1.3
tells us that conditioned on the blue points, the law of the black points is given by the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs measures.

Definition 2.7

(Half-space log-gamma line ensemble)
.

Fix
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. For each
i\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
and
j\in\llbracket 1,2N-2i+2\rrbracket
, we set

\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}(j)=\log\left(\frac{2Z_{\operatorname{sym}}^{(i)}(p,q)}{Z_{\operatorname{sym}}^{(i-1)}(p,q)}\right)+2\Psi(\theta)N.

where
p:=N+\lfloor j/2\rfloor
and
q:=N-\lceil j/2\rceil+1
. We call the collection of random variables

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}(j):i\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket,j\in\llbracket 1,2N-2i+2\rrbracket\big)

the half-space log-gamma (
\mathpzc{HSLG}
) line ensemble with parameters
(\alpha,\theta)
, see Figure
8
.

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Recalling the convention
Z_{\operatorname{sym}}^{(0)}(m,n)\equiv 1
, we can write

\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(j)=\log\left(2Z_{\operatorname{sym}}^{(1)}(N+\lfloor j/2\rfloor,N-\lceil j/2\rceil+1)\right)+2\Psi(\theta)N.

Assuming Part
(ii)
of Theorem
1.3
(verified below), Part
(i)
follows immediately from the identity

2Z_{\operatorname{sym}}^{(1)}(p,q)=Z_{(\alpha,\vec{\theta})}(p,q)
. The above identity is noted in Section 2.1 of
[
BW22
]
and follows easily due to symmetry of the weights (the factor of
2
comes from a lack of double-counting the weight at
(1,1)
). This is an equality (not just in distribution).

Part
(ii)
is a highly non-trivial deduction from first principles. However, the works of
[
COSZ14
,
OSZ14
,
NZ17
,
BZ19
,
BW22
]
have built a rich theory using the geometric RSK correspondence from which this part follows in a rather straightforward manner, as now described. We seek to determine the joint density of the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
line ensemble defined above.
Let us start by defining

K_{N}:=\{(i,j):i\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket,j\in\llbracket 1,2N-2i+2\rrbracket\},\quad\mathcal{I}^{(N)}:=\{(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{2}:i+j\leq 2N+1\}.

Note that the map
(i,j)\mapsto(N+\lfloor j/2\rfloor-i+1,N-\lceil j/2\rceil-i+2)
is a bijection from
K_{N}
to
\mathcal{I}^{(N)}\cap\{i\geq j\}.
For any
(i,j)\in K_{N}
, we then define

T_{N+\lfloor j/2\rfloor-i+1,N-\lceil j/2\rceil-i+2}:=\frac{Z_{\operatorname{sym}}^{(i)}(N+\lfloor j/2\rfloor,N-\lceil j/2\rceil+1)}{Z_{\operatorname{sym}}^{(i-1)}(N+\lfloor j/2\rfloor,N-\lceil j/2\rceil+1)},

and then set
T_{j,i}:=T_{i,j}
for
i\geq j
. From Proposition 2.6 in
[
NZ17
]
,
(T_{i,j})_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{I}^{(N)}}
is precisely the image under the geometric RSK map of the symmetrized weights (
2.9
) with indices restricted to the
\mathcal{I}^{(N)}
array. The density of this image has been computed in
[
BW22
]
. Indeed, setting
m=0,n=N
,
\alpha_{i}=\theta_{i}
for
i\geq 1
, and
\alpha_{0}=\alpha
in the final two (unnumbered) equations on page 28 in
[
BW22
]
(in the arXiv version see the second unnumbered equation on page 20), we see that the density of
(2T_{i,j})_{i\geq j}
at
(t_{i,j})_{i\geq j}
is proportional to

(2.11)

{e^{-\frac{1}{t_{1,1}}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}t_{i,i}^{(-1)^{N-i+1}\alpha}}\prod_{j=1}^{N}\Big(\frac{\tau_{2N-2j+2}\cdot\tau_{2N-2j}}{\tau_{2N-2j+1}^{2}}\Big)^{\theta_{j}}\!\!\exp\Big(\!-\!\sum_{i\geq j>1}\frac{t_{i,j-1}}{t_{i,j}}-\sum_{i>j}\frac{t_{i-1,j}}{t_{i,j}}\Big)\!\!\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{I}^{(N)}}t_{i,j}^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{t_{i,j}>0}

where the
\tau
variables are defined as

\tau_{k}=\prod\big(t_{i,j}:(i,j)\in\mathcal{I}^{(N)},i-j=k\big)=\prod\big(t_{i+k,i}:1\leq i\leq N-\frac{k-1}{2}\big).

In fact, the density formula in
[
BW22
]
is for
(2T_{i,j})_{i\leq j}
at
(t_{i,j})_{i\leq j}
, thus we needed to permute the indices in that formula to arrive at the above formula. The line ensemble
\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}(j)
defined in Definition
2.7
is related to
(2T_{i,j})_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{I}^{(N)}}
via the relation

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}(j)-2\Psi(\theta)N=\log\big(T_{N+\lfloor j/2\rfloor-i+1,N-\lceil j/2\rceil-i+2}\big).

Under the change of variables
u_{i,j}=\log\big(t_{N+\lfloor j/2\rfloor-i+1,N-\lceil j/2\rceil-i+2}\big)
for
(i,j)\in K_{N}
, we claim that the density of
(\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}(j)-2\Psi(\theta)N)
at
(u_{i,j})_{(i,j)\in K_{N}}
is proportional to

(2.12)

\displaystyle{e^{-e^{-u_{N,1}}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}e^{(-1)^{i}u_{i,1}\alpha}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\left(e^{-\theta_{i}u_{i,2N-2i+2}}\prod_{j=1}^{N-i+1}e^{\theta_{N-j+1}(u_{i,2j-1}-u_{i,2j})}\prod_{j=1}^{N-i}e^{-\theta_{N-j+1}(u_{i,2j}-u_{i,2j+1})}\right)

(2.13)

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\cdot\exp\left(-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N-i+1}e^{u_{i,2j-1}-u_{i,2j}}-\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\sum_{j=1}^{N-i}e^{u_{i+1,2j}-u_{i,2j+1}}\right)

(2.14)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\cdot\exp\left(-\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\sum_{j=1}^{N-i}e^{u_{i,2j+1}-u_{i,2j}}-\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\sum_{j=1}^{N-i}e^{u_{i+1,2j}-u_{i,2j-1}}\right).

The justification of going from (
2.11
) to (
2.12
)-(
2.14
) is given in Appendix
D
.
Recall now that we are interested in the density conditioned on
(\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}(j)-2\Psi(\theta)N)
at
(u_{i,j})_{(i,j)\in K_{N}\setminus\Lambda_{N}^{*}}
. To compute this conditional density we may absorb all the
u_{i,j}
terms with
(i,j)\in K_{N}\setminus\Lambda_{N}^{*}
into the proportionality constant. Thus in (
2.12
), we may absorb the
e^{-u_{N,1}}
term and
e^{-\theta_{i}u_{i,2N-2i+2}}
terms and observe

\prod_{i=1}^{N}e^{(-1)^{i}u_{i,1}\alpha}\propto\prod_{i\in\llbracket 1,N/2\rrbracket}e^{-\alpha(u_{2i-1,1}-u_{2i,1})}.

Upon a quick inspection of the form of the weight function in (
1.5
), one sees that these factors are precisely the red edge weights functions in the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs measure on the domain
\Lambda_{N}^{*}
; see Figure
3
(B) and Definition
1.2
. Combining the terms which have
(u_{i,2j-1}-u_{i,2j})
and
(u_{i,2j+1}-u_{i,2j})
in (
2.12
), (
2.13
), (
2.14
) give rise to the following factor

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\prod_{j=1}^{N-i+1}\exp\big(\theta_{N-j+1}(u_{i,2j-1}-u_{i,2j})-e^{u_{i,2j-1}-u_{i,2j}}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{N-1}\prod_{j=1}^{N-i}\exp\big(\theta_{N-j+1}(u_{i,2j+1}-u_{i,2j})-e^{u_{i,2j+1}-u_{i,2j}}\big).

The above factor corresponds to the blue edge weight functions in the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs measure on the domain
\Lambda_{N}^{*}
. Finally, the remaining terms in (
2.13
) and (
2.14
) corresponds to black edge weight function in the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs measure on the domain
\Lambda_{N}^{*}
. Thus the density of
\{\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}(j)-2\Psi(\theta)N:(i,j)\in\Lambda_{N}^{*}\}
conditioned on
\{\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}(j)-2\Psi(\theta)N:(i,j)\in K_{N}\setminus\Lambda_{N}^{*}\}
is precisely given by the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs measure with boundary condition
\{\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}(j)-2\Psi(\theta)N:(i,j)\in K_{N}\setminus\Lambda_{N}^{*}\}
as in Definition
1.2
. By the Gibbs measures translation invariance (Lemma
2.1

(a)
), we obtain Theorem
1.3

(ii)
.
∎

3.
Properties of the first three curves

In this section we extract probabilistic information about the first few curves of
\mathpzc{HSLG}
line ensemble
\mathcal{L}^{N}
(Definition
2.7
). In Section
3.1
we prove Theorem
3.1
, which claims that there is a certain high probability ordering among the points of the curve. Section
3.2
contains Theorem
3.3
which asserts that with high probability there is a point
p=O(N^{2/3})
such that
\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(p)
is reasonably large.
Finally in Section
3.3
, we show Theorem
3.8
which argues that with high probability
(\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(s))_{s\in\llbracket 1,kN^{2/3}\rrbracket}
and
(\mathcal{L}_{3}^{N}(s))_{s\in\llbracket 1,kN^{2/3}\rrbracket}
lie below
MN^{1/3}
for large enough
M
.

3.1.
Ordering of the points in the line ensemble

In this subsection we show that with high probability there is ordering among the points of the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
line ensemble. Throughout this subsection we shall assume
\alpha\in(-\theta,\infty)
is a fixed parameter. The results can be easily extended to the case where
\alpha=\alpha(N)
satisfying

\displaystyle-\theta<\liminf_{N\to\infty}\alpha(N)\leq\limsup_{N\to\infty}\alpha(N)<\infty.

We consider the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
line ensemble
\mathcal{L}^{N}
from Definition
2.7
with parameter
(\alpha,\theta)
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Fix any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
\rho\in(0,1)
. There exists
N_{0}=N_{0}(\rho,k)>0
such that for all
N\geq N_{0}
,
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
and
p\in\llbracket 1,N-k-2\rrbracket
the following inequalities holds:

(3.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}(2p+1)\leq\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}(2p)+(\log N)^{7/6}\big)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\rho^{N},

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}(2p-1)\leq\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}(2p)+(\log N)^{7/6}\big)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\rho^{N},

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{L}_{i+1}^{N}(2p)\leq\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}(2p+1)+(\log N)^{7/6}\big)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\rho^{N},

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{L}_{i+1}^{N}(2p)\leq\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}(2p-1)+(\log N)^{7/6}\big)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\rho^{N}.

We refer to the caption of Figure
9
for a visual interpretation of the above Theorem. The
7/6
appearing above can be replaced with any
\gamma>1
.
N_{0}
will also depend on
\gamma
in that case. In order to prove the above theorem, we first provide an apriori loose bound for the entries of the first
k
curves of the line ensemble
\mathcal{L}^{N}
.

Proposition 3.2
.

Fix any
\rho\in(0,1)
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. There exists a constant
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}(\rho,k)>0
and
N_{0}(\rho,k)>0
such that for all
N\geq N_{0}
,
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
,
j\in\llbracket 1,2N-2i+2\rrbracket
we have

(3.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}(j)|\leq\mathrm{C}\cdot N\right)\geq 1-\rho^{N}.

We first prove Theorem
3.1
assuming Proposition
3.2
.

\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(2)
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(4)
\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(5)
\mathcal{L}_{3}^{N}(3)
\mathcal{L}_{4}^{N}(1)
\mathcal{L}_{4}^{N}(6)

Figure 9
.
The ordering of points within the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
line ensemble. The figure depicts of first four curves of the line ensemble
\mathcal{L}
. Points
v
and
v^{\prime}
connected by a black or blue arrow from
v\to v^{\prime}
satisfy that
\mathcal{L}^{N}(v^{\prime})\geq\mathcal{L}^{N}(v)+(\log N)^{7/6}
with high probability (recall that for
v=(i,j)
we write
\mathcal{L}^{N}(v)=\mathcal{L}^{N}_{i}(j)
). The blue arrows thus imply ordering within a particular indexed curve while the black arrow imply ordering between the two consecutive curves.

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Fix any
\rho\in(0,1)
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. Set
T:=N-k
. Fix
i_{0}\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
and
p\in\llbracket 1,T-2\rrbracket
. We will show only the first of the inequalities in (
3.1
), as the rest are all proved analogously. For simplicity, we write
\mathcal{L}
for
\mathcal{L}^{N}
. Consider the event

\displaystyle\mathsf{V}:=\left\{\mathcal{L}_{i_{0}}(2p+1)\geq\mathcal{L}_{i_{0}}(2p)+(\log N)^{7/6}\right\}.

We apply Proposition
3.2
with
k\mapsto k+1
and
\rho\mapsto\rho/2
to get
\mathrm{C}>0
so that for all large enough
N
, by union bound we have
\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{A})\geq 1-2Nk\cdot(\rho/2)^{N}
where

\displaystyle\mathsf{A}:=\Big\{|\mathcal{L}_{k+1}(j)|,|\mathcal{L}_{i}(2T-1)|\leq\mathrm{C}\cdot N,\textrm{ for all }j\in\llbracket 1,2T\rrbracket,i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\Big\}.

Thus if we consider the
\sigma
-algebra

\mathcal{F}:=\sigma\Big(\mathcal{L}_{k+1}(j),\mathcal{L}_{i}(2T-1):\ j\in\llbracket 1,2T\rrbracket,i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\Big),

by union bound and tower property of the conditional expectation we have

(3.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{V})\leq\mathbb{P}(\neg\mathsf{A})+\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{V}\cap\mathsf{A})\leq 2Nk\cdot(\rho/2)^{N}+\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{V}}\mid\mathcal{F}]\right].

Recall
\mathcal{K}_{k,T}^{\prime}
from (
2.4
). From Theorem
1.3
and Lemma
2.1

(b)
, the law of
\{\mathcal{L}(v):v\in\mathcal{K}_{k,T}^{\prime}\}
conditioned on
\mathcal{F}
is given by the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs measure on the domain
\mathcal{K}_{k,T}^{\prime}
with boundary condition
\vec{y}:=\{\mathcal{L}_{j}(2T-1)\}_{j=1}^{k}
.
\vec{z}:=\{\mathcal{L}_{k+1}(2i)\}_{i=1}^{T-1}
. In view of Lemma
2.2

(b)
we see that

(3.4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{V}}\mid\mathcal{F}]=\frac{\int_{\mathsf{V}}Q_{k,T}^{\vec{y},\vec{z}}(\mathbf{u})d\mathbf{u}}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{K}_{k,T}^{\prime}|}}Q_{k,T}^{\vec{y},\vec{z}}(\mathbf{u})d\mathbf{u}}

where
Q_{k,T}^{\vec{y},\vec{z}}(\mathbf{u})
is defined in (
2.6
). We will now bound the numerator and denominator of (
3.4
) respectively. We claim that there exists
R,\tau>0
depending only on
k,\alpha,\theta,\mathrm{C}
such that

(3.5)

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}\cdot\int_{\mathsf{V}}Q_{k,T}^{\vec{y},\vec{z}}(\mathbf{u})d\mathbf{u}\leq\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}\exp(-\tfrac{1}{2}e^{(\log N)^{7/6}})\cdot R^{N},\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{K}_{k,T}^{\prime}|}}Q_{k,T}^{\vec{y},\vec{z}}(\mathbf{u})d\mathbf{u}\geq\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}\cdot\tau^{N}.

Clearly plugging this bounds back in (
3.4
) and then back in (
3.3
) leads to
\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{V})\leq\rho^{N}
for all large enough
N
, as desired. Thus we focus on proving the two inequalities in (
3.5
).

Proof of the first inequality in (
3.5
).
Recall
G
defined in (
2.2
). Set

H_{\theta,(-1)^{k}}(y):=e^{\frac{1}{2}e^{(-1)^{k}y}}\cdot G_{\theta,(-1)^{k}}(y)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\theta)}\exp({\theta(-1)^{k}y-\tfrac{1}{2}e^{(-1)^{k}y}}).

Set
\sqrt{W}(a;b,c):=\sqrt{W(a;b,c)}
where
W
is defined in (
2.1
). From (
2.6
) we have

\displaystyle Q_{k,T}^{\vec{y},\vec{z}}(\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left(e^{(-1)^{i}\alpha u_{i,1}}\prod_{j=1}^{T-1}\sqrt{W}(u_{i+1,2j};u_{i,2j+1},u_{i,2j-1})\prod_{j=1}^{2T-2}H_{\theta,(-1)^{j+1}}(u_{i,j}-u_{i,j+1})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{T-1}\sqrt{W}(u_{i+1,2j};u_{i,2j+1},u_{i,2j-1})\prod_{j=1}^{2T-2}\exp(-\tfrac{1}{2}e^{(-1)^{j+1}(u_{i,j}-u_{i,j+1})})\right).

On
\mathsf{V}
, among the terms appearing in the last line of the above equation, the term
\exp(-\frac{1}{2}e^{u_{2p+1,i_{0}}-u_{2p,i_{0}}})
is at most
\exp(-\frac{1}{2}e^{(\log N)^{7/6}})
. We bound the rest of the terms in the above last line just by
1
, so that on
\mathsf{V}
, we have
Q_{k,T}^{\vec{y},\vec{z}}(\mathbf{u})\leq e^{-\frac{1}{2}e^{(\log N)^{7/6}}}\widetilde{Q}_{k,T}^{\vec{y},\vec{z}}(\mathbf{u})
where

\displaystyle\widetilde{Q}_{k,T}^{\vec{y},\vec{z}}(\mathbf{u}):=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left(e^{(-1)^{i}\alpha u_{i,1}}\prod_{j=1}^{T-1}\sqrt{W}(u_{i+1,2j};u_{i,2j+1},u_{i,2j-1})\prod_{j=1}^{2T-2}H_{\theta,(-1)^{j+1}}(u_{i,j}-u_{i,j+1})\right).

By Lemma
B.2
it follows that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{K}_{k,T}^{\prime}|}}\widetilde{Q}_{k,T}^{\vec{y},\vec{z}}(\mathbf{u})d\mathbf{u}\leq R^{N}
for some
R>0
depending on
k,\alpha,\theta
and
\mathrm{C}
only. This verifies the first inequality in (
3.5
).

Proof of the second inequality in (
3.5
).
We define the event

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}:=\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}\bigcap_{j=1}^{2T-2}\big\{\mathcal{L}_{i}(j)-CN-2N+2i\in[0,1]\big\}.

Note that on
\mathsf{D}
,
|\mathcal{L}_{i}(1)|\leq CN+2N+3
and
\mathcal{L}_{i+1}(2j)\leq L_{i}(2j+1),\mathcal{L}_{i}(2j-1)
. Hence on
\mathsf{D}
we have

W(\mathcal{L}_{i+1}(2j);\mathcal{L}_{i}(2j+1),\mathcal{L}_{i}(2j-1))=\exp\left(-e^{\mathcal{L}_{i+1}(2j)-\mathcal{L}_{i}(2j+1)}-e^{\mathcal{L}_{i+1}(2j)-\mathcal{L}_{i}(2j-1)}\right)\geq e^{-2}.

Hence on
\mathsf{D}
we have

\displaystyle Q_{k,T}^{\vec{y},\vec{z}}(\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left(e^{(-1)^{i}\alpha u_{i,1}}\prod_{j=1}^{T-1}W(u_{i+1,2j};u_{i,2j+1},u_{i,2j-1})\hskip-5.69046pt\prod_{j=1}^{2T-2}G_{\theta,(-1)^{j+1}}(u_{i,j}-u_{i,j+1})\right)

\displaystyle\geq e^{-\alpha k(CN+2N)}e^{-2kT}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\prod_{j=1}^{2T-2}G_{\theta,(-1)^{j+1}}(u_{i,j}-u_{i,j+1}).

Again note that on
\mathsf{D}
,
|\mathcal{L}_{i}(j)-\mathcal{L}_{i}(j+1)|\leq 2
for all
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
and
j\in\llbracket 1,2T-3\rrbracket
, whereas on
\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{D}
,

\mathcal{L}_{i}(2T-2)-\mathcal{L}_{i}(2T-1)\in[0,2CN+2N].

Thus, on
\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{D}

\displaystyle Q_{k,T}^{\vec{y},\vec{z}}(\mathbf{u})\geq e^{-\alpha k(CN+2N)-2kT}\left(\inf_{|x|\leq 2}G_{\theta,1}(x)\right)^{k(2T-3)}\left(\inf_{x\in[0,2CN+2N]}G_{\theta,1}(-x)\right)^{k}.

Note that the lower tail of
G_{\theta,1}(x)
is exponential. Thus
\inf_{x\in[0,2CN+2N]}G_{\theta,1}(-x)\geq\tau_{1}^{N}
for some
\tau_{1}>0
depending on
\alpha,\theta,
and
C
. Thus overall on
\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{D}
,
Q_{k,T}^{\vec{y},\vec{z}}(\mathbf{u})\geq\tau^{N}
for some
\tau
depending on
\alpha,\theta,k,
and
C
.
Since the Lebesgue measure of
\mathsf{D}
is
1
we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{K}_{k,T}^{\prime}|}}Q_{k,T}^{\vec{y},\vec{z}}(\mathbf{u})d\mathbf{u}\geq\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}\int_{\mathsf{D}}Q_{k,T}^{\vec{y},\vec{z}}(\mathbf{u})d\mathbf{u}\geq\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}\cdot\tau^{N}\int_{\mathsf{D}}d\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}\cdot\tau^{N}.

This proves the second inequality in (
3.5
) completing the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.2
.

Recall
\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}(j)
from Definition
2.7
. Fix any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
\rho\in(0,1)
. For all
r\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
and
j\in\llbracket 1,2N-2i+2\rrbracket
set

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{r}(j):=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}(j)

\displaystyle=r\log 2+2r\Psi(\theta)N+\log Z_{\operatorname{sym}}^{(r)}(N+\lfloor j/2\rfloor,N-\lceil j/2\rceil+1),

where recall
Z_{\operatorname{sym}}^{(r)}(\cdot,\cdot)
defined in (
2.10
). Set
\mathcal{B}_{0}(j)\equiv 0
. We claim that there exist
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}(\rho,k)>0
and
N_{0}=N_{0}(\rho,k)>0
, such that for all
N\geq N_{0}
and
r\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket

(3.6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\log Z_{\operatorname{sym}}^{(r)}(N+\lfloor j/2\rfloor,N-\lceil j/2\rceil+1)\right|\leq\mathrm{C}\cdot N\right)\geq 1-\rho^{N}.

Setting
\mathrm{C}^{\prime}=\mathrm{C}+2k|\Psi(\theta)|+k\log 2
we see from above that, by triangle inequality and union bound

\mathbb{P}(|\mathcal{L}_{r}(j)|\leq 2\mathrm{C}^{\prime}\cdot N)\geq\mathbb{P}(|\mathcal{B}_{r-1}(j)|\leq\mathrm{C}^{\prime}\cdot N)+\mathbb{P}(|\mathcal{B}_{r}(j)|\leq\mathrm{C}^{\prime}\cdot N)-1\geq 1-2\cdot\rho^{N}.

Adjusting
\rho,N_{0}
the above inequality yields (
3.2
). The rest of the proof is devoted in proving (
3.6
).

Recall that
Z_{\operatorname{sym}}^{(r)}(\cdot,\cdot)
, defined in (
2.10
), can be viewed as sum of weights of
r
-tuple of non-intersecting paths. We first provide concentration bound for weight of a given path
\pi
with endpoints in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{sym}}^{(N)}:=\{(i,j):i+j\leq 2N+1\}
via standard Chernoff bound for i.i.d. random variables. Then we provide an upper bound on the number of
r
-tuple of non-intersecting paths. Via union bound, this gives a concentration bound of type (
3.6
) for
Z_{\operatorname{sym}}^{(r)}(\cdot,\cdot)
.

Recall the symmetric weight
\widetilde{W}_{i,j}
from (
2.9
). Note that for an upright path
\pi
,
(i,j)\in\pi
and
(j,i)\in\pi
cannot happen simultaneously provided
i\neq j
. Thus
(\widetilde{W}_{i,j})_{(i,j)\in\pi}
forms an independent collection. Set

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle:=\max\{\log\Gamma(\theta)-\log\Gamma(2\theta),\log\Gamma(\alpha)-\theta\log 2-\log\Gamma(\alpha+\theta)\},

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle:=\max\{\log\Gamma(3\theta)-\log\Gamma(2\theta),\log\Gamma(\alpha+2\theta)+\theta\log 2-\log\Gamma(\alpha+\theta)\}.

Using moments of Gamma distribution and Markov inequality for each
s>0
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{(i,j)\in\pi}\log\widetilde{W}_{i,j}\geq\tfrac{s+R_{1}}{\theta}|\pi|\right)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-(s+R_{1})|\pi|}\prod_{(i,j)\in\pi}\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{W}_{i,j}^{\theta}]

\displaystyle=e^{-(s+R_{1})|\pi|}\prod_{(i,j)\in\pi,i\neq j}\frac{\Gamma(\theta)}{\Gamma(2\theta)}\prod_{(i,i)\in\pi}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha)}{2^{\theta}\Gamma(\alpha+\theta)}\leq e^{-s|\pi|},

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{(i,j)\in\pi}\log\widetilde{W}_{i,j}\leq-\tfrac{s+R_{2}}{\theta}|\pi|\right)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-(s+R_{2})|\pi|}\prod_{(i,j)\in\pi}\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{W}_{i,j}^{-\theta}]

\displaystyle=e^{-(s+R_{2})|\pi|}\prod_{(i,j)\in\pi,i\neq j}\frac{\Gamma(3\theta)}{\Gamma(2\theta)}\prod_{(i,i)\in\pi}\frac{2^{\theta}\Gamma(\alpha+2\theta)}{\Gamma(\alpha+\theta)}\leq e^{-s|\pi|}.

This leads to the following concentration bound

(3.7)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg(\bigg|\sum_{(i,j)\in\pi}\log\widetilde{W}_{i,j}\bigg|\leq\tfrac{s+R_{1}+R_{2}}{\theta}|\pi|\bigg)\geq 1-2e^{-s|\pi|}.

To upgrade the above bound to (
3.6
), we need an upper bound for the number of
r
-tuples of non-intersecting upright paths. To do this, we introduce a few notations. Set
m:=N+\lfloor j/2\rfloor
,
n:=N-\lceil j/2\rceil+1
. Given two points
(i_{1},j_{1}),(i_{2},j_{2})\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{sym}}^{(N)}
, let
F_{N}((i_{1},j_{1})\to(i_{2},j_{2}))
be the set of all upright paths from
(i_{1},j_{1})
to
(i_{2},j_{2})
. For any
\pi\in\Pi_{(m,n)}^{(r)}
we have
N\leq|\pi|\leq 2N
. Furthermore,
|F_{N}((i_{1},j_{1})\to(i_{2},j_{2}))|\leq 4^{N}
for all
(i_{1},j_{1}),(i_{2},j_{2})\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{sym}}^{(N)}
. Thus
|\Pi_{(m,n)}^{(r)}|\leq 4^{kN}
as
r\leq k
. Fix
s=s(\rho,k)>0
such that
4^{kN}\cdot 2e^{-sN}\leq\rho^{N}
and consider the event

\displaystyle\mathsf{A}:=\bigg\{\bigg|\log\prod_{(i,j)\in\pi_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\pi_{r}}\widetilde{W}_{i,j}\bigg|\leq\tfrac{s+R_{1}+R_{2}}{\theta}\cdot 2rN\mbox{ for all }(\pi_{q})_{q=1}^{r}\in\Pi_{(m,n)}^{(r)}\bigg\}.

Applying the concentration bound (
3.7
) for each path in
\Pi_{(m,n)}^{(r)}
, an union bound yields

(3.8)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{A}\right)\geq 1-4^{kN}\cdot 2e^{-sN}\geq 1-\rho^{N}.

Next set
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}(\rho,k):=k\log 4+\frac{s+R_{1}+R_{2}}{\theta}2k
. Note that on
\mathsf{A}
we have

(3.9)

\displaystyle\log Z_{\operatorname{sym}}^{(r)}(m,n)

\displaystyle\leq\log\left(\sum_{(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{r})\in\Pi_{(m,n)}^{(r)}}\prod_{(i,j)\in\pi_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\pi_{r}}\widetilde{W}_{i,j}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\log\left(4^{kN}\cdot e^{\frac{s+R_{1}+R_{2}}{\theta}2rN}\right)\leq kN\log 4+\tfrac{s+R_{1}+R_{2}}{\theta}2kN\leq\mathrm{C}\cdot N.

Similarly for the lower bound we consider any
(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{r})\in\Pi_{(m,n)}^{(r)}
which forms a disjoint collection of paths. Then on
\mathsf{A}
we have

(3.10)

\displaystyle\log Z_{\operatorname{sym}}^{(r)}(m,n)\geq\log\left(\prod_{(i,j)\in\pi_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\pi_{r}}\widetilde{W}_{i,j}\right)\geq-\tfrac{s+R_{1}+R_{2}}{\theta}2kN\geq-\mathrm{C}\cdot N.

Now (
3.6
) follows from (
3.9
), (
3.10
) and the bound in (
3.8
).
∎

3.2.
High point on the second curve

The goal of this subsection is to show there is a point
p=O(N^{2/3})
such that with high probability
\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(2p)\geq-\mathrm{C}N^{1/3}
where
\mathcal{L}^{N}
is the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
line ensemble defined in Definition
2.7
. For the rest of this section we work with the boundary parameter fixed in the critical or supercritical phase. We assume
\alpha
equals
\alpha_{1}
or
\alpha_{2}
where

(3.11)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\alpha_{1}:=\alpha_{1}(N):=N^{-1/3}\mu&\mbox{(Critical)}\\
\alpha_{2}:=\zeta&\mbox{(Super-Critical)}\end{cases}

where
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
and
\zeta>0
are fixed numbers. The labeling of the parameter might seem a bit unnatural at this moment. Essentially, when the boundary parameter is
\alpha_{i}
, we shall resample the top
i
curves of the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
line ensemble in the arguments of Section
4
.

Theorem 3.3

(High point on the second curve)
.

Fix any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
k>0
. There exist
R_{0}(k,\varepsilon)>0
such that for all
R\geq R_{0}

(3.12)

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{p\in[kN^{2/3},RN^{2/3}]}\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(2p)\geq-\big(\tfrac{1}{8}R^{2}\nu+2\sqrt{R}\big)N^{1/3}\right)>1-\varepsilon.

where

(3.13)

\displaystyle\nu:=\frac{(\Psi^{\prime}(\theta))^{2}}{(-\Psi^{\prime\prime}(\theta))^{4/3}}.

The factor
1/8
appearing in (
3.12
) can be replaced by any constant
\gamma>0
.
R_{0}
will depend on
\gamma
in that case. The proof of Theorem
3.3
relies on two results related to the first curve.

Proposition 3.4

(High point on the first curve)
.

Fix any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. There exists
M_{0}(\varepsilon)>0
such that for all
M_{1},M_{2}\geq M_{0}
and
k>0
we have

(3.14)

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{p\in\llbracket kN^{2/3},(M_{1}+2k)N^{2/3}\rrbracket}\frac{\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(2p+1)}{N^{1/3}}+k^{2}\nu\leq M_{2}\right)>1-\varepsilon,

(3.15)

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{p\in\llbracket kN^{2/3},(M_{1}+2k)N^{2/3}\rrbracket}\frac{\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(2p+1)}{N^{1/3}}+k^{2}\nu\geq-M_{2}\right)>1-\varepsilon.

where
\nu
is defined in (
3.13
).

Figure
10
depicts the high probability events considered in Proposition
3.4
.

Figure 10
.
Events considered in Proposition
3.4
. Here
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(2p+1)
is given by the black rough curve. The parabolic curves
f_{\pm}(x):=-(N\nu)^{-1}x^{2}\pm M_{2}N^{1/3}
are also depicted. Horizontal lines eminate from these parabolas starting at
x=kN^{2/3}
. The event in (
3.14
) tells us that on the horizontal interval
\mathcal{I}_{k}:=\llbracket kN^{2/3},(M_{1}+2k)N^{2/3}\rrbracket
the black rough curve stays entirely below the black horizontal line while the event in (
3.15
) tells us that there is a point in
\mathcal{I}_{k}
at which the black rough curve exceeds the red horizontal curve.

Proposition 3.5

(Low point on the first curve)
.

Fix any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. There exists
M_{0}(\varepsilon)
such that for all
M\geq M_{0}
,

(3.16)

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(2MN^{2/3}+1)\leq-\tfrac{1}{8}M^{2}N^{1/3}\nu\right)>1-\varepsilon,

where
\nu
is defined in (
3.13
).

Definition 3.6

(
n
-step random walk and bridge measures)
.

Recall the spaces
(\Omega^{p}_{n},\mathcal{F}^{p}_{n})
for
p\in\{1,2\}
from Definition
1.7
. For
p=2
that definition provided coordinate function notation
S_{i}(k):=\omega_{i}(k)
for
i\in\{1,2\}
and
k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
. For
p=1
similarly define coordinate functions
S(k):=\omega(k)
for
k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
. Recall
\mathsf{f}_{\theta}
from (
2.3
).

For
a\in\mathbb{R}
define the probability measure
\mathbb{P}^{n;a}
on
(\Omega^{1}_{n},\mathcal{F}^{1}_{n})
for a single
n
-step random walk started at
a
to be proportional to the product of the Dirac delta function
\delta_{\omega(1)=a}
and a density (against Lebesgue on
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
) given by

\displaystyle\prod_{k=2}^{n}\mathsf{f}_{\theta}\big(\omega(k)-\omega(k-1)\big)\,d\omega(k).

Similarly, for
(a_{1},a_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
define the probability measure
\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}
on
(\Omega^{2}_{n},\mathcal{F}^{2}_{n})
for a pair of independent
n
-step random walk started at
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
by taking the product of

\mathbb{P}`^{n;a_{1}}
and
\mathbb{P}^{n;a_{2}}
.

For
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
define the probability measure
\mathbb{P}^{n;a;b}

(\Omega^{1}_{n},\mathcal{F}^{1}_{n})
for a single
n
-step random bridge started at
a
and ended at
b
to be proportional to the product of two Dirac delta function
\delta_{\omega(1)=a}\delta_{\omega(n)=b}
and a density (against Lebesgue on
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
) given by

\displaystyle\prod_{k=2}^{n}\mathsf{f}_{\theta}\big(\omega(k)-\omega(k-1)\big)\prod_{k=2}^{n-1}d\omega(k).

Similarly, for
(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
define the probability measure
\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2});(b_{1},b_{2})}
on
(\Omega^{2}_{n},\mathcal{F}^{2}_{n})
for a pair of independent
n
-step random briges started at
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
and ended (respectively) at
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
by taking the product of

\mathbb{P}^{n;a_{1};b_{1}}
and
\mathbb{P}^{n;a_{2};b_{2}}
.

The proofs of Propositions
3.4
and
3.5
rely on the fluctuation results from
[
BW22
]
, as restated earlier in Theorem
1.5
, and are postponed to the next subsection. Assuming their validity, we complete the proof of Theorem
3.3
.

Proof of Theorem
3.3
.

For clarity we divide the proof into two steps.

Step 1.
In this step we define notation and events used in the proof. Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
k>0
. Take
M_{0}
from Proposition
3.4
. We set
R
large enough so that

(3.17)

\displaystyle 2^{-5}R\geq 2k+1,\qquad M_{0}-2^{-5}(\tfrac{1}{8}R^{2}\nu+M_{0})+R^{3/2}\leq-M_{0}-2^{-10}R^{2}\nu,\qquad R\geq 2M_{0}

and
Q:=2^{-5}R
. We will assume some additional conditions on
R
later, which will depend on certain probability bounds that will be specified in the next step. For convenience, we will also assume
kN^{2/3}
and
RN^{2/3}
are integers (instead of using floor functions below). We set

\displaystyle a:=M_{0}N^{1/3},\ \ b:=-\tfrac{1}{8}R^{2}N^{1/3}\nu,\ \ n:=RN^{2/3}-kN^{2/3}+1,\ \ v:=-\big(\tfrac{1}{8}R^{2}\nu+2\sqrt{R}\big)N^{1/3}.

Let us define the sets
\mathcal{I}:=\llbracket QN^{2/3},(M_{0}+2Q)N^{2/3}\rrbracket
and
\mathcal{J}:=\llbracket kN^{2/3},RN^{2/3}\rrbracket
. Due to (
3.17
), we have
\mathcal{I}\subset\mathcal{J}
. Next we define the following events:

\displaystyle\mathsf{A}

\displaystyle:=\bigg\{\sup_{p\in\mathcal{J}}{\mathcal{L}_{2}(2p)}\leq v\bigg\},\quad\mathsf{B}:=\bigg\{\mathcal{L}_{1}(2kN^{2/3}+1)\leq a,\mathcal{L}_{1}(2RN^{2/3}+1)\leq b\bigg\}.

The
\mathsf{A}
event demands that the second curve
\mathcal{L}^{N}_{2}(2p)
does not rise above
v
for any
p\in\mathcal{J}
. The
\mathsf{B}
event requires both
\mathcal{L}^{N}_{1}(2kN^{2/3}+1)
and
\mathcal{L}^{N}_{1}(2RN^{2/3}+1)
to be less than
a
and
b
respectively. Finally we set

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}:=\bigg\{\sup_{p\in\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{L}^{N}_{1}(2p+1)}+Q^{2}\nu N^{1/3}\geq-a\bigg\}

In words,
\mathsf{C}
ensures there exists some
p\in\mathcal{I}
such that
\mathcal{L}^{N}_{1}(2p+1)
is greater than
-a-Q^{2}\nu N^{1/3}
.

Note that by Proposition
3.4
we have
\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{C})\geq 1-\varepsilon
. Furthermore, by Propositions
3.4
and
3.5
for large enough
R
we also have
\mathbb{P}(\neg\mathsf{B})\leq 2\varepsilon
. We claim that
for all large enough
R
we have

(3.18)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{B}\cap\mathsf{C})\leq\varepsilon.

We prove (
3.18
) in the next step. Assuming this, note that by union bound we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\neg\mathsf{A})\geq\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{C})-\mathbb{P}(\neg\mathsf{B})-\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{B}\cap\mathsf{C})\geq 1-4\varepsilon.

Changing
\varepsilon\mapsto\varepsilon/4
we arrive at (
3.12
). This completes the proof modulo (
3.18
).

Figure 11
.
In this figure
\mathcal{L}^{N}_{1}(2p+1)
(black curve) and
\mathcal{L}^{N}_{2}(2p)
(blue curve) are plotted for
p\in\mathcal{J}
.
\mathsf{A}
denotes the event that the blue curve lies below the horizontal line
y=v
.
\mathsf{B}
denotes the event that the black curve starts below
a
and ends below
b
. The curve
f
in the figure is given by
f(x)=-(N\nu)^{-1}x^{2}-a
. The event
\mathsf{C}
denotes that there is a point
p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}
where the black rough curve stays above the red horizontal line (this event does not occur in the above figure). The key idea is that on
\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{B}
, the blue curve lies below
y=v
completely, and the black curve behaves like a simple random bridge and follows a linear trajectory with starting and ending points less than
a
and
b
respectively. As a result, the event
\mathsf{C}
(which requires the black curve to follow parabolic trajectory) does not occur with high probability. But we know both
\mathsf{B}
and
\mathsf{C}
occurs with high probability. Thus the event
\mathsf{A}
occurs with low probability.
