\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
\put(1.0,61.0){$a$}
\put(96.0,9.0){$b$}
\put(51.0,53.0){${\mathcal{K}}$}
\put(36.0,90.0){\tiny{$y=-a-Q^{2}\nu N^{1/3}$}}
\end{overpic}

Figure 12
.
In the above figure the random bridge
S(i)
from
a
to
b
is depicted by the black curve. The event
\mathsf{D}
ensures the random bridge lies below the blue line
y=a+\frac{x}{n}(b-a)+\sqrt{Rn}
. The event
\mathsf{C}
requires
S(i)\geq-\big(M_{0}+Q^{2}\nu\big)N^{1/3}
for some
i\in\mathcal{K}:=\llbracket(Q-k)N^{2/3},(M_{0}+2Q-k)N^{2/3}\rrbracket
. One can choose
R
large enough so that the horizontal black line
y=-a-Q^{2}\nu N^{1/3}=-\big(M_{0}+Q^{2}\nu\big)N^{1/3}
lies above the blue line
y=a+\frac{x}{n}(b-a)+\sqrt{Rn}
for all
x\geq(Q-k)N^{2/3}
. This forces
\mathsf{D}\subset\neg\mathsf{C}
.

Step 2.
In this step we will prove (
3.18
). The reader is encouraged to consult with Figure
11
and its caption to get an overview of the key idea behind the proof.

We consider the
\sigma
-algebra:

\mathcal{F}:=\sigma\big(\mathcal{L}^{N}_{2}\llbracket 1,2N-2i\rrbracket,\mathcal{L}^{N}_{1}(\llbracket 1,2kN^{2/3}+1\rrbracket\cup\llbracket 2RN^{2/3}+1,2N\rrbracket)\big).

Note that
\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{B}
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}
. Hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{B}\cap\mathsf{C})=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{B}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{C}}\mid\mathcal{F}\right]\right].

Using the Gibbs property for two-sided boundaries (see Lemma
2.5
), the conditional law is determined by the boundary data and is monotone with respect to the boundary data (see Proposition
2.6
). On the event
\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{B}
,
\mathcal{L}^{N}_{2}
(on even points) is at most
v
,
\mathcal{L}^{N}_{1}(2kN^{2/3}+1)
is at most
a
and
\mathcal{L}^{N}_{1}(2RN^{2/3}+1)
is at most
b
. Thus by stochastic monotonicity we have

(3.19)

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{B}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\big(\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{C}}\mid\mathcal{F}\big)\leq\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{B}}\cdot\frac{\mathbb{E}^{n;a;b}\left(W(S,v)\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{C}}\right)}{\mathbb{E}^{n;a;b}\left(W(S,v)\right)}\leq\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{B}}\cdot\frac{\mathbb{E}^{n;a;b}\left(\mathsf{C}\right)}{\mathbb{E}^{n;a;b}\left(W(S,v)\right)}.

where
S=(S(1),\ldots,S(n))
is distributed according to
\mathbb{P}^{n;a;b}
, the
n
-step random bridge measure from
a
to
b
, and where
W(S,v):=\exp\left(-2\sum_{i=2}^{n-1}e^{v-S(i)}\right)
. The event
\mathsf{C}
should now be treated as being defined in terms of
S
as

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}=\bigg\{\sup_{p\in\llbracket QN^{2/3},(M_{0}+2Q)N^{2/3}\rrbracket}S(p-kN^{2/3}+1)+Q^{2}\nu N^{1/3}\geq-a\bigg\}.

Note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{n;a;b}\left(W(S,v)\right)

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left(-2ne^{-\sqrt{n}}\right)\mathbb{P}^{n;a;b}\left(S(i)\geq v+\sqrt{n}\mbox{ for all }i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\right)

(3.20)

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left(-2ne^{-\sqrt{n}}\right)\mathbb{P}^{n;a;b}\left(S(i)-a-\tfrac{i(b-a)}{{n}}\geq-\sqrt{n}\mbox{ for all }i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\right).

where the last inequality follows by noting that
S(i)-a-\frac{i(b-a)}{n}\geq-\sqrt{n}
implies
S(i)\geq b-\sqrt{n}\geq v+\sqrt{n}
. Since random bridges weakly converge to Brownian bridges (see
[
Lig68
]
and
[
DW21a
]
for a quantitative version), using estimates for Brownian bridges, we see that the r.h.s. (
3.20
) is uniformly bounded below by some absolute constant
\delta
. We now claim that for all large enough
R

(3.21)

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}\subset\neg\mathsf{{C}},\quad\mathbb{P}^{n;a;b}(\mathsf{D})\geq 1-\varepsilon\delta,\ \ \mbox{ where }\ \mathsf{D}:=\left\{\sup_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket}\left(S(i)-a-\tfrac{i(b-a)}{n}\right)\leq\sqrt{R}\sqrt{n}\right\}.

Note that (
3.21
) implies
\mathbb{P}^{n;a;b}(\mathsf{C})\leq\varepsilon\delta
. Plugging this back in (
3.19
) along with the bound
\mathbb{E}^{n;a;b}\left(W(S,z)\right)\geq\delta
, yields that r.h.s. (
3.19
) is at most
\varepsilon
. This proves (
3.18
).

Let us now verify (
3.21
). Indeed,
\mathbb{P}^{n;a;b}(\mathsf{D})
can be made arbitrarily close to
1
by choosing
R
large enough (as random bridges weakly converge to Brownian bridges
[
Lig68
]
). We choose
R
so large that
\mathbb{P}^{n;a;b}(\mathsf{D})
is at least
1-\varepsilon\delta
.
Let us now verify
\mathsf{D}\subset\neg\mathsf{C}
(see also Figure
12
and its caption). For
q\geq Q
we see that

\displaystyle a+\tfrac{(q-k)(b-a)}{R-k}+\sqrt{R}\sqrt{n}

\displaystyle\leq\left(M_{0}-\tfrac{Q-k}{R-k}(\tfrac{1}{8}R^{2}\nu+M_{0})+R^{3/2}\right)N^{1/3}

\displaystyle\leq\left(M_{0}-2^{-5}(\tfrac{1}{8}R^{2}\nu+M_{0})+R^{3/2}\right)N^{1/3}\leq-\left(M_{0}+Q^{2}\nu\right)N^{1/3}

The penultimate inequality follows by observing that as
Q=2^{-5}R
, we have
Q-k\geq 2^{-5}(R-k)>0
. Finally the last inequality follows from (
3.17
). Thus for all
p\geq QN^{2/3}
,

\displaystyle x+\tfrac{(p-kN^{2/3})(y-x)}{(R-k)N^{2/3}}+\sqrt{R}\sqrt{n}\leq M_{0}N^{1/3}-Q^{2}\nu N^{1/3}

Clearly this implies
\mathsf{D}\subset\neg\mathsf{C}
, completing the proof (
3.21
).
∎

3.2.1.
Proof of Propositions
3.4
and
3.5

The proofs of Propositions
3.4
and
3.5
uses the following.

Lemma 3.7

(Uniform tightness)
.

Recall
Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}(m)
, the point-to-(partial)line partition function defined in (
1.8
). Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. There exists
K_{0}=K_{0}(\varepsilon)>0
, such that for all
M>0
and
K\geq K_{0}
we have

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(-K\leq\frac{\log Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}(MN^{2/3})+2\Psi(\theta)N}{N^{1/3}}+M^{2}\nu\leq K\right)>1-\varepsilon

where
\nu
is defined in (
3.13
).

We remark that the above lemma was alluded in the introduction in the form of (
1.9
).

Proof.

We recall the notations introduced in Section
1.3.2
. Fix any
M>0
. Set
k=MN^{2/3}
and
p:=1+\frac{2k}{N-k}
. Let
\theta_{c}
be the unique solution to
\Psi^{\prime}(\theta_{c})-p\Psi^{\prime}(2\theta-\theta_{c})=0
. Set
f_{\theta,p}=-\Psi(\theta_{c})-p\Psi(2\theta-\theta_{c})
and
\sigma_{\theta,p}^{3}=\frac{1}{2}(-\Psi^{\prime\prime}(\theta_{c})-\Psi^{\prime\prime}(2\theta-\theta_{c}))
where
\Psi
is the digamma function defined in (
1.4
). A straightforward calculation (done at the end of Appendix
D
) shows

(3.22)

(N-k)f_{\theta,p}=-2N\Psi(\theta)+M^{2}N^{1/3}(\Psi^{\prime}(\theta))^{2}/\Psi^{\prime\prime}(\theta)+O(1),\,\,\,\mbox{ and }\,\,\,\sigma_{\theta,p}/(-\Psi^{\prime\prime}(\theta))^{1/3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N\to\infty}}{{\to}}1,

where
O(1)
terms depend on
M,\theta
, but are bounded in
N
.
When
\alpha=\alpha_{2}>0
, we have that
\lim_{N\to\infty}(N-k)^{1/3}\sigma_{\theta,p}(\alpha_{2}+\theta-\theta_{c})=\infty
for each fixed
M>0
. Thus by Theorem
1.5
we get

\displaystyle\frac{\log Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}(MN^{2/3})+2\Psi(\theta)N}{(-N\Psi^{\prime\prime}(\theta))^{1/3}}+M^{2}\nu\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{\Longrightarrow}}\operatorname{TW}_{\operatorname{GUE}},

where
\operatorname{TW}_{\operatorname{GUE}}
is the GUE Tracy-Widom distribution
[
TW94
]
and
\nu
is defined in (
3.13
). For
\alpha=\alpha_{1}=N^{-1/3}\mu
, we have
\lim_{N\to\infty}(N-k)^{1/3}\sigma_{\theta,p}(\alpha_{1}+\theta-\theta_{c})=y:=\sigma_{\theta,1}(\mu-M\Psi^{\prime}(\theta)/\Psi^{\prime\prime}(\theta)).
Another application of Theorem
1.5
yields

\displaystyle\frac{\log Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}(MN^{2/3})+2\Psi(\theta)N}{(-N\Psi^{\prime\prime}(\theta))^{1/3}}+M^{2}\nu\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{\Longrightarrow}}U_{-y}.

where
U_{-y}
is the Baik-Ben Arous-Péché distribution
[
BAP05
]
(see
[
BW22
, (5.2)]
for definition). As
M\to\infty
, so does
y\to\infty
. Since
U_{-y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{\Longrightarrow}}\operatorname{TW}_{\operatorname{GUE}}
as
y\to\infty
(see
[
BR01b
, (2.36)]
), we thus get tightness uniformly in
M
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.4
.

Fix
k>0
,
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Since for any
M_{1}>0

\displaystyle\sup_{j\in\llbracket kN^{2/3},(M_{1}+2k)N^{2/3}\rrbracket}Z(N+j,N-j)\leq Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}(kN^{2/3}),

appealing to Lemma
3.7
with
M\mapsto k
we see that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{j\in\llbracket kN^{2/3},(M_{1}+2k)N^{2/3}\rrbracket}\frac{\log Z(N+j,N-j)+2\Psi(\theta)N}{N^{1/3}}+k^{2}\nu\leq M_{2}\right)\geq 1-\varepsilon,

where
M_{2}
can be chosen to be any
M\geq K_{0}
where
K_{0}(\varepsilon)
comes from Lemma
3.7
. Recalling that
\mathcal{L}^{N}_{1}(2j+1)=\log Z(N+j,N-j)+2\Psi(\theta)N
from (
1.7
), we get (
3.14
).

The remainder of the proof is now devoted in proving (
3.15
). Towards this end, set
K_{1}=\frac{1}{2}(M_{1}+2k)^{2}\nu
. Choose
M_{1}
large enough so that
K_{1}\geq K_{0}(\varepsilon/4)
where
K_{0}
comes from Lemma
3.7
. Applying Lemma
3.7
with
M\mapsto M_{1}+2k
,
K\mapsto K_{1}
, and
\varepsilon\mapsto\varepsilon/4
we have

(3.23)

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{\log Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}((M_{1}+2k)N^{2/3})+2\Psi(\theta)N}{N^{1/3}}\leq-\tfrac{1}{2}(M_{1}+2k)^{2}\nu\right)>1-\tfrac{1}{4}\varepsilon.

Now we take
K_{2}=(\frac{(M_{1}+2k)^{2}}{4}-k^{2})\nu-\log 2\geq\frac{1}{4}M_{1}^{2}\nu
. We again choose
M_{1}
large enough so that
K_{2}\geq K_{0}(\varepsilon/4)
. Then applying Lemma
3.7
with
M\mapsto k
,
K\mapsto K_{2}
, and
\varepsilon\mapsto\varepsilon/4
we have

(3.24)

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{\log Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}(kN^{2/3})+2\Psi(\theta)N}{N^{1/3}}\geq-\tfrac{1}{4}(M_{1}+2k)^{2}\nu+\log 2\right)>1-\tfrac{1}{4}\varepsilon.

By union bound the above two estimates implies for all large enough
M_{1}
we have

(3.25)

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}(kN^{2/3})>2\cdot Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}((M_{1}+2k)N^{2/3})\right)>1-\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon.

Let us temporarily set
A=Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}(kN^{2/3})-Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}((M_{1}+2k)N^{2/3}
and
B=Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}((M_{1}+2k)N^{2/3}
. Observe that
A+B>2B
implies
2A>A+B
. Recall from (
1.8
) that

A=\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\lceil kN^{2/3}\rceil}^{\lceil(M_{1}+2k)N^{2/3}\rceil-1}\!\!\!\!\!\!Z(N+j,N-j)\leq(M_{1}+k)N^{\frac{2}{3}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\sup_{j\in\llbracket kN^{\frac{2}{3}},(M_{1}+2k)N^{\frac{2}{3}}\rrbracket}\!\!\!\!\!\!Z(N+j,N-j).

We thus have

(3.26)

\displaystyle\big\{Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}(kN^{2/3})>2\cdot Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}((M_{1}+2k)N^{2/3})\big\}

\displaystyle\subset\bigg\{\sup_{j\in\llbracket kN^{\frac{2}{3}},(M_{1}+2k)N^{\frac{2}{3}}\rrbracket}\!\!\!\!\!\!\log Z(N+j,N-j)>\log Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}(kN^{\frac{2}{3}})-\log(2(M_{1}+k)N^{\frac{2}{3}})\bigg\}.

By Lemma
3.7
, one can choose
M_{2}
large enough (but free of
k
) so that

\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\Big(\log Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}(kN^{2/3})+2\Psi(\theta)N+k^{2}\nu N^{1/3}\geq-M_{2}N^{\frac{1}{3}}+\log(2(M_{1}+k)N^{\frac{2}{3}})\Big)>1-\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon.

Using this, in view of (
3.26
) and (
3.25
), and using
\mathcal{L}^{N}_{1}(2j+1)=\log Z(N+j,N-j)+2\Psi(\theta)N
(see (
1.7
)) we arrive at (
3.15
). This proves Proposition
3.4
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.5
.

We use the same notations as from the proof of Proposition
3.4
and utilize (
3.23
) and (
3.24
) obtained there with
k=1
. Set
M=M_{1}+2
. Combining (
3.23
) and (
3.24
) implies

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\log Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}(N^{2/3})>\tfrac{1}{4}M^{2}N^{1/3}\nu+\log Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}(MN^{2/3})\right)\geq 1-\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon.

As
Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}(MN^{2/3})\geq Z(N+MN^{2/3};N-MN^{2/3})
, this leads to

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\log Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}(N^{2/3})>\tfrac{1}{4}M^{2}N^{1/3}\nu+\log Z(N+MN^{2/3},N-MN^{2/3})\right)\geq 1-\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon.

Again by Lemma
3.7
, one can choose
M
large enough so that

\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\bigg(\log Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}(N^{2/3})\leq\tfrac{1}{8}M^{2}N^{1/3}\nu-2\Psi(\theta)N\bigg)>1-\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon,

which forces

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\log Z(N+MN^{2/3};N-MN^{2/3})<-2N\Psi(\theta)-\tfrac{1}{8}M^{2}N^{1/3}\nu\right)\geq 1-\varepsilon.

By (
1.7
),
\mathcal{L}^{N}_{1}(2MN^{2/3}+1)=\log Z(N+MN^{2/3};N-MN^{2/3})-2\Psi(\theta)N
hence (
3.16
) follows. ∎

3.3.
Spatial properties of the lower curves

In this subsection, we study spatial properties of the lower curves of the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
line ensemble. The main result of this section is the following.

Theorem 3.8
.

Fix any
p\in\{1,2\}
. Set
\alpha:=\alpha_{p}
according to (
3.11
). Consider the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
line ensemble from Definition
2.7
with parameters
(\alpha,\theta)
. Given any
k,\varepsilon>0
, there exist constants
M=M(k,\varepsilon)\geq 1
and
N_{0}(k,\varepsilon)\geq 1
such that for all
N\geq N_{0}(k,\varepsilon)
and
v\in\{2,3\}
we have

(3.27)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{s\in\llbracket 1,kN^{2/3}\rrbracket}\mathcal{L}_{v}^{N}(s)\geq MN^{1/3}\right)\leq\varepsilon.

In plain words, Theorem
3.8
argues that with high probability on the domain
\llbracket 1,kN^{2/3}\rrbracket
, the entire second curve and third curve lies below a threshold
MN^{1/3}
. The proof of Theorem
3.8
can be easily extended to include other lower indexed curves as well. However, for the proofs of our main results, it suffices to consider the first three curves.

Recall from Theorem
1.3
that the conditional laws of the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
line ensemble are given by
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs measures introduced in Definition
1.2
. The key technical ingredient in proving Theorem
3.8
is the tightness of left boundary points of the first two curves under the bottom-free measure defined in Definition
2.4
.

Proposition 3.9
.

Fix any
p\in\{1,2\}
. Set
\alpha:=\alpha_{p}
according to (
3.11
). Fix any
r\geq 1
and
\varepsilon>0
. Set
T=\lfloor rN^{2/3}\rfloor
. Define

(3.28)

\displaystyle A:=\begin{cases}1+\sqrt{r}|\mu|\Psi^{\prime}(\frac{1}{2}\theta)&\mbox{if }p=1,\\
1&\mbox{if }p=2.\end{cases}

There exists
M=M(\varepsilon)>0
and
N_{0}(\varepsilon)>0
such that for all
N\geq N_{0}
we have

(3.29)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{p}}^{(0,-A\sqrt{T}),(-\infty)^{T};2,T}\big(|L_{1}(1)|+|L_{2}(2)|\geq M\sqrt{T}\big)\leq\varepsilon.

where the law
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{p}}^{\vec{y},(-\infty)^{2T};2,T}
is defined in Definition
2.4
. Furthremore, there exists
\widetilde{M}=\widetilde{M}(\varepsilon)>0
and
\widetilde{N}_{0}(\varepsilon)>0
such that for all
N\geq\widetilde{N}_{0}
we have

(3.30)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{1}}^{0,(-\infty)^{T};1,T}\big(|L_{1}(1)|\geq\widetilde{M}\sqrt{T}\big)\leq\varepsilon.

As we shall see in the next section, the proof of the above lemma can be extended to include
L_{2}(1)
instead of
L_{2}(2)
. For technical reasons we work with
L_{2}(2)
here.

As mentioned in the introduction, the proof of Proposition
3.9
relies on several ingredients related to non-intersecting random walks. We postpone its proof to Section
4
. We now complete the proof of Theorem
3.8
assuming Proposition
3.9
.

Proof of Theorem
3.8
.

We prove the
v=2
case and then use it to show the
v=3
case.

Part I:
v=2
case.
For clarity we divide the proof into two steps.

Step 1.
Recall that the points in the line ensemble satisfy certain high probability ordering due to Theorem
3.1
. In particular, if we know the even points on
\mathcal{L}^{N}_{2}
are not too high, Theorem
3.1
will force that with high probability the odd points are not too high as well. Thus it suffices to control the even points on
\mathcal{L}^{N}_{2}
. In this step, we flesh out the details of the above idea. The proof of control on even points on
\mathcal{L}^{N}_{2}
appears in the second step of the proof.

We begin by defining a few events that will appear in the rest of the proof. Fix
k,\varepsilon>0
. For any
r\in\llbracket 1,kN^{2/3}\rrbracket\cap 2\mathbb{Z}
, define

\displaystyle\mathsf{A}(r,M):=\big\{\mathcal{L}^{N}_{2}(r)\geq MN^{1/3}\big\},\quad\mathsf{F}(r,M):=\{\mathcal{L}^{N}_{1}(r-1)\geq\tfrac{3M}{4}N^{1/3}\}.

Define

\displaystyle\mathsf{B}(r,M):=\mathsf{A}(r,M)\cap\hskip-17.07182pt\bigcap_{s\in\llbracket r+2,kN^{2/3}\rrbracket\cap 2\mathbb{Z}}\hskip-17.07182pt\neg\mathsf{A}(s,M),

so that
(\mathsf{B}(r,M))_{r\in\llbracket 1,kN^{2/3}\rrbracket}
forms a disjoint collections of events. Note that

\bigsqcup_{r\in\llbracket 1,kN^{2/3}\rrbracket\cap 2\mathbb{Z}}\hskip-17.07182pt\mathsf{B}(r,M)=\hskip-17.07182pt\bigcup_{r\in\llbracket 1,kN^{2/3}\rrbracket\cap 2\mathbb{Z}}\hskip-17.07182pt\mathsf{A}(r,M)=\bigg\{\sup_{r\in\llbracket 1,kN^{2/3}\rrbracket\cap 2\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{L}^{N}_{2}(r)\geq MN^{1/3}\bigg\}.

In the above equation, we use
\sqcup
instead of
\cup
to stress on the fact that it is an union of disjoint events. Thus the above union demands at least one of the even points in
\llbracket 1,kN^{2/3}\rrbracket
of
\mathcal{L}^{N}_{2}
to exceed
MN^{1/3}
. We next define

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}^{+}(M):=\hskip-17.07182pt\bigsqcup_{r\in\llbracket 1,kN^{2/3}\rrbracket\cap 2\mathbb{Z}}\hskip-17.07182pt\mathsf{B}(r,M)\cap\mathsf{F}(r,M),\quad\mathsf{G}^{-}(M):=\hskip-17.07182pt\bigsqcup_{r\in\llbracket 1,kN^{2/3}\rrbracket\cap 2\mathbb{Z}}\hskip-17.07182pt\mathsf{B}(r,M)\cap\neg\mathsf{F}(r,M).

Finally set
\mathsf{G}(M):=\mathsf{G}^{+}(M)\sqcup\mathsf{G}^{-}(M).
Observe that the event

\displaystyle\neg\mathsf{G}(M)=\left\{\sup_{s\in\llbracket 1,kN^{2/3}\rrbracket\cap 2\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{L}^{N}_{2}(s)<MN^{1/3}\right\}

controls the supremum of the second curve over the even points.
Take
0<k^{\prime}<k
. By the union bound we get that

(3.31)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{s\in\llbracket 1,k^{\prime}N^{2/3}\rrbracket}\mathcal{L}^{N}_{2}(s)\geq 3MN^{\frac{1}{3}}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{G}(2M))+\mathbb{P}\bigg(\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\llbracket 1,k^{\prime}N^{2/3}\rrbracket\\
s\in(2\mathbb{Z}+1)\end{subarray}}\mathcal{L}^{N}_{2}(s)\geq 3MN^{\frac{1}{3}},\neg\mathsf{G}(2M)\bigg).

Note that on
-\mathsf{G}(2M)
the supremum of
\mathcal{L}^{N}_{2}(s)
over all
s\in\llbracket 1,kN^{2/3}\rrbracket
is at most
2MN^{1/3}
. Then by the ordering of the line ensemble (Theorem
3.1
) on
\neg\mathsf{G}(2M)
it is exponentially unlikely that any odd point within
\llbracket 1,k^{\prime}N^{2/3}\rrbracket
will exceed
2MN^{1/3}+(\log N)^{7/6}
. In particular the second term in r.h.s. (
3.31
) can be made smaller than
\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
by choosing
N
large enough and taking
M\geq 1
. For the first term we claim that there exists
M_{0},N_{0}
depending on
k,\varepsilon
such that for all
N\geq N_{0}
and
M\geq M_{0}
we have

(3.32)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{G}(2M))\leq\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}.

Clearly plugging this bound back in r.h.s. (
3.31
) proves (
3.27
) with
M\mapsto 3M
and
k^{\prime}\mapsto k
. For the remainder of the proof we focus on proving (
3.32
).

Step 2.
In this step we prove (
3.32
). Observe that from the definition of
\mathsf{G}^{-}(2M)
we have

\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{G}^{-}(2M))\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{L}^{N}_{1}(r-1)-\mathcal{L}^{N}_{2}(r)\geq-\tfrac{M}{2}N^{1/3}\mbox{ for some }r\in\llbracket 1,kN^{1/3}\rrbracket\cap 2\mathbb{Z}\Big).

However by Theorem
3.1
the right-hand side of the above equation can be made smaller that
\frac{\varepsilon}{4}
for all
N\geq N_{0}
and
M\geq 1
, by choosing
N_{0}:=N_{0}(k,\varepsilon)>0
appropriately. We next claim that

(3.33)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{G}^{+}(2M))\leq 2\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{A}(2,M))\leq\tfrac{\varepsilon}{4}.

As
\mathsf{G}(2M)=\mathsf{G}^{-}(2M)\cup\mathsf{G}^{+}(2M)
, in view of the above claim, (
3.32
) follows via a union bound.

Let us now prove (
3.33
). Observe that by definition of
G^{+}(2M)
we have

(3.34)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\mathsf{A}(2,M)\big)\geq\mathbb{P}\big(\mathsf{G}^{+}(2M)\cap\mathsf{A}(2,M)\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\in\llbracket 1,kN^{2/3}\rrbracket\cap 2\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{P}\big(\mathsf{B}(r,2M)\cap\mathsf{F}(r,2M)\cap\mathsf{A}(2,M)\big).

We focus on each of the terms in the above sum. Using the tower property we have

(3.35)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\mathsf{B}(r,2M)\cap\mathsf{F}(r,2M)\cap\mathsf{A}(2,M)\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{B}(r,2M)\cap\mathsf{F}(r,2M)}\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}(2,M)}\mid\sigma\big(\mathcal{L}^{N}_{3},\mathcal{L}^{N}_{1}\llbracket r-1,kN^{2/3}\rrbracket,\mathcal{L}^{N}_{2}\llbracket r,kN^{2/3}\rrbracket\big)\right)\right].

Using the Gibbs property (see Theorem
1.3
and Lemma
2.2

(a)
) we have almost surely that

(3.36)

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{B}(r,2M)\cap\mathsf{F}(r,2M)}\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}(2,M)}\mid\sigma\big(\mathcal{L}^{N}_{3},\mathcal{L}^{N}_{1}\llbracket r-1,kN^{2/3}\rrbracket,\mathcal{L}^{N}_{2}\llbracket r,kN^{2/3}\rrbracket\big)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{B}(r,2M)\cap\mathsf{F}(r,2M)}\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{p}}^{\vec{y},\vec{z};2,r/2}(L_{2}(2)>MN^{1/3})

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{B}(r,2M)\cap\mathsf{F}(r,2M)}\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{p}}^{\vec{w},(-\infty)^{r};2,r/2}(L_{2}(2)>MN^{1/3}),

where
\vec{y}=(\mathcal{L}^{N}_{1}(r-1),\mathcal{L}^{N}_{2}(r))
,
\vec{z}=(\mathcal{L}^{N}_{3}(2v))_{v=1}^{r/2}
and
\vec{w}:=(\frac{3M}{2}N^{1/3},\frac{3M}{2}N^{1/3}-A\sqrt{r/2})
(
A\geq 1
is defined in (
3.28
)). The last inequality above follows by stochastic monotonicity (Proposition
2.6
). We now briefly explain how stochastic monotonicity works here. Note that the event
\{L_{2}(2)>MN^{1/3}\}
is decreasing thus by stochastic monotonicity to achieve a lower bound, we can reduce the boundary
\vec{z}
to
(-\infty)^{r}
. Furthermore, on
\mathsf{B}(r,2M)\cap\mathsf{F}(r,2M)
, we may reduce
\vec{y}
to
\vec{w}
as
y_{i}\geq w_{i}
on
\mathsf{B}(r,2M)\cap\mathsf{F}(r,2M)
.

Note that
MN^{1/3}\geq Mk^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{r/2}
. By translation invariance (Lemma
2.1

(a)
) and Proposition
3.9
, we may choose
M_{0}(k,\varepsilon)
large enough so that for all
M\geq M_{0}
and
r\in\llbracket 1,kN^{2/3}\rrbracket\cap 2\mathbb{Z}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{p}}^{\vec{w},(-\infty)^{r};2,r/2}(L_{2}(2)>MN^{1/3})=\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{p}}^{(0,-A\sqrt{r/2}),(-\infty)^{r};2,r/2}\big(L_{2}(2)>-\tfrac{1}{2}MN^{1/3}\big)\geq\tfrac{1}{2}.

Inserting the above bound in (
3.36
) and then going back to (
3.35
) we get

\displaystyle\mbox{r.h.s.~\eqref{e:gmp1}}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{P}\big(\mathsf{B}(r,2M)\cap\mathsf{F}(r,2M)\big).

Recall that
\mathsf{B}(r,2M)\cap\mathsf{F}(r,2M)
are all disjoint events whose union over
r\in\llbracket 1,kN^{2/3}\rrbracket\cap 2\mathbb{Z}
is given by
\mathsf{G}^{+}(2M)
. Summing the above inequality over
r\in\llbracket 1,kN^{2/3}\rrbracket\cap 2\mathbb{Z}
, in view of (
3.34
), we thus arrive at
\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{A}(2,M))\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{G}^{+}(2M))
. This proves the first inequality in (
3.33
). For the second one observe that by union bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{A}(2,M))\leq\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{L}^{N}_{1}(3)-\mathcal{L}^{N}_{2}(2)\leq-N^{1/3}\big)+\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{L}^{N}_{1}(3)\geq(M-1)N^{1/3}\big).

By Theorem
3.1
the first term on the right-hand side of the above equation can be made arbitrarily small by choosing
N
large enough. As for the second term, recall the point-to-line partition function
Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}(\cdot)
from (
1.8
). From Theorem
1.5
we know
N^{-1/3}[\log Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}(1)+2\Psi(\theta)N]
is tight. Since
\mathcal{L}^{N}_{1}(3)\leq\log Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}(1)+2\Psi(\theta)N
(see (
1.7
)), one can make the second term arbitrarily small enough by choosing
M,N
large enough. This completes the proof of (
3.33
).

Part II:
v=3
case.
Fix
k>0
. Let us define

\mathsf{E}:=\bigg\{\sup_{s\in\llbracket 1,kN^{2/3}\rrbracket}\mathcal{L}^{N}_{3}(s)\geq MN^{1/3}\bigg\},\quad\mathsf{F}:=\bigg\{\sup_{s\in\llbracket 1,kN^{2/3}\rrbracket}\mathcal{L}^{N}_{2}(s)\geq\tfrac{1}{2}MN^{1/3}\bigg\}.

By repeated application of the union bound we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{E})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{F})+\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{E}\cap\neg\mathsf{F})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{F})+\mathbb{P}\bigg(\mathcal{L}^{N}_{2}(s)-\mathcal{L}^{N}_{3}(s)\leq-\tfrac{1}{2}MN^{1/3},\mbox{ for some }s\in\llbracket 1,kN^{2/3}\rrbracket\bigg)

(3.37)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{F})+\sum_{\llbracket 1,kN^{2/3}\rrbracket}\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{L}^{N}_{2}(s)-\mathcal{L}^{N}_{3}(s)\leq-\tfrac{1}{2}MN^{1/3}\big).

By Theorem
3.1
, there exists an absolute constant
N_{0}
such that for all
s\geq 1
, and
M\geq 1
, we have
\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{N}_{2}(s)-\mathcal{L}^{N}_{3}(s)<-\tfrac{1}{2}MN^{1/3}\right)\leq 2^{-N}
. Since we have established
v=2
case of Theorem
3.8
, we may directed use (
3.27
) with
v\mapsto 2
,
M\mapsto\frac{1}{2}M
and
\varepsilon\mapsto\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon
, to get that
\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{F})\leq\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon
for all large enough
N,M
. Thus for all
N,M
large enough we have
\eqref{lastwo}\leq\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon+kN^{2/3}2^{-N}\leq\varepsilon
.
∎

Theorem
3.8
and Proposition
3.9
can be used to deduce left boundary tightness for the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
line ensemble. We shall refer to this property as
endpoint tightness
.

Theorem 3.10

(Endpoint Tightness)
.

Fix any
p\in\{1,2\}
. Set
\alpha:=\alpha_{p}
according to (
3.11
). Recall the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
line ensemble from Definition
2.7
with parameters
(\alpha,\theta)
. The sequences
\{{N^{-1/3}}\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(1)\}_{N\geq 1}
and
\{{N^{-1/3}}\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(2)\}_{N\geq 1}
are tight.

Again the proof can be extended to include tightness of
N^{-1/3}\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(1)
as well, once we have the corresponding version in Proposition
3.9
. We again refrain from doing so, as it is inconsequential to the proofs of our main theorem. We refer to the discussion in the introduction (Remark
1.6
) about how Theorem
3.10
relates to the work of
[
IMS22
]
.

Proof of Theorem
3.10
.

Fix an
\varepsilon>0
. We shall show that for all large enough
N,M
we have

(3.38)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(1)\leq MN^{1/3})\geq 1-3\varepsilon,\quad\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(2)\leq-MN^{1/3})\leq 3\varepsilon.

In view of the ordering of points in the line ensemble (Theorem
3.1
), we know
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(1)\geq\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(2)-(\log N)^{7/6}
with probability at least
1-2^{-N}
. This along with the above equation ensures endpoint tightness. We thus focus on proving (
3.38
).

Proof of the first inequality in (
3.38
).
Recall the point-to-line partition function
Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}(\cdot)
from (
1.8
). From Theorem
1.5
, we know
N^{-1/3}\big(\log Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}(1)+2\Psi(\theta)N\big)
is tight. Since
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(3)\leq\log Z_{N}^{\operatorname{line}}(1)+2\Psi(\theta)N
, there exists
M_{1}(\varepsilon)>0
such that for all
N\geq 3
we have
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(3)\leq M_{1}N^{1/3})\geq 1-\varepsilon.
Thanks to Theorem
3.1
, there exists
M_{2}(\varepsilon)>M_{1}(\varepsilon)
such that for all
N\geq 3

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{A})\geq 1-2\varepsilon,\quad\mathsf{A}:=\bigg\{\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(3)\leq M_{1}N^{1/3},\sup_{j\in\llbracket 1,4\rrbracket}\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(j)\leq M_{2}N^{1/3}\bigg\}.

Define
\mathcal{F}:=\sigma\big((\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(j))_{j\geq 3},(\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}\llbracket 1,2N-2i+2\rrbracket)_{i\geq 2}\big)
. By the union bound and tower property of the conditional expectation, for any
M_{3}>0
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(1)\geq M_{2}N^{1/3}+M_{3})\leq 2\varepsilon+\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(1)\geq M_{2}N^{1/3}+M_{3}}\mid\mathcal{F}\big]\right]

Using Theorem
1.3
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(1)\geq M_{2}N^{1/3}+M_{3}}\mid\mathcal{F}\big]=\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{p}}^{\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(3),(\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(2),\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(4));1,2}(L_{1}(1)\geq M_{2}N^{1/3}+M_{3})

On event
\mathsf{A}
, the boundary data are at most
M_{2}N^{1/3}
. By stochastic monotonicity (Proposition
2.6
) and translation invariance of the Gibbs measure (Lemma
2.1

(a)
) we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}\cdot\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{p}}^{\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(3),(\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(2),\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(4));1,2}(L_{1}(1)\geq M_{2}N^{1/3}+M_{3})\leq\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}\cdot\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{p}}^{0,(0,0);1,2}(L_{1}(1)\geq M_{3}).

The last probability can be made less than
\varepsilon
by taking
M_{3}
large enough. Thus setting
M_{4}=M_{4}(\varepsilon):=M_{3}+M_{2}
, we see that for all
N\geq 3
, the first inequality in (
3.38
) holds with
M=M_{4}
.

Proof of the second inequality in (
3.38
).
We start by defining two high probability events
\mathsf{B}_{1}
and
\mathsf{B}_{2}
. The idea is to then show
\mathbb{P}\big(\{\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(2)\leq-MN^{1/3}\}\cap\mathsf{B}_{1}\cap\mathsf{B}_{2}\big)
can be made arbitrarily small by choosing
N,M
large enough.

We shall use Theorem
3.3
(high point on the second curve) with
k\mapsto 1
. Consider
R_{0}=R_{0}(1,\varepsilon)>0
from Theorem
3.3
. Set
R=\max\{R_{0},1\}
. By Theorem
3.3
with
k\mapsto 1
, there exists
M_{5}(\varepsilon)>0
such that for all large enough
N

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{B}_{1})\geq 1-\varepsilon,\quad\mathsf{B}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{q\in\llbracket N^{2/3},RN^{2/3}\rrbracket}\mathsf{B}_{1}(p),\quad\mathsf{B}_{1}(q):=\big\{\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(2q)\geq-M_{5}N^{1/3}\big\}.

We write the set
\mathsf{B}_{1}
as a union of disjoint sets as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{1}(q):=\mathsf{B}_{1}(q)\cap\bigcap_{s\in\llbracket q+1,RN^{2/3}\rrbracket}\neg\mathsf{B}_{1}(s),\quad\mathsf{C}_{1}:=\bigsqcup_{q\in\llbracket N^{2/3},RN^{2/3}\rrbracket}\mathsf{C}_{1}(q)=\mathsf{B}_{1}.

By Theorem
3.1
, for large enough
N
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{B}_{2})\geq 1-\varepsilon,\quad\mathsf{B}_{2}

\displaystyle:=\bigcap_{q\in\llbracket N^{2/3},RN^{2/3}\rrbracket}\mathsf{B}_{2}(q),\quad\mathsf{B}_{2}(q):=\big\{\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(2q)-\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(2q-1)\leq N^{1/3}\big\}.

Set
\mathcal{F}_{q}:=\sigma\big((\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(j-1),\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(j))_{j\geq 2q},(\mathcal{L}_{i}^{N}\llbracket 1,2N-2i+2\rrbracket)_{i\geq 3}\big)
. Observe that
\mathsf{B}_{2}(q)\cap\mathsf{C}_{1}(q)
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{q}
. Note that for any
M_{6}>0
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg(\left\{\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(2)\leq-{M}_{6}N^{1/3}\right\}\cap\mathsf{B}_{1}\cap\mathsf{B}_{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{q\in\llbracket N^{2/3},RN^{2/3}\rrbracket}\mathbb{P}\bigg(\left\{\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(2)\leq-{M}_{6}N^{1/3}\right\}\cap\mathsf{C}_{1}(p)\cap\mathsf{B}_{2}(p)\bigg)

(3.39)

\displaystyle=\sum_{q\in\llbracket N^{2/3},RN^{2/3}\rrbracket}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{B}_{2}(q)\cap\mathsf{C}_{1}(q)}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(2)\leq-{M}_{6}N^{1/3}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{q}\right]\right].

By the Gibbs property (Theorem
1.3
) we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{B}_{2}(q)\cap\mathsf{C}_{1}(q)}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(2)\leq-{M}_{6}N^{1/3}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{q}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{B}_{2}(q)\cap\mathsf{C}_{1}(q)}\cdot\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{p}}^{(\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(2q-1),\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(2q)),(\mathcal{L}_{3}^{N}(2i))_{i=1}^{q};2,q}(L_{2}(2)\leq-{M}_{6}N^{\frac{1}{3}})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{B}_{2}(q)\cap\mathsf{C}_{1}(q)}\cdot\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{p}}^{(y_{1},y_{2}),(-\infty)^{q};2,q}(L_{2}(2)\leq-{M}_{6}N^{1/3}),

where
y_{1}=-(M_{5}+1)N^{1/3}
,
y_{2}=-M_{5}N^{1/3}
. The last inequality follows due to stochastic monotonicity (Proposition
2.6
) as on the event
\mathsf{B}_{2}(q)\cap\mathsf{C}_{1}(q)
we have
\mathcal{L}_{2}^{N}(2q)\geq-M_{5}N^{1/3}
and
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(2q-1)\geq-(M_{5}+1)N^{1/3}
. By translation invariance (Lemma
2.1

(a)
) and stochastic monotonicity (Proposition
2.6
) we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{p}}^{(y_{1},y_{2}),(-\infty)^{q};2,q}(L_{2}(2)\leq-{M}_{6}N^{1/3})\leq\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{p}}^{(0,-A\sqrt{q}),(-\infty)^{q};2,q}(L_{2}(2)\leq(M_{5}+1-{M}_{6})N^{1/3})\leq\varepsilon,

where the last inequality is uniform over
q\in\llbracket N^{2/3},RN^{2/3}\rrbracket
and follows from Proposition
3.9
by taking
M_{6}
large enough (
A\geq 1
is defined in (
3.28
)). Plugging the above bound back in (
3.39
), and noting that
(\mathsf{B}_{2}(q))_{q\in\llbracket N^{2/3},RN^{2/3}\rrbracket}
forms a disjoint collection of events we have that
\eqref{abd}\leq\varepsilon
. Using the fact that
\mathbb{P}(\neg\mathsf{B}_{i})\leq\varepsilon
for
i=1,2
, an application of the union bound yields the second inequality in (
3.38
) with
M={M}_{6}
.
∎

4.
Properties of the first two curves of Gibbs measures with no bottom curve

In this section, we prove Proposition
3.9
that asserts endpoint tightness of bottom-free measures defined in Definition
2.4
. Along with Proposition
3.9
, we also study probabilities of a certain event which we call
region pass event
under the bottom-free measure.

Proposition 4.1
.

Fix any
r,M>0
and
p\in\{1,2\}
. Set
T=\lfloor rN^{2/3}\rfloor
. We set
\alpha=\alpha_{p}
according to (
3.11
). Recall the bottom-free measure from Definition
2.4
. Let
\vec{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
with
y_{i}=-(M+i-1)N^{1/3}
. There exists
\phi=\phi(r,M)>0
and
N_{0}(r,M)>0
such that for all
N\geq N_{0}
we have

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{p}}^{\vec{y},(-\infty)^{2T};p,2T}(\mathsf{RP}_{p,M})\geq\phi,

where the region pass event is defined as

(4.2)

\displaystyle\mathsf{RP}_{p,M}:=\left\{\inf_{j\in\llbracket 1,2T+p-2\rrbracket}L_{p}(j)\geq 2MN^{1/3}\right\}.

2MN^{1/3}
-MN^{1/3}
-(M+1)N^{1/3}
2T
4T

Figure 13
.
The above figure depicts the event
\mathsf{RP}_{2,M}
under the law
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{2}}^{\vec{y},(-\infty)^{2T};2,2T}
.

The event
\mathsf{RP}_{p,M}
requires the first
2T+p-2
points of the
p
-th curve to lie above
2MN^{1/3}
. Although this is a low probability event, Proposition
4.1
says that this event always has positive probability (independent of
N
) under the bottom-free measure. (see Figure
13
for
p=2
case).

Recall from (
3.11
) that
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
are the boundary parameters corresponding to critical and supercritical phases respectively. Depending on the phase being critical or supercritical, the arguments for proving Proposition
3.9
and Proposition
4.1
are markedly different. We first give interpretation of the bottom-free laws under the two phases in Section
4.1
. In Section
4.2
and
4.3
, we provide proofs of the aforementioned lemmas for critical and supercritical phases respectively.

In the critical phase, the left boundary attraction between the first two curves is weak as
\alpha_{1}=O(N^{-1/3})
– it is the soft-intersection that only comes into the effect. The analysis of the Gibbs measures in this case is similar to the one done in studying full-space line ensembles and relies on KMT coupling type results. In the supercritical phase, we have
\alpha_{2}=O(1)
and the soft-intersection and attraction acts as two opposite forces: one tries to repel the curves and another tries to attract. This situation has asymptotically zero probability. The KMT coupling is no longer suitable to analyze events under this setting. This makes the argument for the supercritical phase more involved.

Let us first introduce a piece of notation that we will use frequently for the remainder of the paper. Consider any probability measure
\mathbb{P}^{\bullet}
on
\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{K}_{k,T}|}
equipped with Borel
\sigma
-algebra on
\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{K}_{k,T}|}
where
\mathcal{K}_{k,T}
is defined in (
2.4
). For
\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{K}_{k,T}|}
, we denote the coordinate functions as
L_{i}(j)(\omega):=\omega_{i}(j)
for
(i,j)\in\mathcal{K}_{k,T}
. We will simply write
(L_{i}(j))_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{K}_{k,T}}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\bullet}
for the random variables
(L_{i}(j))_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{K}_{k,T}}
under the measure
\mathbb{P}^{\bullet}
.

4.1.
The bottom-free laws under critical and supercritical phase

In this section, we provide two alternative (and ultimately equivalent) representations of the bottom-free laws defined in Definition
2.4
. The first representation, provided in Lemma
4.3
, is most suitable for studying the critical phase while the second representation, provided in Lemma
4.4
(see also Definition
1.7
and the discussion in the introduction), is most suitable for studying the supercritical phase.

We begin with the following lemma where we mention how the bottom-free measure on the domain
\mathcal{K}_{k,T}
(see (
2.4
)) with boundary condition
\vec{y}
is well-defined under certain cases.

Lemma 4.2

(Well-definedness of bottom-free measures)
.

Take
\vec{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. For
k\geq 1
and
\alpha\in(-\theta,\theta)
,
f_{k,T}^{\vec{y},(-\infty)^{T}}(\mathbf{u})
(see (
2.5
)) is proportional to

(4.3)

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\prod_{j=1}^{T-\mathbf{1}_{i=1}}W(u_{i+1,2j};u_{i,2j+1},u_{i,2j-1})\prod_{i=1}^{k}\prod_{j=1}^{2T-1-\mathbf{1}_{i=1}}G_{\theta+(-1)^{i+j-1}\alpha,(-1)^{j+1}}(u_{i,j}-u_{i,j+1}).

where
W
and
G
are defined in (
2.1
) and (
2.2
). Furthermore,
f_{2,T}^{\vec{y},(-\infty)^{T}}(\mathbf{u})
is proportional to

(4.4)

\displaystyle\exp\left(-e^{u_{2,2}-u_{1,3}}\right)G_{\alpha,1}(u_{2,2}-u_{1,1})G_{\theta,1}(u_{1,1}-u_{1,2})G_{\alpha+\theta,1}(u_{2,1}-u_{2,2})

\displaystyle\prod_{j=2}^{T-1}W(u_{2,2j};u_{1,2j+1},u_{1,2j-1})\prod_{i=1}^{2}\prod_{j=2}^{2T-1-\mathbf{1}_{i=1}}G_{\theta,(-1)^{j+1}}(u_{i,j}-u_{i,j+1}).

Moreover, the above two densities are integrable.

Proof.

Recall the definition of the
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs measure from (
2.5
) and the corresponding graphical representation from Figure
3
. Note that the red colored edges comes with a weight of the form
\exp(-\alpha(u_{2i-1,1}-u_{2i,1}))
which can be written as a product
e^{-\alpha u_{2i-1,1}}\cdot e^{\alpha u_{2i,1}}
. Thus we will think of each red edge as two red rings on the endpoints of the edges that comes with the (vertex) weight
e^{(-1)^{i}\alpha u_{i,1}}
(see Figure
14
(C)). Upon doing this vertex weight identification, the case
k=1
and
T=3
corresponds to the top graph of Figure
14
(C). One can check that the weights corresponding two graphs in Figure
14
(C) are equal. The red vertex weight on the right boundary of Figure
14
(C) can be absorbed in the constant of proportionality of the Gibbs measure. Thus ignoring this weight, the remaining weight is precisely given by the expression in (
4.3
). The general
k
odd case follows by redistributing the weights according to Figure
14
(A). For the case when
k
is even and
\alpha>0
, we redistribute according to Figure
14
(B). This leads to the
k=2
density given in (
4.4
). One can compute also the explicit density for the general even case from Figure
14
(B). Since
0\leq W\leq 1
and
G
s are densities, it is clear that the expressions in (
4.3
) and (
4.4
) are integrable.
∎

,

This redistribution described in Figure

will allows us to view the bottom free laws as laws which are absolutely continuous with respect to random walks. We give two such representations which will be useful in our critical and supercritical phase analysis. Towards this end, we introduce

resp.).

Lemma 4.3

(Critical phase representation)
.

Consider an independent collection of random variables
Y_{i}(j)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}G_{\theta+\alpha_{1},1}
and
U_{i}(j)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\operatorname{Beta}(\theta-\alpha_{1},2\alpha_{1})
for
i=1,2
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. Define

(4.6)

\displaystyle V_{i}(j):=Y_{i}(2j)+\log U_{i}(2j)-\mathbb{E}[\log U_{i}(2j)]-Y_{i}(2j-1).

so that
V_{i}(j)
form an i.i.d. collection of mean zero random variables. Set
L_{i}(2T+i-2)=y_{i}
and
\mbox{for }k\in\llbracket 1,T-1\rrbracket
define

(4.7)

\displaystyle L_{i}(2T+i-2k-2):=\left(y_{i}+(-1)^{i+1}(k-1)\mathbb{E}[\log U_{1}(1)]\right)+(-1)^{i+1}\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}V_{i}(j),

and set

(4.8)

\displaystyle W_{\operatorname{cr}}:=\exp\left(-\sum_{k=1}^{T-1}\left(e^{L_{2}(2k)-L_{1}(2k-1)}+e^{L_{2}(2k)-L_{1}(2k+1)}\right)\right),

where ‘cr’ stands for critical. Conditioned on
(L_{i}(2j+i-2))_{i\in\{1,2\},j\in\llbracket 1,T\rrbracket}
, we set

L_{i}(2k+i-1)\sim\xi_{\theta-\alpha_{1},\theta+\alpha_{1},(-1)^{i+1}}^{(L_{i}(2k+i-2),L_{i}(2k+i))}\mbox{ for }i\in\{1,2\},k\in\llbracket 1,T-1\rrbracket

and
L_{2}(1)=X+L_{2}(2)
where
X\sim G_{\theta+\alpha_{1},1}
. We have

(a)

(L_{1}(j))_{(1,j)\in\mathcal{K}_{1,T}}
is distributed as
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{1}}^{y_{1},(-\infty)^{T};1,T}
.

(b)

Let
\overline{\mathbb{P}}^{(y_{1},y_{2})}
denotes the joint law of
\{(L_{i}(j))_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{K}_{2,T}}\}
. This law has graphical representation given by the middle figure in Figure
15
(C). The law
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{1}}^{(y_{1},y_{2}),(-\infty)^{T};2,T}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\overline{\mathbb{P}}^{(y_{1},y_{2})}
with

\displaystyle\frac{d\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{1}}^{(y_{1},y_{2}),(-\infty)^{S};2,T}}{d\overline{\mathbb{P}}^{(y_{1},y_{2})}}\propto W_{\operatorname{cr}}.

Proof.

Let us consider
(L_{1}(j))_{(1,j)\in\mathcal{K}_{1,T}}\sim\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{1}}^{y_{1},(-\infty)^{T};1,T}
. See Figure
15
(A) for the graphical representation of the law. We focus on the odd points (shaded inside the gray box in the figure). Note that
(L_{1}(2T-1-2k))_{k=0}^{T-1}
is a random walk starting at
L_{1}(2T-1)=y_{1}
with increments distributed as
G_{\theta+\alpha_{1},1}\ast G_{\theta-\alpha_{1},-1}
. Conditioned on the odd points, we have
L_{1}(2k)\sim\xi_{\theta-\alpha_{1},\theta+\alpha_{1};1}^{(L(2k-1),L(2k+1))}
. Since
V_{1,j}+\mathbb{E}[\log U_{1,1}]\sim G_{\theta+\alpha,1}\ast G_{\theta-\alpha,-1}
, Part (a) of the lemma follows.

Let us now consider the
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{1}}^{(y_{1},y_{2}),(-\infty)^{T};2,T}
law whose graphical representation is given in Figure
15
(C). We view the graph as superimposition of two graphs where in one graph we collect all the non-black edges and the other graph we include only the black edges (see Figure
15
(C)). We denote the law of the Gibbs measure formed by deleting the black edges as
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{(y_{1},y_{2})}
(middle figure in Figure
15
(C)). The law
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{1}}^{(y_{1},y_{2}),(-\infty)^{T};2,T}
can be recovered from
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{(y_{1},y_{2})}
by viewing the black edges as a Radon-Nikodym derivative. Note that
W_{\operatorname{cr}}
, defined in (
4.8
), precisely contains all the effect of the black edges in the Gibbs measure.

If
(L_{i}(j))_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{K}_{2,T}}\sim\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{(y_{1},y_{2})}
, we have
L_{1}(\cdot)
independent of
L_{2}(\cdot)
and
L_{1}
is distributed as
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{1}}^{y_{1},(-\infty)^{T};1,T}
.
L_{2}
has a similar representation with even points
(L_{2}(2T-2k))_{k=0}^{T-1}
forming a random walk starting at
y_{2}
with increments distributed as
G_{\theta+\alpha,-1}\ast G_{\theta-\alpha,1}
. Conditioned on the even points, we have
L_{2}(2k+1)\sim\xi_{\theta-\alpha_{1},\theta+\alpha_{1};-1}^{(L(2k),L(2k+2))}
and
L_{2}(1)\sim G_{\theta+\alpha_{1},1}+L_{2}(2)
. Since
-V_{2,j}-\mathbb{E}[\log U_{1,1}]\sim G_{\theta+\alpha,-1}\ast G_{\theta-\alpha,1}
, we see that
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{(y_{1},y_{2})}
is equal to the law
\overline{\mathbb{P}}^{(y_{1},y_{2})}
defined in Part (b) of the lemma. This completes the proof of Part (b).
∎

In the supercritical phase, the weighted paired random walk (
\mathsf{WPRW}
) measure (recall this and the
\mathsf{PRW}
measure from Definition
1.7
) provides a useful way to describe the measure
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{2}}^{\vec{y},(-\infty)^{T};2,T}
.

Lemma 4.4

(Supercritical phase representation)
.

Fix any
\vec{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
T\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
. Suppose
(L_{1}(2j-1),L_{2}(2j))_{j\in\llbracket 1,T-1\rrbracket}\sim\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{WPRW}}^{T;\vec{y}}
. Conditioned on
(L_{1}(2i-1),L_{2}(2i))_{i\in\llbracket 1,T-1\rrbracket}
, set
L_{2}(1)\sim X+L_{2}(2)
where
X\sim G_{\alpha_{2}+\theta,1}
and

L_{1}(2k)\sim\xi_{\theta,\theta;1}^{(L_{1}(2k-1),L_{1}(2k+1))},\ \ L_{2}(2k+1)\sim\xi_{\theta,\theta;-1}^{(L_{2}(2k),L_{2}(2k+2))}\mbox{ for }k\in\llbracket 1,T-1\rrbracket.

Then
(L_{i}(j))_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{K}_{2,T}}
is distributed as
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{2}}^{\vec{y},(-\infty)^{T};2,T}
.

Proof.

We use the alternative graph representation of
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{2}}^{\vec{y},(-\infty)^{T};2,T}
law from Figure
14
(B). We decompose this graph into two graphs: one without the black edges (middle figure of Figure
16
), and one with the black edges, (right figure of Figure
16
). However, unlike the critical phase, the Gibbs measure corresponding to the middle figure does not split into two independent parts because of the
gray
(dashed) edge. For this measure, the marginal law of the odd points of the first curve and even points of the second curve together form the paired random walk. Upon taking the black edges into consideration (which corresponds to the
W_{\operatorname{sc}}
weight), the odd points of the first curve and even points of the second curve jointly follow the
\mathsf{WPRW}
law.
∎

y_{1}
y_{2}
y_{1}
y_{2}
y_{1}
y_{2}
\cdot
