Figure 16
.
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{2}}^{(y_{1},y_{2}),(-\infty)^{3};2,3}
law is decomposed into two parts. We use the representation from Figure
14
(B) here. The first part (middle figure) shaded region corresponds to a paired random walk. The second part (right figure) corresponds to
W_{\operatorname{sc}}
.

4.2.
Proof of Proposition
3.9
and Proposition
4.1
in the critical phase

We continue with the notations from Lemma
4.3
. By the KMT coupling for random walks
[
KMT75
]
, we may find independent Brownian motions
B_{1},B_{2}
defined on the same probability space such that the following holds. There exists a constant
\mathrm{C}>0
depending only on
\theta
and
\mu
such that for all
T
large enough,

(4.9)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left(\max_{k\leq T-2}\big|\sum_{j=1}^{k}V_{i}(j)-\sigma B_{i}(k)\big|\geq\mathrm{C}\log T\right)\leq 1/T.

V_{1}(1)
defined in (
4.6
) and
\sigma^{2}:=\operatorname{Var}(V_{1}(1))
. Recall that in the critical phase we have
\alpha_{1}=N^{-1/3}\mu
. Set
\kappa:=\tfrac{1}{4}|\mu|\Psi^{\prime}(\tfrac{1}{2}\theta)\geq 0
. As
\Psi^{\prime}
is a decreasing nonnegative function on
[0,\infty)
, for large enough
N

(4.10)

\displaystyle\big|\mathbb{E}[\log U_{1}(1)]\big|=\big|\Psi(\theta-\alpha_{1})-\Psi(\theta+\alpha_{1})\big|\leq 2|\alpha_{1}|\Psi^{\prime}(\tfrac{1}{2}\theta)=\tfrac{1}{2}\kappa N^{-1/3}.

Propositions
3.9
and
4.1
can now be proven using the above coupling and estimate for
|\mathbb{E}[\log U_{1}(1)]|
.

Proof of Proposition
3.9
in the case
p=1
(critical phase).

Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Set

\beta_{1}:=\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{x\in[0,T]}B_{1}(x)\leq\tfrac{\sqrt{T}}{8}\Big)=\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{x\in[0,1]}B_{1}(x)\leq\tfrac{1}{8}\Big)>0,\quad\beta_{2}:=\inf_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\exp\big(-2(n-1)e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{n}}\big)>0.

Set
T:=\lfloor rN^{2/3}\rfloor
. Continuing with the notations from Lemma
4.3
, let us assume

(L_{i}(j))_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{K}_{2,T}}\sim\overline{\mathbb{P}}^{(0,-A\sqrt{T})}.

Observe that
|(T-1)\mathbb{E}[\log U_{1}(1)]|\leq\sqrt{r}\kappa\sqrt{T}
. Following the relation in (
4.7
) and the estimate in (
4.9
) we get that

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{P}}^{(0,-A\sqrt{T})}\bigg(L_{1}(2k-1)

\displaystyle\geq-\tfrac{1}{8}\sqrt{T}-\sqrt{r}\kappa\sqrt{T}-\mathrm{C}\log T\mbox{ for all }k\in\llbracket 1,T\rrbracket,\mbox{ and}

\displaystyle L_{2}(2k)

\displaystyle\leq-A\sqrt{T}+\tfrac{1}{8}\sqrt{T}+\sqrt{r}\kappa\sqrt{T}+\mathrm{C}\log T\mbox{ for all }k\in\llbracket 1,T\rrbracket\bigg)\geq\beta_{1}^{2}-\tfrac{2}{T}.

Recall that
A=1+2\sqrt{r}\kappa
from (
3.28
). Thus for large enough
T
we can ensure that

\big(-\tfrac{1}{8}\sqrt{T}-\sqrt{r}\kappa\sqrt{T}-\mathrm{C}\log T\big)-\big(-A\sqrt{T}+\tfrac{1}{8}\sqrt{T}+\sqrt{r}\kappa\sqrt{T}+\mathrm{C}\log T\big)\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\sqrt{T},

and
\beta_{1}^{2}-\frac{2}{T}\geq\frac{1}{2}\beta_{1}^{2}
. Thus for large enough
T
we have

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{P}}^{(0,-A\sqrt{T})}\Big(L_{1}(2k-1)\wedge L_{1}(2k+1)\geq L_{2}(2k)+\tfrac{1}{2}\sqrt{T},\mbox{ for all }k\in\llbracket 1,T-1\rrbracket\Big)\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\beta_{1}^{2}.

Following the definition of
W_{\operatorname{cr}}
from (
4.8
) we thus get

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{(0,-A\sqrt{T})}[W_{\operatorname{cr}}]\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\beta_{1}^{2}\cdot\exp\big(-2(T-1)e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{T}}\big)\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\beta_{1}^{2}\beta_{2}.

By Lemma
4.3
, this forces

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{1}}^{(0,-A\sqrt{T}),(-\infty)^{T};2,T}\big(|L_{2}(2)|\geq M\sqrt{T}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{(0,-A\sqrt{T})}\big[W_{\operatorname{cr}}\mathbf{1}_{|L_{2}(2)|\geq M\sqrt{T}}\big]}{\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{(0,-A\sqrt{T})}[W_{\operatorname{cr}}]}

\displaystyle\leq 2\beta_{1}^{-2}\beta_{2}^{-1}\cdot\overline{\mathbb{P}}^{(0,-A\sqrt{T})}\big(|L_{2}(2)|\geq M\sqrt{T}\big).

Under
\overline{\mathbb{P}}^{(0,-A\sqrt{T})}
,
L_{2}(2)
has variance
T\cdot\operatorname{Var}(V_{1}(1))
and mean
-A\sqrt{T}+(T-1)\mathbb{E}[\log U_{1}(1)]
. One can thus choose
M
large enough so that the last term in the above equation is at most
\varepsilon
. Similarly one can show
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{2}}^{(0,-A\sqrt{T}),(-\infty)^{T};2,T}\big(|L_{1}(1)|\geq M\sqrt{T}\big)\leq\varepsilon
for all large enough
M
. This proves (
3.29
) for
p=1
. For (
3.30
), observe that by Lemma
4.3
(a) and the Markov inequality one can take
\widetilde{M}
large enough so that have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{1}}^{0,(-\infty)^{T};1,T}\big(|L_{1}(1)|\geq\widetilde{M}\sqrt{T}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{\widetilde{M}^{2}T}\big(T\cdot\operatorname{Var}(V_{1}(1))+(|(T-1)\mathbb{E}[\log U_{1}(1)])^{2}\big)\leq\varepsilon.

∎

Proof of Proposition
4.1
in the case
p=1
(critical case).

We continue with the same notations as in Lemma
4.3
with
T\mapsto 2T
. Set
T:=\lfloor rN^{2/3}\rfloor
. Consider the collection of random variables
(L_{i}(j))_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{K}_{2,2T}}
defined in (
4.7
) with
y_{1}=-MN^{\frac{1}{3}}
. By Lemma
4.3
(a), we get that

(L_{1}(j))_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{K}_{1,2T}}\sim\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{1}}^{-MN^{1/3};(-\infty)^{2T};1,2T}.

We may assume
V_{1}(j)
are defined in a probability space that includes a Brownian motion
B_{1}(\cdot)
such that (
4.9
) holds. Recall that given a standard Brownian motion
B
and an open set
\mathcal{U}\subset C([0,1])
with
\{f:f(0)=0\}\subset\mathcal{U}
, we have
\mathbb{P}(B|_{[0,1]}\in\mathcal{U})>0
. Thus by the scale invariance of Brownian motion, there exists
\phi(\theta,\mu,r,M)>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(0\leq\sigma B_{1}(x)-(16M+5\kappa r)N^{1/3}\leq{MN^{1/3}}\mbox{ for all }x\in[\tfrac{T}{2},2T]\Big)\geq 2\phi.

Here
\kappa=\frac{1}{4}|\mu|\Psi^{\prime}(\frac{1}{2}\theta)\geq 0
. Now for
y=-MN^{\frac{1}{3}}
we have
|y+(k-1)\mathbb{E}[\log U_{1}(1)]|\leq(M+\kappa r)N^{\frac{1}{3}}
for all
k\leq 2T
. For large enough
N
we also have
\mathrm{C}\log 2T\leq MN^{1/3}
where
\mathrm{C}
comes from (
4.9
). Thus in view of (
4.7
) and (
4.9
) we have

(4.11)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{1}\bigg((14M+4\kappa r)N^{\frac{1}{3}}\leq L_{1}(4T-1-2k)

\displaystyle\leq(19M+6\kappa r)N^{\frac{1}{3}}\mbox{ for all }k\in\llbracket\tfrac{T}{2},2T-1\rrbracket\bigg)\geq 2\phi-\tfrac{1}{2T},

where for simplicity we write
\mathbb{P}_{1}:=\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{1}}^{-MN^{\frac{1}{3}},(-\infty)^{2T};1,2T}
. Let us set

\displaystyle\mathsf{A}

\displaystyle:=\left\{(14M+4\kappa r)N^{\frac{1}{3}}\leq L_{1}(4T-1-2k)\leq(19M+6\kappa r)N^{\frac{1}{3}}\mbox{ for all }k\in\llbracket\tfrac{T}{2},2T-1\rrbracket\right\},

\displaystyle\mathsf{B}(k)

\displaystyle:=\left\{|L_{1}(2k-1)-L_{1}(2k)|,|L_{1}(2k+1)-L_{1}(2k)|\geq 2(5M+2\kappa r)N^{\frac{1}{3}}\right\}.

Recall the event
\mathsf{RP}_{1,M}
from (
4.2
). Observe that

\displaystyle\mathsf{RP}_{1,M}\supset\mathsf{A}\cap\bigcap_{k\in\llbracket 1,3T/2-1\rrbracket}\mathsf{B}(k)

Thus by applying the union bound we get

(4.12)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{1}(\mathsf{RP}_{1,M})\geq\mathbb{P}_{1}\bigg(\mathsf{A}\cap\bigcap_{k\in\llbracket 1,3T/2-1\rrbracket}\mathsf{B}(k)\bigg)\geq\mathbb{P}_{1}(\mathsf{A})-\sum_{k\in\llbracket 1,3T/2-1\rrbracket}\mathbb{P}_{1}\bigg(\mathsf{A}\cap\neg\mathsf{B}(k)\bigg)

Let us denote
\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{odd}}:=\sigma\big((L_{1}(2k-1))_{k=1}^{2T}\big)
. Note that the event
\mathsf{A}
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{odd}}
. On the event
\mathsf{A}
,
|L_{1}(2k+1)-L_{1}(2k-1)|\leq(5M+2\kappa r)N^{\frac{1}{3}}
for all
k\in\llbracket 1,3T/2-1\rrbracket
. Recall that the distribution of even points of
L_{1}
conditioned on
\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{odd}}
are given by the
\xi
-distributions (see (
4.5
) and Lemma
4.3
). Applying the tail bound for the
\xi
-distribution from Lemma
B.4
we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}\mathbb{E}_{1}\big(\mathbf{1}_{\neg\mathsf{B}(k)}\mid\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{odd}}\big)\leq\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}\cdot\exp\left(-\mathrm{C}(5M+2\kappa r)N^{\frac{1}{3}}\right),

for all
k\in\llbracket 1,3T/2-1\rrbracket
. Taking another expectation with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{odd}}
above and then plugging the bound back in (
4.12
), along with the lower bound of
\mathbb{P}_{1}(\mathsf{A})
from (
4.11
) we get that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{1}(\mathsf{RP}_{1,M})\geq 2\phi-\tfrac{1}{2T}-3rN^{\frac{2}{3}}\exp\left(-\mathrm{C}(5M+2\kappa r)N^{\frac{1}{3}}\right).

For large enough
N
, the right side of above equation is always larger than
\phi
.
∎

4.3.
Proof of Propositions
3.9
and
4.1
in the supercritical phase

Recall that Lemma
4.4
establishes that the law of
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{2}}^{\vec{y},(-\infty)^{T};2,T}
is related to the law of weighted paired random walk (
\mathsf{WPRW}
) defined in Definition
1.7
. We will start by developing a few important properties of the paired random walk and weighted paired random walk measures before going into the proof of Propositions
3.9
and
4.1
in the
p=2
case (supercritical phase).

Lemma 4.5

(Properties of the increments)
.

The densities
\mathsf{f}_{\theta}
and
\mathsf{g}_{\zeta}
, defined in (
2.3
), enjoy the following properties.

(1)

The density
\mathsf{f}_{\theta}
is symmetric and
\log\mathsf{f}_{\theta}
is concave.

(2)

Let
\psi
denote the characteristic function corresponding to
\mathsf{f}_{\theta}
.
|\psi|
is integrable. Given any
\delta>0
, there exists
\eta
such that
\sup_{t\geq\delta}|\psi(t)|=\eta<1
.

(3)

For any
a<b
,
\inf_{x\in[a,b]}\mathsf{f}_{\theta}(x)>0
and
\inf_{x\in[a,b]}\mathsf{g}_{\zeta}(x)>0
.

(4)

There exists a constant
\mathrm{C}>0
such that
\mathsf{f}_{\theta}(x)\leq\mathrm{C}e^{-|x|/\mathrm{C}}
and
\mathsf{g}_{\zeta}(x)\leq\mathrm{C}e^{-|x|/\mathrm{C}}
. In particular, this implies that if
X\sim\mathsf{f}_{\theta}
and
Y\sim\mathsf{g}_{\zeta}
, there exists
v>0
such that and

\displaystyle\sup_{|t|\leq v}\big[\mathbb{E}[e^{tX}]+\mathbb{E}[e^{tY}]\big]<\infty.

In other words
X
and
Y
are subexponential random variables.

Proof.

Recall that
\mathsf{f}_{\theta}=G_{\theta,+1}\ast G_{\theta,-1}
. Thus the random variable corresponding to
\mathsf{f}_{\theta}
can be viewed as difference of two independent random variables drawn from
G_{\theta,+1}
. Hence symmetricity claim of part (1) follows. Concavity of
\log\mathsf{f}_{\theta}
can be checked by computing the second derivative explicitly. For the characteristic function from
[
DLMF
, Formula 5.8.3]
one has

(4.13)

\displaystyle\psi(t)=\left|\frac{\Gamma(\theta+it)}{\Gamma(\theta)}\right|^{2}=\prod_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(1+\frac{t^{2}}{(\theta+n)^{2}}\right)^{-1}\leq\left(1+\frac{t^{2}}{\theta^{2}}\right)^{-1}.

From here, one can verify part (2) of the above lemma. Part (3) and (4) follows from the explicit form of the
G
function.
∎

The following corollary allows us to use estimates developed in Appendix
C
regarding non-intersection probabilities for random walks and bridges with general jump distributions.

Corollary 4.6
.

The density
\mathsf{f}_{\theta}
defined in (
2.3
) satisfies Assumption
C.1
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from Lemma
4.5
.
∎

Recall the
\mathsf{PRW}
law from Definition
1.7
. Let
\overline{\mathsf{f}_{\theta}}^{(n)}
be the density of
\frac{X(1)+\cdots+X(n)}{\sqrt{n}}
where
X(i)
are i.i.d. drawn from
\mathsf{f}_{\theta}
. Assume
U(n),V(n)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\overline{\mathsf{f}_{\theta}}^{(n-1)}
. Note that for any Borel set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
we have

(4.14)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(x,y)}\big((S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1))\in A\big)=\frac{\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathsf{g}_{\zeta}(\sqrt{n}(V(n)-U(n)-\frac{x-y}{\sqrt{n}}))\mathbf{1}_{(U(n)+x,V(n)+y)\in{n}^{-1/2}A}\Big]}{\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{g}_{\zeta}(\sqrt{n}(V(n)-U(n)-\frac{x-y}{\sqrt{n}}))]}.

The above formula is the guiding principle for extracting tail estimates of various kinds of functions of
(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1))
. We list few of them that are indispensable for our later analysis.

Lemma 4.7

(Tail estimates for the entrance law)
.

Fix any
M>0
,
n\geq 1
, and consider
x_{n},y_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
with
|x_{n}|,|y_{n}|\leq M\sqrt{n}
. Fix two open intervals
I_{1},I_{2}>0
. Under the above setup, there exists a constant
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}(M,I_{1},I_{2})>1
such that for all
n\geq 1
and
\tau\geq 1
, we have

(4.15)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(x_{n},y_{n})}\big(|S_{1}(1)|\geq\tau\sqrt{n}\big)\leq\mathrm{C}e^{-\frac{1}{\mathrm{C}}\tau},

(4.16)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(x_{n},y_{n})}\big(|S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)|\geq\tau\big)\leq\mathrm{C}e^{-\frac{1}{\mathrm{C}}\tau},

(4.17)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(x_{n},y_{n})}\big(S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)\in I_{1},S_{1}(1)\in\sqrt{n}I_{2}\big)\geq\tfrac{1}{\mathrm{C}}.

Proof of Lemma
4.7
.

For simplicity let us write
z_{n}:=\frac{x_{n}-y_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}
. It is enough to prove the Lemma
4.7
for large enough
n
. So, throughout the proof we will assume
n
is large enough. We first claim that the denominator of the right-hand side of (
4.14
) is of the order
n^{-1/2}
, i.e. there exists a
\mathrm{C}>1
such that for all large enough
n
we have

(4.18)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathrm{C}\sqrt{n}}\leq\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{g}_{\zeta}(\sqrt{n}(V(n)-U(n)-z_{n}))]\leq\frac{\mathrm{C}}{\sqrt{n}}

Fix any
\tau\geq 0
. Using the exponential tails of
\mathsf{g}_{\zeta}
(part (4) from Lemma
4.5
) we have

(4.19)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathsf{g}_{\zeta}(\sqrt{n}(V(n)-U(n)-z_{n}))\mathbf{1}_{|\sqrt{n}(V(n)-U(n)-z_{n})|\geq\tau}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathsf{g}_{\zeta}(\sqrt{n}(V(n)-U(n)-z_{n}))\mathbf{1}_{|\sqrt{n}(V(n)-U(n)-z_{n})|\geq\tau+\sqrt{n}}\bigg]

\displaystyle+\sum_{p\in\llbracket\tau,\tau+\sqrt{n}\rrbracket}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathsf{g}_{\zeta}(\sqrt{n}(V(n)-U(n)-z_{n}))\mathbf{1}_{|\sqrt{n}(V(n)-U(n)-z_{n})|\in[p,p+1]}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{C}e^{-\frac{1}{\mathrm{C}}(\sqrt{n}+\tau)}+\sum_{p\in\llbracket\tau,\tau+\sqrt{n}\rrbracket}\mathrm{C}e^{-\frac{1}{\mathrm{C}}p}\cdot\mathbb{P}\big(\tfrac{p}{\sqrt{n}}\leq|V(n)-U(n)-z_{n}|\leq\tfrac{p+1}{\sqrt{n}}\big).

Note that
\overline{\mathsf{f}_{\theta}}^{(n)}(x)=\sqrt{n}\mathsf{f}_{\theta}^{\ast n}(x\sqrt{n})
where
\mathsf{f}_{\theta}^{\ast n}
denotes the
n
-fold convolution of
\mathsf{f}_{\theta}
. As
n\to\infty
, we know by central limit theorem that this should converge to a Gaussian density with appropriate variance. Lemma
C.2
(recall Corollary
4.6
) records a sharp quantitative version of this convergence. Indeed, the estimate from Lemma
C.2
(recall Corollary
4.6
) ensures that given any interval
B:=[\frac{p}{\sqrt{n}},\frac{p+1}{\sqrt{n}}]\subset[-2,2]
, for all large enough
n
, we have

\mathbb{P}\big((V(n)-U(n)-z_{n})\in B\big)=(1+o(1))\mathbb{P}\big(Z_{1}-Z_{2}-z_{n}\in B\big)

where
Z_{1},Z_{2}
are independent Gaussian random variables with same variance as of
\mathsf{f}_{\theta}
. By Gaussian computations we can ensure that for all large enough
n
we have

\mathbb{P}\big((V(n)-U(n)-z_{n})\in B\big)\in[R^{-1}/\sqrt{n},R/\sqrt{n}]

for some
R>1
depending only on
M
. This ensures
\mathbb{P}\big(\tfrac{p}{\sqrt{n}}\leq|V(n)-U(n)-z_{n}|\leq\tfrac{p+1}{\sqrt{n}}\big)\leq\frac{R}{\sqrt{n}}
for all
p\in\llbracket 0,\sqrt{n}\rrbracket
. Plugging this bound back in r.h.s. (
4.19
) leads to

(4.20)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathsf{g}_{\zeta}(\sqrt{n}(V(n)-U(n)-z_{n}))\mathbf{1}_{|\sqrt{n}(V(n)-U(n)-z_{n})|\geq\tau}\bigg]\leq\frac{\mathrm{C}}{\sqrt{n}}e^{-\tau/\mathrm{C}}.

Taking
\tau=0
leads to the upper bound in (
4.18
). For the lower bound we note

(4.21)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[g(\sqrt{n}(V(n)-U(n)-z_{n}))]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}\bigg[g(\sqrt{n}(V(n)-U(n)-z_{n}))\mathbf{1}_{V_{n}-U_{n}-x_{n}+y_{n}\in(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},\frac{2}{\sqrt{n}})}\bigg]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}\bigg(V(n)-U(n)-z_{n}\in(\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}},\tfrac{2}{\sqrt{n}})\bigg)\cdot\inf_{x\in[1,2]}\mathsf{g}_{\zeta}(x)

\displaystyle\geq\tfrac{R^{-1}}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\inf_{x\in[1,2]}\mathsf{g}_{\zeta}(x),

which is bounded below by
1/[\mathrm{C}\sqrt{n}]
, by the property of
\mathsf{g}_{\zeta}
from part (3) of Lemma
4.5
. This proves the lower bound in (
4.18
).

Let us now prove the inequalities in Lemma
4.7
one by one. Inserting the upper bound in (
4.20
) and lower bound in (
4.18
) in the formula (
4.14
) leads to (
4.16
). For (
4.17
) notice that due to (
4.14
) and (
4.21
) we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)\in I_{1},S_{1}(1)\in\sqrt{n}I_{2}\big)

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{C}_{1}^{-1}\sqrt{n}\cdot\inf_{x\in I_{1}}g(-x)\cdot\mathbb{P}\Big(U(n)+\tfrac{x_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\in\sqrt{n}I_{2},U(n)+z_{n}-V(n)\in{n}^{-1/2}I_{1}\Big).

Using Lemma
C.2
(recall Corollary
4.6
) again, the probability above can be shown lower bounded by
\frac{\mathrm{C}_{2}^{-1}}{\sqrt{n}}
for some
\mathrm{C}_{2}
depending on
M,I_{1},I_{2}
but free of
n
. This proves (
4.17
). For (
4.15
) we observe

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[g(\sqrt{n}(V(n)-U(n)-z_{n}))\mathbf{1}_{|U(n)|\geq\tau}\big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{C}e^{-\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\mathrm{C}}}+\sum_{p\in\llbracket 0,\sqrt{n}\rrbracket}\mathrm{C}e^{-\frac{p}{\mathrm{C}}}\mathbb{P}\Big(\tfrac{p}{\sqrt{n}}\leq|V(n)-U(n)-z_{n}|\leq\tfrac{p+1}{\sqrt{n}},|U(n)|\geq\tau\Big).

By a union bound followed by tower property of conditional expectation, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\tfrac{p}{\sqrt{n}}\leq|V(n)-U(n)-z_{n}|\leq\tfrac{p+1}{\sqrt{n}},|U(n)|\geq\tau\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\tau\leq|U(n)|\leq(\log n)^{3/2}}\mathbb{P}\left(\tfrac{p}{\sqrt{n}}\leq|V(n)-U(n)-z_{n}|\leq\tfrac{p+1}{\sqrt{n}}\mid U(n)\right)\right]+\mathbb{P}\big(|U(n)|\geq(\log n)^{3/2}\big).

By Lemma
C.2
(recall Corollary
4.6
), the conditional probability above can be uniformly bounded above by
\frac{\mathrm{C}_{3}}{\sqrt{n}}
for some
\mathrm{C}_{3}
independent of
p
and
n
. Exponential tail estimates of
U(n)
, which follows from sub-exponential property (part (4) of Lemma
4.5
) of
\mathsf{f}_{\theta}
(see Theorem 2.8.1 from
[
Ver18
]
), show that the right-hand side of the above equation is at most
\frac{\mathrm{C}}{\sqrt{n}}e^{-\frac{1}{\mathrm{C}}\tau}
. Combining these estimates yields

\mathbb{E}\left[g(\sqrt{n}(V(n)-U(n)-z_{n}))\mathbf{1}_{|U(n)|\geq\tau}\right]\leq\tfrac{\mathrm{C}}{\sqrt{n}}e^{-\frac{1}{\mathrm{C}}\tau}.

Using the lower bound for the denominator from (
4.21
), in view of (
4.14
), we get (
4.15
).
∎

In order to deal with the
\mathsf{WPRW}
law, the weighted version of the
\mathsf{PRW}
law (see Definition
1.7
), we next analyze the
W_{\operatorname{sc}}
weight defined in (
1.15
). We record a convenient lower bound for
W_{\operatorname{sc}}
that will be useful in our later analysis. Fix any
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
with
p+q\leq n-1
. Given any
\beta>0
, we consider several ‘Gap’ events:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Gap}_{1,\beta}

\displaystyle:=\{S_{1}(k)-S_{2}(k)\geq\beta k^{1/4}\mbox{ for all }k\in\llbracket 2,p\rrbracket\},

\displaystyle\mathsf{Gap}_{2,\beta}

\displaystyle:=\{S_{1}(k)-S_{2}(k)\geq\beta(n-k)^{1/4}\mbox{ for all }k\in\llbracket n-q,n-1\rrbracket\},

\displaystyle\mathsf{Gap}_{3,\beta}

\displaystyle:=\{S_{1}(k)-S_{2}(k)\geq n^{1/4}\mbox{ for all }k\in\llbracket p+1,n-q\rrbracket\},

\displaystyle\mathsf{Gap}_{4,\beta}

\displaystyle:=\{S_{1}(k-1)-S_{1}(k)\leq\beta^{-1}k^{1/8}\mbox{ for all }k\in\llbracket 2,p\rrbracket\},

\displaystyle\mathsf{Gap}_{5,\beta}

\displaystyle:=\{S_{1}(k-1)-S_{1}(k)\leq\beta^{-1}(n-k+1)^{1/8}\mbox{ for all }k\in\llbracket n-q+1,n\rrbracket\},

\displaystyle\mathsf{Gap}_{6,\beta}

\displaystyle:=\{|S_{1}(k)-S_{1}(k-1)|\leq\beta^{-1}(\log n)\mbox{ for all }k\in\llbracket p+1,n-q\rrbracket\}.

The events depend on
p
and
q
as well, but we have suppressed it from the notation.
\mathsf{Gap}_{1,\beta},\mathsf{Gap}_{2,\beta},
and
\mathsf{Gap}_{3,\beta}
requires
S_{1}(k)-S_{2}(k)
to be bigger than a threshold pointwise in the left (
\llbracket 2,p\rrbracket
), right (
\llbracket n-q,n-1\rrbracket
), and middle (
\llbracket p+1,n-q\rrbracket
) region respectively. The type of threshold depends on the region.
\mathsf{Gap}_{4,\beta},\mathsf{Gap}_{5,\beta},
and
\mathsf{Gap}_{6,\beta}
controls the increments of
S_{1}(k)
. Set

(4.22)

\displaystyle\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta}:=\bigcap_{i=1}^{6}\mathsf{Gap}_{i,\beta}.

We have the following deterministic inequality for
W_{\operatorname{sc}}
.

Lemma 4.8
.

Recall
W_{\operatorname{sc}}
from (
1.15
). Given any
\beta>0
, there exists
a_{\beta}>0
such that for all
n\geq 1
,

W_{\operatorname{sc}}\geq a_{\beta}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta}\cap\{|S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)|\leq\beta^{-1}\}}.

where
W_{\operatorname{sc}}
is defined in (
1.15
).

Proof.

Assume
\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta}
holds. For
k\in\llbracket 2,n-1\rrbracket
we have

\displaystyle S_{2}(k)-S_{1}(k)

\displaystyle\leq-\min(\beta k^{1/4},\beta(n-k)^{1/4},n^{1/4})=:\tau^{(n)}(k).

Clearly
\sum_{k=2}^{n-1}e^{S_{2}(k)-S_{1}(k)}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}e^{\tau^{(n)}(k)}
is uniformly bounded in
n
and hence can be bounded by some constant
{C}_{\beta}\in(0,\infty)
. Similarly for
k\in\llbracket 2,n-1\rrbracket
we have

\displaystyle S_{2}(k)-S_{1}(k+1)

\displaystyle=S_{2}(k)-S_{1}(k)+S_{1}(k)-S_{1}(k+1)

\displaystyle\leq\tau_{n}^{(1)}(k)+\beta^{-1}\max((k+1)^{1/8},(n-k)^{1/8},(\log n))=:\widetilde{\tau}^{(n)}(k).

Clearly
\sum_{k=2}^{n-1}e^{S_{2}(k)-S_{1}(k+1)}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}e^{\widetilde{\tau}^{(n)}(k)}
is uniformly bounded in
n
and hence can be bounded by some constant
\widetilde{C}_{\beta}\in(0,\infty)
. Finally,

S_{2}(1)-S_{1}(1)=S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(2)+S_{2}(2)-S_{1}(2)\leq 3\beta^{-1}

on the event
\{|S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)|\leq\beta^{-1}\}\cap\mathsf{Gap}_{4,\beta}
. Thus from the definition of
W_{\operatorname{sc}}
in (
1.15
) we have

\displaystyle W_{\operatorname{sc}}\geq\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta}\cap\{|S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)|\leq\beta^{-1}\}}\cdot\exp(-e^{3\beta^{-1}}-C_{\beta}-\widetilde{C}_{\beta}).

Taking
a_{\beta}:=\exp(-e^{3\beta^{-1}}-C_{\beta}-\widetilde{C}_{\beta})
completes the proof.
∎

Note that upon conditioning on the values of
S_{1}(1)
and
S_{2}(1)
, a paired random walk (recall Definition
3.6
) has the law of two independent
n
-step random bridges (recall Definition
3.6
) starting from
(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1))
and ending at
(x,y)
.

We now introduce modified random bridge above as they are easier to work with than random bridges. Indeed, as described in the proof idea section of the introduction, the main advantage of working with modified random bridges is that they have a (true) random walk portion and one can appeal to classical non-intersection probability estimates available for the random walks. On the other hand, the laws of random bridge and modified random bridge can be compared with the help of Lemma
4.10
below.
Figure
5
contains an illustration of such a bridge for
p=q=\lfloor n/4\rfloor
.

Definition 4.9

(
(n;p,q)
-modified random bridge)
.

Fix
n\geq 1
, and
p,q\in\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket
with
p+q\leq n
and
p\neq 0
. Take any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
(X(i),Y(i))_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\mathsf{f}_{\theta}
where
\mathsf{f}_{\theta}
is defined in (
2.3
). Set
S(1):=a
and
S(n):=b
. Set For
k\in\llbracket 2,p\rrbracket
, set

S(k):=a+\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}X_{j}\mbox{ for }k\in\llbracket 2,p\rrbracket,\quad S(n-k):=b-\sum_{j=1}^{k}Y_{j}\mbox{ for }k\in\llbracket 1,q\rrbracket.

Conditioned on
(S(k))_{k\in\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket\cup\llbracket n-q,n\rrbracket}
, set
(S(k))_{k=p}^{n-q}\sim\mathbb{P}^{n-p-q+1;\widetilde{a},\widetilde{b}}
where
\widetilde{a}:=S(p)
,
\widetilde{b}:=S(n-q)
, and
\mathbb{P}^{m;a,b}
is a
m
-step random walk from
a
to
b
. We call
(S(k))_{k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket}
a
(n;p,q)
-modified random bridge of length
n
starting at
a
and ending at
b
and denote its law as
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{(n;p,q);a,b}
. Again, we shall often consider two independent
(n;p,q)
-modified random bridges starting from
(a_{1},a_{2})
and ending at
(b_{1},b_{2})
. Such bridges can be viewed as a measure on
(\Omega^{2}_{n},\mathcal{F}^{2}_{n})
space introduced in Definition
1.7
. We write
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{(n;p,q);(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}
to denote its law.

Lemma 4.10

(Comparison Lemma)
.

Fix any
M,\widetilde{M}>0
and
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\in[0,1/2)
, and
n\geq 1
. Set
p=\lfloor n\delta_{1}\rfloor
and
q=\lfloor n\delta_{2}\rfloor
. For
\vec{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
, let
V_{a,b}(\vec{x})
and
\widetilde{V}_{a,b}(\vec{x})
be the joint density of a
n
-step random bridge and
(n;p,q)
-modified random bridge starting at
a
and ending at
b
. Suppose
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
with
|a-b|\leq M\sqrt{n}
. Then, there exists two constants
\mathrm{C}_{1}=\mathrm{C}_{1}(M,\delta_{1},\delta_{2})>0
and
\mathrm{C}_{2}=\mathrm{C}_{2}(M,\widetilde{M},\delta_{1},\delta_{2})>0
such that for all
\vec{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
and all
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
with
|a-b|\leq M\sqrt{n}
we have

(4.23)

\displaystyle V_{a,b}(\vec{x})\leq\mathrm{C}_{1}\!\cdot\!\widetilde{V}_{a,b}(\vec{x}),

(4.24)

\displaystyle V_{a,b}(\vec{x})\!\cdot\!\mathbf{1}_{|x_{p}-x_{n-q}|\leq\widetilde{M}\sqrt{n}}\geq\mathrm{C}_{2}^{-1}\!\cdot\!\widetilde{V}_{a,b}(\vec{x})\!\cdot\!\mathbf{1}_{|x_{p}-x_{n-q}|\leq\widetilde{M}\sqrt{n}}.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle V_{a,b}(\vec{x}):=\frac{\prod_{j=0}^{n-2}\mathsf{f}_{\theta}(x_{j+1}-x_{j})}{\mathsf{f}_{\theta}^{\ast(n-1)}(b-a)},\quad\widetilde{V}_{a,b}(\vec{x}):=\frac{\prod_{j=0}^{n-2}\mathsf{f}_{\theta}(x_{j+1}-x_{j})}{\mathsf{f}_{\theta}^{\ast(n-p-q)}(x_{p}-x_{n-q})}.

where
x_{0}:=a
and
x_{n-1}:=b
. We thus have

(4.25)

\displaystyle\frac{V_{a,b}(\vec{x})}{\widetilde{V}_{a,b}(\vec{x})}=\frac{\mathsf{f}_{\theta}^{\ast(n-p-q)}(x_{p}-x_{n-q})}{\mathsf{f}_{\theta}^{\ast(n-1)}(b-a)}.

By
[
Fel08
, Theorem 2, Chapter XV.5]
we know

(4.26)

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\bigg|{\sqrt{k}}\mathsf{f}_{\theta}^{\ast k}(z)-\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma}}e^{-\frac{z^{2}}{2k\sigma^{2}}}\bigg|\xrightarrow{k\to\infty}0,

where
\sigma=\int_{\mathbb{R}}x^{2}\mathsf{f}_{\theta}(x)dx
. Thus, there exist a constant
\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}_{1}>1
depending on
M,\delta_{1},\delta_{2}
such that

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}\mathsf{f}_{\theta}^{\ast(n-p-q)}(z)\leq\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}_{1}\mbox{ for all }z\in\mathbb{R},\quad\tfrac{1}{\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}\leq\sqrt{n}\mathsf{f}_{\theta}^{\ast(n-1)}(b-a)\leq{\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}_{1}}.

for all large enough
n
. Inserting these bounds in the numerator and denominator of r.h.s. (
4.25
) we get the (
4.23
) by setting
\mathrm{C}_{1}:=\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}_{1}^{2}
. When
|x_{p}-x_{n-q}|\leq\widetilde{M}\sqrt{n}
, we may utilize the limit result in (
4.26
) to obtain a new constant
\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}_{2}>0
depending on
\widetilde{M},\delta_{1},\delta_{2}
such that

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}\mathsf{f}_{\theta}^{\ast(n-p-q)}(x_{p}-x_{n-q})\geq\tfrac{1}{\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}_{2}}\mbox{ whenever }|x_{p}-x_{n-q}|\leq\widetilde{M}\sqrt{n},

for all large enough
n
. Using this bound and the upper bound for
\sqrt{n}\mathsf{f}_{\theta}^{\ast(n-1)}(b-a)
we get the desired result. We arrive at (
4.24
) by setting
\mathrm{C}_{2}:=\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}_{2}\cdot\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}_{1}
.
∎

With all the preparatory results in hand, we are now ready to prove Propositions
3.9
and
4.1
. Recall that in the introduction we gave a proof sketch for Proposition
3.9
(that does not appeal to Proposition
4.1
). In what follows, we shall use the techniques outlined in that sketch to establish more sophisticated intermediate results (such as Lemma
4.11
). These results will allow us to prove Proposition
4.1
first. Then using those intermediate results we shall then establish Proposition
3.9
.

Proof of Proposition
4.1
for the
p=2
case (supercritical phase).

We split the proof into several steps.

Step 1.
In this step, we reduce the proof of Proposition
4.1
to the claim around (
4.27
). Fix
r>0
. Set
T:=\lfloor rN^{2/3}\rfloor
and
n=2T
. Recall
y_{i}
’s and the event
\mathsf{RP}_{2,M}
from the statement of the lemma. Since
\mathsf{RP}_{2,M}
is a monotone event, by Proposition
2.6
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{2}}^{\vec{y},(-\infty)^{2T};2,2T}(\mathsf{RP}_{2,M})\geq\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{2}}^{\vec{x},(-\infty)^{2T};2,2T}(\mathsf{RP}_{2,M}).

where
x_{1}=-2MN^{1/3}
,
x_{2}=-2MN^{1/3}-\sqrt{n}
. By translation invariance (Lemma
2.1

(a)
), we may lift the Gibbs measure by
2MN^{1/3}
units so that the boundary conditions changes from
(x_{1},x_{2})
to
(0,-\sqrt{n})
. The
\mathsf{RP}_{2,M}
event now requires the second curve to be above the (lifted) barrier
4MN^{1/3}
under this new boundary condition. Since
4MN^{1/3}\leq 8Mr^{-1/2}\sqrt{n}
. it thus suffices to show that there exists
\phi=\phi(r,M)>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{2}}^{(0,-\sqrt{n}),(-\infty)^{n};2,n}\bigg(\inf_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket}L_{2}(i)\geq 8Mr^{-1/2}\sqrt{n}\bigg)\geq\phi.

for all large enough
n
. Towards this end we claim that there exists
\phi=\phi(r,M)>0
such that

(4.27)

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{2}}^{(0,-\sqrt{n}),(-\infty)^{2n};2,n}(\mathsf{D}_{m})\geq 2\phi,

where

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{m}:=\bigg\{(L_{1}(2i-1),L_{2}(2i))\in(10m\sqrt{n},11m\sqrt{n})^{2}\mbox{ for all }i\in\llbracket 1,n/2\rrbracket\bigg\},

and
m:=Mr^{-1/2}
. Let us complete the proof assuming (
4.27
). Note that (
4.27
) controls the even points of the second curve. By Lemma
4.4
, we know conditioned on the even points,
L_{2}(2k+1)\sim\xi_{\theta,\theta;-1}^{(L_{2}(2k),L_{2}(2k+2))}
for
k=1,2,\ldots,2n-1
. In view of Lemma
B.4
, on the event
\mathsf{D}_{m}
we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{L_{2}(2k+1)\leq 8m\sqrt{n}}\mid\sigma\big(L_{2}(2k),L_{2}(2k+2)\big)\right]\leq\mathrm{C}e^{-\frac{1}{\mathrm{C}}m\sqrt{n}}.

By Lemma
4.4
,
L_{2}(1)\sim G_{\alpha_{2}+\theta,1}+L_{2}(2)
. Since the density
G_{\alpha_{2}+\theta,1}
have exponential tails, we see that on the event
\mathsf{D}_{m}
we have
\mathbb{P}(L_{2}(1)\leq 8m\sqrt{n}\mid L_{2}(2))\leq\mathrm{C}e^{-\frac{1}{\mathrm{C}}m\sqrt{n}}.

Thus by a union bound,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{2}}^{(0,-\sqrt{n}),(-\infty)^{2n};2,n}\bigg(\inf_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket}L_{2}(i)\geq 8m\sqrt{n}\bigg)\geq\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{2}}^{(0,-\sqrt{n}),(-\infty)^{2n};2,n}(\mathsf{D}_{m})-\mathrm{C}\cdot ne^{-\frac{1}{\mathrm{C}}m\sqrt{n}}\geq\phi,

for large enough
n
. This establishes Proposition
4.1
for
p=2
, modulo (
4.27
).

Step 2.
In this and subsequent steps we prove (
4.27
). Recall the
\mathsf{PRW}
and
\mathsf{WPRW}
laws from Definition
1.7
. Recall from Lemma
4.4
that
(L_{1}(2i-1),L_{2}(2i))_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket}\sim{\mathbb{P}}_{\operatorname{WPRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}
. We the terminology from Definition
1.7
to write

(4.28)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{\operatorname{WPRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}(\mathsf{D}_{m})=\frac{{\mathbb{E}}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}[W_{\operatorname{sc}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{D}_{m}}]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}[W_{\operatorname{sc}}]}

where
W_{\operatorname{sc}}
is defined in (
1.15
) and
\mathsf{D}_{m}
is now defined as

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{m}:=\big\{(S_{1}(i),S_{2}(i))\in(10m\sqrt{n},11m\sqrt{n})^{2}\mbox{ for all }i\in\llbracket 1,n/2\rrbracket\big\}.

For the remainder of the proof we write
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{E}
for
{\mathbb{P}}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}
and
{\mathbb{E}}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}
respectively. We claim that there exists constants
\mathrm{C}>0
and
\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}=\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}(m)>0
such that

(4.29)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[W_{\operatorname{sc}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{D}_{m}}]\leq\tfrac{\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}^{-1}}{\sqrt{n}},\qquad\mathbb{E}[W_{\operatorname{sc}}]\geq\tfrac{\mathrm{C}}{\sqrt{n}}.

Clearly plugging these bounds back in (
4.28
) verifies (
4.27
). Let us thus focus on proving (
4.29
). For the upper bound we use the following lemma.

Lemma 4.11
.

There exists a constant
\mathrm{C}>0
such that for all Borel sets
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[W_{\operatorname{sc}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}\right]\leq\tfrac{\mathrm{C}}{n}+\tfrac{\mathrm{C}}{\sqrt{n}}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}\Big((S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)+1)\vee 1\Big)\Big(\tfrac{|S_{1}(1)|}{\sqrt{n}}\vee 2\Big)^{3/2}\bigg],

where
\mathsf{A}:=\{(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1))\in A\}
.

Note that taking
A=\mathbb{R}^{2}
in Lemma
4.11
and utilizing the exponential tail estimates of
|S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)|
and
|S_{1}(1)|/\sqrt{n}
from Lemma
4.7
, the upper bound in (
4.29
) follows.

Proof of Lemma
4.11
.

As in (
1.18
), define

(4.30)

\displaystyle\mathsf{NI}_{p}:=\left\{S_{1}(k)-S_{2}(k)\geq-p\mbox{ for all }k\in\llbracket 2,n-1\rrbracket\right\}.

We set
\mathsf{NI}:=\mathsf{NI}_{0}
. Here
\mathsf{NI}
stands for non-intersection. Observe that for any
q\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
we have

(4.31)

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{NI}}+\sum_{p=0}^{q}\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{NI}_{p+1}\cap\mathsf{NI}_{p}^{c}}+\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{NI}_{q}^{c}}=1.

Thus, taking
q=\lfloor\log\log n\rfloor
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[W_{\operatorname{sc}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[W_{\operatorname{sc}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{NI}}]+\sum_{p=0}^{\lfloor\log\log n\rfloor-1}\mathbb{E}[W_{\operatorname{sc}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{NI}_{p+1}\cap\mathsf{NI}_{p}^{c}}]+\mathbb{E}[W_{\operatorname{sc}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{NI}_{\lfloor\log\log n\rfloor}^{c}}]

(4.32)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{p=0}^{\lfloor\log\log n\rfloor}e^{-e^{p-1}}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{NI}_{p}\right)+\tfrac{1}{n},

where the above inequality follows by noting that on
\mathsf{NI}_{p}^{c}
we have
W\leq e^{-e^{p-1}}
. For the probability term above we condition on
\mathcal{F}:=\sigma(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1))
and write
\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{NI}_{p})=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{NI}_{p}}\mid\mathcal{F}]\right]
. Upon conditioning on
S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1)
, the paired random walk
\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}
law is equal to the law of two independent
n
-step random walks starting from
(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1))
to
(0,-\sqrt{n})
. Thanks to this observation, we may now appeal to Lemma
C.8
to conclude that
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{NI}_{p}}\mid\mathcal{F}]\leq e^{\mathrm{C}p}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{NI}}\mid\mathcal{F}]
holds for some deterministic constant
\mathrm{C}>0
. This allows us to conclude

\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{NI}_{p})\leq e^{\mathrm{C}p}\cdot\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{NI}).

Plugging this into (
4.32
) and observing that the series
\sum_{p\geq 0}e^{-e^{p-1}}\cdot e^{\mathrm{C}p}
is summable shows

(4.33)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[W_{\operatorname{sc}}\mathbf{1}_{A}]\leq\tfrac{1}{n}+\mathrm{C}\cdot\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{NI}).

Thus to suffices to bound
\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{NI})
. Towards this end, we first define the event

\displaystyle\mathsf{B}:=\left\{|S_{i}(1)|\leq(\log n)^{3/2}\sqrt{n}\mbox{ for }i=1,2,\mbox{ and }|S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)|\leq(\log n)^{3/2}\right\}.

By the union bound we have
\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{NI})\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{B}\cap\mathsf{NI})+\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{B}^{c}).

For the second term note that by tail estimates from Lemma
4.7
we have

(4.34)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{B}^{c})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{2}\mathbb{P}(|S_{i}(1)|\geq(\log n)^{3/2})+\mathbb{P}\left(|S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)|\geq(\log n)^{3/2}\right)\leq\tfrac{\mathrm{C}}{n}.

For the first term we write
\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{B}\cap\mathsf{NI})=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{B}}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{NI}}\mid\mathcal{F}]\right]
. Again, since upon conditioning on
S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1)
, the paired random walk law is equal to the law of of two independent
n
-step random walks starting from
(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1))
to
(0,-\sqrt{n})
, we may use random bridge estimates from Appendix
C
. In particular, Lemma
C.9
(recall Corollary
4.6
) shows there exists a constant
\mathrm{C}>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{B}}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{NI}}\mid\mathcal{F}]\leq\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{B}}\cdot\tfrac{\mathrm{C}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big((S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)+1)\vee 1\Big)\Big(\tfrac{|S_{1}(1)|}{\sqrt{n}}\vee 2\Big)^{3/2}.

for all
n
. Taking expectation with respect to
\mathcal{F}
on both sides of the above equation and then using the fact that
\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{B}}\leq\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}
leads to

(4.35)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{B}\cap\mathsf{NI})\leq\tfrac{\mathrm{C}}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}}\Big((S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)+1)\vee 1\Big)\Big(\tfrac{|S_{1}(1)|}{\sqrt{n}}\vee 2\Big)^{3/2}\right].

Inserting this bound along with the bound in (
4.34
) back in (
4.33
), we arrive at the desired bound stated in the statement of the lemma.
∎

Step 3.
In this step we prove the lower bound in (
4.29
). Towards this end, consider the event

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{m}:=\big\{1\leq S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)\leq 2,\mbox{ and }S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1)\in(\tfrac{41}{4}m\sqrt{n},\tfrac{43}{4}m\sqrt{n})\big\},

and
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}:=\sigma(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1))
. Fix any
\beta>0
and recall
\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta}
from (
4.22
). We have

(4.36)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[W_{\operatorname{sc}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{D}_{m}}]\geq\mathbb{E}[W_{\operatorname{sc}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{E}_{m}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{D}_{m}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta}}]\geq a_{\beta}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{E}_{m}}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{D}_{m}\cap\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta}}\mid{\mathcal{F}}]\big]

where the second inequality above follows by noting that
W\geq a_{\beta}
on
\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta}\cap\mathsf{E}
(Lemma
4.8
). As mentioned in the proof of Lemma
4.11
, upon conditioning on
\mathcal{F}
, the paired random walk
\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}
law is equal to the law of two independent
n
-step random walks starting from
(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1))
to
(0,-\sqrt{n})
. For simplicity set
b_{1}=0
,
b_{2}=-\sqrt{n}
. We shall use the comparison between random bridge to
(n;n\rho,0)
-modified random bridge from Lemma
4.10
, for a special
\rho\in(0,1)
coming from Corollary
C.6
(recall Corollary
4.6
). Using the lower bound in (
4.24
) we get

(4.37)

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{E}_{m}}\cdot\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{D}_{m}\cap\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta}}\mid\mathcal{F}]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{E}_{m}}\mathrm{C}^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho}^{(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1))}(\mathsf{D}_{m}\cap\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{E}_{m}}\mathrm{C}^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho}^{(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1))}(\mathsf{D}_{m}\cap\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta}\mid\mathsf{NI})\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho}^{(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1))}(\mathsf{NI})

for some
\mathrm{C}>0
depending on
m
and
\rho
. Here
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho}^{(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1))}:=\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{(n;n\rho,0);(S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1)),(0,\sqrt{n})}
is the joint law of two independent
(n;n\rho,0)
-modified random bridge from
S_{i}(1)
to
b_{i}
defined in Definition
4.9
.
We now claim that there exists
\widetilde{\phi}=\widetilde{\phi}(m)>0
such that

(4.38)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho}^{(a_{1},a_{2})}(\mathsf{D}_{m}\mid\mathsf{NI})\geq 2\widetilde{\phi}.

uniformly over all
(a_{1},a_{2})\in\mathcal{P}_{1}
where we define

\mathcal{P}_{1}:=\big\{(z_{1},z_{2})\in(\tfrac{41}{4}m\sqrt{n},\tfrac{43}{4}\sqrt{n}):1\leq z_{1}-z_{2}\leq 2\big\}.

We postpone the proof of this claim to the next step. Let us complete the proof of the lemma assuming it. Since, under
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho}^{(a_{1},a_{2})}
,
S_{1}(\llbracket 1,n\rho\rrbracket),S_{2}(\llbracket 1,n\rho\rrbracket)
are two independent random walks, we may use non-intersection type estimates for random walks from Appendix
C
. In particular, using Lemmas
C.3
and
C.5
(recall Corollary
4.6
) we can get constants
\delta>0,M_{2}>0
and
\mathrm{C}_{1}>0
all depending on
m
and
\rho
such that uniformly over
(a_{1},a_{2})\in\mathcal{P}_{1}
we have

\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho}^{(a_{1},a_{2})}\big(S_{1}(k)\geq S_{2}(k)\mbox{ for all }k\in\llbracket 2,n\rho\rrbracket,S_{1}(n\rho)-S_{2}(n\rho)\geq\delta\sqrt{n},|S_{i}(n\rho)|\leq M_{2}\sqrt{n}\big)\geq\tfrac{\mathrm{C}_{1}^{-1}}{\sqrt{n}}.

Set
\mathsf{G}:=\{S_{1}(n\rho)-S_{2}(n\rho)\geq\delta\sqrt{n},|S_{i}(n\rho)|\leq M_{2}\sqrt{n}\}
. Recall from the definition of
(n;n\rho,0)
-modified random bridge that on
\llbracket n\rho,n\rrbracket
the modified random bridge is just a random bridge from
S_{i}(n\rho)
to
b_{i}
. Applying Lemma
C.7
(recall Corollary
4.6
) it follows that

\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{G}}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho}^{(a_{1},a_{2})}\big(S_{1}(k)\geq S_{2}(k)\mbox{ for all }k\in\llbracket n\rho,n-1\rrbracket\big)\geq\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{G}}\cdot{\mathrm{C}_{2}^{-1}},

for some constant
\mathrm{C}_{2}>0
depending on
m
and
\rho
only. Thus we get
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho}^{(a_{1},a_{2})}(\mathsf{NI})\geq\frac{\mathrm{C}_{4}^{-1}}{\sqrt{n}}
uniformly on
\mathsf{E}_{m}
for some deterministic constant
\mathrm{C}_{4}
depending on
m
and
\rho
only. By Lemma
C.11
(recall Corollary
4.6
), we may choose
\beta
small enough depending on
m
and
\rho
such that
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho}^{(a_{1},a_{2})}(\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta}\mid\mathsf{NI})\geq 1-\widetilde{\phi}
uniformly over
(a_{1},a_{2})\in\mathcal{P}_{1}
. Plugging this estimates back in (
4.37
), we see that

\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{E}_{m}}\cdot\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{D}_{m}\cap\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta}}\mid\mathcal{F}]\geq\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{E}_{m}}\cdot\widetilde{\phi}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{C}_{4}^{-1}}{\sqrt{n}}.

Now, by Lemma
4.7
(equation (
4.17
) in particular) we know that
\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{E}_{m})\geq\mathrm{C}_{5}^{-1}>0
for some
\mathrm{C}_{5}
depending on
m
. Plugging this back in (
4.36
) we see that

(4.39)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[W_{\operatorname{sc}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{D}_{m}}]\geq a_{\beta}\cdot\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{E}_{m})\cdot\widetilde{\phi}\cdot\tfrac{\mathrm{C}_{4}^{-1}}{\sqrt{n}}=:\tfrac{\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}^{-1}}{\sqrt{n}}.

where
\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}>0
is a constant depending only on
m
and
\rho
.

Step 4.
In this step we prove (
4.38
). By equation (
C.1
) in Lemma
C.5
(recall Corollary
4.6
), we know there exists
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{8}(m\wedge 1))
small enough depending only on
\rho
such that

(4.40)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho}^{(a_{1},a_{2})}\big(S_{1}(n\rho)-S_{2}(n\rho)\geq\delta\sqrt{n}\mid\mathsf{NI}\big)\geq\tfrac{15}{16}

uniformly over
(a_{1},a_{2})\in\mathcal{P}_{1}
. We shall now choose
\rho
as
\rho(\frac{1}{16},\frac{m\wedge 1}{8})
where the latter is a constant depending on
m
and comes from Corollary
C.6
(recall Corollary
4.6
). In view of this choice of
\rho
, applying Corollary
C.6
(recall Corollary
4.6
), we see that uniformly over
(a_{1},a_{2})\in\mathcal{P}_{1}
we have

(4.41)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho}^{(a_{1},a_{2})}\left(\sup_{k\in\llbracket 1,n\rho\rrbracket,i=1,2}|S_{i}(k)-S_{i}(1)|\leq\tfrac{m\wedge 1}{8}\sqrt{n}\mid\mathsf{NI}\right)\geq\tfrac{15}{16}.

Since on
\mathcal{P}_{1}
we also have
(a_{1},a_{2})\in(\tfrac{41}{4}m\sqrt{n},\tfrac{43}{4}m\sqrt{n})
, combining (
4.40
) and (
4.41
) we get

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho}^{(a_{1},a_{2})}\bigg(\big\{S_{1}(n\rho)-S_{2}(n\rho)\geq\delta\sqrt{n}\big\}\cap\mathsf{K}_{1}\mid\mathsf{NI}\bigg)\geq\tfrac{7}{8},

where

\displaystyle\mathsf{K}_{1}:=\left\{S_{1}(k),S_{2}(k)\in(\tfrac{81}{8}m\sqrt{n},\tfrac{87}{8}m\sqrt{n})\mbox{ for all }k\in\llbracket 1,n\rho\rrbracket,\mbox{ and }|S_{1}(n\rho)-S_{2}(n\rho)|\leq\tfrac{\sqrt{n}}{2}\right\}.

Following the definition of
(n;n\rho,0)
-modified random bridge, to prove (
4.38
) it suffices to show

(4.42)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n-n\rho+1;(c_{1},c_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}\bigg(\big\{S_{1}(k),S_{2}(k)\in(10m\sqrt{n},11m\sqrt{n})\mbox{ for all }k\in\llbracket 1,n/2\rrbracket\big\}\cap\mathsf{NI}\bigg)\geq\tfrac{16}{7}\widetilde{\phi},

uniformly over
(c_{1},c_{2})\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
where

\mathcal{P}_{2}:=\{(c_{1},c_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:c_{i}\in(\tfrac{81}{8}m\sqrt{n},\tfrac{87}{8}m\sqrt{n}),\mbox{ and }\tfrac{1}{2}\sqrt{n}\geq c_{1}-c_{2}\geq\delta\sqrt{n}\}.

and
\mathbb{P}^{n-n\rho+1;(c_{1},c_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}
is the law of two independent random bridges of length
n-n\rho+1
starting at
(c_{1},c_{2})
and ending at
(b_{1},b_{2})
. For simplicity set
u:=n-n\rho+1\geq\frac{3}{4}n
. By the KMT coupling of random bridges
[
DW21a
]
we may assume there are two independent Brownian bridges
B_{1},B_{2}
(with variance
\int x^{2}\mathsf{f}_{\theta}(x)dx
) on the same probability space such that

(4.43)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{u;(c_{1},c_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}\left(\sup_{k\in\llbracket 1,u\rrbracket,i=1,2}|S_{i}(k)-\sqrt{u}B_{i}(k/u)-c_{i}-\tfrac{k}{u}(b_{i}-c_{i})|\geq\mathrm{C}\log n\right)\leq\tfrac{1}{n}.

Let
r_{n,i}(x)
be the piece-wise linear function interpolated by three points:
r_{n,i}(0)=r_{n,i}(1)=0
and
r_{n,i}(3/4)=\frac{3}{4\sqrt{u}}(b_{i}-c_{i})
. Let
\mathcal{U}_{i}
be the
L^{\infty}
open ball of
r_{n,i}(x)
of radius
\frac{1}{4}\delta
(this is the same
\delta
that was chosen at the beginning of Step 4). By properties of Brownian bridge, there exists a
\widetilde{\phi}=\widetilde{\phi}(m)>0
such that for all
(c_{1},c_{2})\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{u;(c_{1},c_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}(B^{(i)}\in\mathcal{U}_{i}\mbox{ for }i=1,2)\geq\tfrac{32}{7}\widetilde{\phi}.

Note that the above equation along with (
4.43
) implies that with probability
\tfrac{32}{7}\widetilde{\phi}-\frac{1}{n}
, for all
n
large enough (and hence
u
large enough) under the law
\mathbb{P}^{u;(c_{1},c_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}
we have the following items simultaneously:

•

For all
k\in\llbracket 1,3/4u\rrbracket

|S_{i}(k)-c_{i}|\leq\mathrm{C}\log n+\tfrac{1}{4}\sqrt{u}\delta\leq\tfrac{m}{8}\sqrt{u}<\tfrac{m}{8}\sqrt{n}.

•

For all
k\in\llbracket 1,u\rrbracket
we have

\displaystyle S_{1}(k)

\displaystyle\geq\sqrt{u}r_{n,1}(\tfrac{k}{u})+c_{1}+\tfrac{k}{u}(b_{1}-c_{1})-\tfrac{1}{4}\sqrt{u}\delta-\mathrm{C}\log n

\displaystyle\geq S_{2}(k)+\sqrt{u}(r_{n,1}(\tfrac{k}{u})-r_{n,2}(\tfrac{k}{u}))-\tfrac{1}{2}\sqrt{u}\delta+c_{1}-c_{2}+\tfrac{k}{u}(b_{1}-b_{2}-c_{1}+c_{2})-2\mathrm{C}\log n

\displaystyle\geq\sqrt{u}(r_{n,1}(k/u)-r_{n,2}(k/u))+\tfrac{1}{2}\sqrt{u}\delta-2\mathrm{C}\log n+S_{2}(k).

We have
r_{n,1}(x)\geq r_{n,2}(x)
by construction, and
c_{1}-c_{2}+\tfrac{k}{u}(b_{1}-b_{2}-c_{1}+c_{2})\geq\sqrt{u}\delta
for all
(c_{1},c_{2})\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
. Thus for all large enough
n
,
S_{1}(k)>S_{2}(k)
for all
k\in\llbracket 0,u\rrbracket
.

For
n
large enough
\tfrac{32}{7}\widetilde{\phi}-\frac{1}{n}\geq\tfrac{16}{7}\widetilde{\phi}
. This establishes (
4.42
) and hence also Proposition
4.1
. ∎

Corollary 4.12
.

There exists an absolute constant
\mathrm{C}>0
such that for all
n\geq 1
.

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}[W_{\operatorname{sc}}]\geq\tfrac{\mathrm{C}^{-1}}{\sqrt{n}}.

The above corollary follows from (
4.39
) as
{\mathbb{E}}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}[W_{\operatorname{sc}}]\geq{\mathbb{E}}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(0,-\sqrt{n})}[W_{\operatorname{sc}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{D}_{1}}]
. We remark that here it is important that the endpoints are
O(\sqrt{n})
apart to get the precise order of
\mathbb{E}[W_{\operatorname{sc}}]
. We expect a different order if the endpoints are closer or lie in a reversed order. Later, in Lemma
5.6
, we shall prove a different lower bound for
{\mathbb{E}}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{n;(x,y)}[W_{\operatorname{sc}}]
that is uniform over all possible endpoints
(x,y)
in a specific window.

Proof of Proposition
3.9
in the
p=2
case (supercritical phase).

Given the machinery developed in the above proof, proof of Proposition
3.9
follows easily. By Lemma
4.4
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{2}}^{(0,-\sqrt{T}),(-\infty)^{T};2,T}(|L_{i}(i)|\geq M\sqrt{T})

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}_{\operatorname{WPRW}}^{T;(0,-\sqrt{T})}(|S_{i}(1)|\geq M\sqrt{T})

(4.44)

\displaystyle=\frac{{\mathbb{E}}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{T;(0,-\sqrt{T})}[W_{\operatorname{sc}}\mathbf{1}_{{|S_{i}(1)|\geq M\sqrt{T}}}]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{T;(0,-\sqrt{T})}[W_{\operatorname{sc}}]}.

Now by Corollary
4.12
we have
{\mathbb{E}}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{T;(0,-\sqrt{T})}[W_{\operatorname{sc}}]\geq\frac{\mathrm{C}}{\sqrt{T}}
and by Lemma
4.11
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{T;(0,-\sqrt{T})}[W_{\operatorname{sc}}\mathbf{1}_{|S_{i}(1)|\geq M\sqrt{T}}]

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{T}+\tfrac{\mathrm{C}}{\sqrt{T}}{\mathbb{E}}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{T;(0,-\sqrt{T})}\bigg[\mathbf{1}_{|S_{i}(1)|\geq M\sqrt{T}}\Big((S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)+1)\vee 1\Big)\Big(\tfrac{|S_{i}(1)|}{\sqrt{T}}\vee 2\Big)^{3}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{T}+\tfrac{\mathrm{C}}{\sqrt{T}}\sqrt{{\mathbb{E}}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{T;(0,-\sqrt{T})}\left[\Big((S_{1}(1)-S_{2}(1)+1)\vee 1\Big)^{2}\right]}\sqrt{{\mathbb{E}}_{\operatorname{PRW}}^{T;(0,-\sqrt{T})}\left[\mathbf{1}_{|S_{i}(1)|\geq M\sqrt{T}}\Big(\tfrac{|S_{1}(1)|}{\sqrt{T}}\vee 2\Big)^{6}\right]},

where the last inequality follows from Cauchy-Schwarz. Taking
T
and
M
large enough, in view of the tail estimates from Lemma
4.7
, it follows that (
4.44
) can be made arbitrarily small. By a union bound, we can thus make
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{2}}^{(0,-\sqrt{T}),(-\infty)^{T};2,T}(|L_{1}(1)|+|L_{2}(2)|\geq M\sqrt{T})
arbitrarily small by taking
T
and
M
large enough.
∎

5.
Modulus of continuity: proof of Theorem
1.1

In this section we prove our main theorem, Theorem
1.1
, about spatial tightness of
\mathpzc{HSLG}
polymers. Due to the relation in (
1.7
), Theorem
1.1
essentially follows by controlling modulus of continuity of the first curve of log-gamma line ensemble. Towards this end, we recall the definition of modulus of continuity function. Given a continuous function
f:\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\to\mathbb{R}
and
U>1
, we define the modulus of continuity function as

(5.1)

\displaystyle\omega_{\delta}^{N}(f;\llbracket 1,U\rrbracket):=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},i_{2}\in\llbracket 1,U\rrbracket\\
|i_{1}-i_{2}|\leq\delta N^{2/3}\end{subarray}}|f(i_{1})-f(i_{2})|.

We have the following result that is proved in Section
5.2
.

Proposition 5.1
.

Fix
r,\gamma>0
and
p\in\{1,2\}
. Set
\alpha=\alpha_{p}
according to (
3.11
). We have

(5.2)

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{p}}\left(\omega_{\delta}^{N}(\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N},\llbracket 1,2\lfloor rN^{2/3}\rfloor-1\rrbracket)\geq\gamma N^{1/3}\right)=0.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

By a standard criterion for functional tightness
[
Bil13
, Theorem 7.3]
, Proposition
5.1
along with endpoint tightness from Theorem
3.10
implies tightness of the probability law of
N^{-1/3}\mathcal{L}_{1}^{N}(\llbracket 1,2\lfloor rN^{2/3}\rfloor-1\rrbracket)
. In light of the matching in distribution in Theorem
1.3
(i), this immediately translates into tightness of the measure
\mathbb{P}^{N}_{\alpha}
desired to prove Theorem
1.1
. Note that cases (1) and (2) of Theorem
1.1
follow by using the
p=1
and
p=2
cases of Proposition
5.1
and Theorem
3.10
.
∎

The rest of this section is devoted to proving Proposition
5.1
. This relies on the following technical result which deals with the modulus of continuity for the bottom-free measure.

Proposition 5.2
.

Fix any
M,V,k_{1},k_{2},\gamma>0
with
k_{2}>k_{1}
. For each
N>0
, define the sets

\displaystyle I_{1,M}:=\{y\in\mathbb{R},|y|\leq 2MN^{1/3}\},\mbox{ and }I_{2,M}:=\{(y_{1},y_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:y_{i}\in I_{1,M/2},y_{1}-y_{2}\geq-(\log N)^{7/6}\}.

For each
p\in\{1,2\}
, there exist
\delta=\delta(M,V,k_{1},k_{2},\gamma,\varepsilon)>0
and
N_{0}=N_{0}(M,V,k_{1},k_{2},\gamma,\varepsilon)>0
such that for all
\vec{x}\in I_{p,M}
,
T\in\llbracket k_{1}N^{2/3},k_{2}N^{2/3}\rrbracket
, and
N\geq N_{0}
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{p}\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{p}}^{\vec{x},(-\infty)^{T};p,T}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{i}|L_{k}(k)|\leq VN^{1/3},\mbox{ and }\omega_{\delta}^{N}(L_{i},\llbracket 1,T/4+i-2\rrbracket)\geq\gamma N^{1/3}\right)\leq\varepsilon.

We postpone the proof of Proposition
5.2
to Section
5.3
. Section
5.1
contains a few lemmas that are used in the proof of Proposition
5.1
later in Section
5.2
.

5.1.
Preparatory Lemmas

We first discuss a few consequences of Proposition
3.9
that form the preparatory tools for our modulus of continuity analysis.

Lemma 5.3
.

Fix any
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
and
T\geq 2
. Let
(X(i))_{i=1}^{2T-1}
be a random vector with
X(1)=X(2T-1)\equiv 0
and density at
(X_{i})_{i=2}^{2T-2}=(u_{i})_{2=1}^{2T-2}
proportional to

\prod_{i=1}^{2T-2}G_{\theta,(-1)^{i+1}}(u_{i}-u_{i+1})

respectively where
u_{1}=0
and
u_{2T-1}=0
and
G
is defined in (
2.2
). Similarly, define an independent random vector
(Y(i))_{i=1}^{2T-1}
precisely as with
X
except that
G_{\theta,(-1)^{i+1}}
is replaced by
G_{\theta,(-1)^{i}}
. Then, there exists
M_{0}(\varepsilon)>0
such that for all
T\geq 2
we have

(5.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg(\sup_{i\in\llbracket 1,2T-1\rrbracket}\big(|X(i)|+|Y(i)|\big)\geq M_{0}\sqrt{T}\bigg)\leq\varepsilon.

We refer to Figure
17
for graphical representation of the distributions appearing in Lemma
5.3
.

0
0
0
0

Figure 17
.
Graphical representation of
X
(left) and
Y
(right) distribution from Lemma
5.3
.

Proof.

Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Note that
(X(2i-1))_{i=1}^{T}
forms a random bridge from
0
to
0
with increment from
G_{\theta,+1}\ast G_{\theta,-1}
. By the KMT coupling for random bridges
[
DW21a
]
along with Brownian bridge estimates, there exists a constant
M>0
such that (here we temporarily use
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{E}
for the probability and expectation for the
X
and
Y
vectors)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{A})\leq\tfrac{\varepsilon}{4},\mbox{ where }\mathsf{A}:=\left\{\sup_{i\in\llbracket 1,T\rrbracket}|X(2i-1)|\geq M\sqrt{T}\right\}.

Let us write
\mathcal{F}:=\sigma\big((X(2i-1))_{i=1}^{T}\big)
. By a union bound we have

(5.4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{i\in\llbracket 1,2T-1\rrbracket}|X(i)|\geq 5M\sqrt{T}\Big)\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}+\sum_{i=1}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\neg\mathsf{A}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{|X(2i)|\geq 5M\sqrt{T}}\mid\mathcal{F}\right]\right].

Note that the distribution of even points given the odd points are given by the
\xi
-distribution introduced in (
4.5
). Observe that by Lemma
B.4
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{X(2i-1),X(2i+1)\in(-M\sqrt{T},M\sqrt{T})}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{|X(2i)|\geq 5M\sqrt{T}}\mid\mathcal{F}\right]\leq\mathrm{C}\exp(-\tfrac{1}{\mathrm{C}}\sqrt{T}),

for some absolute constant
\mathrm{C}>0
. Plugging the above bound back in (
5.4
) and taking
T
large enough we get that r.h.s. (
5.4
) is at most
\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
. Similarly we see that
\mathbb{P}\big(\sup_{i\in\llbracket 1,2T-1\rrbracket}|Y(i)|\geq 5M\sqrt{T}\big)\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
. Adjusting
M
, we arrive at (
5.7
).
∎

Lemma 5.4
.

Recall for
p\in\{1,2\}
,
\alpha:=\alpha_{p}
from (
3.11
). Fix any
r\geq 1
and
\varepsilon>0
. Set
T=\lfloor rN^{2/3}\rfloor
. There exists
M=M(\varepsilon)>0
and
N_{0}(\varepsilon)>0
such that for all
N\geq N_{0}
we have

(5.5)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{1}}^{0,(-\infty)^{T};1,T}\bigg(\sup_{i\in\llbracket 1,2T-1\rrbracket}|L_{1}(i)|\geq M\sqrt{T}\bigg)\leq\varepsilon,

(5.6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{2}}^{(0,-\sqrt{T}),(-\infty)^{T};2,T}\bigg(\sup_{i\in\llbracket 1,2T-1\rrbracket}|L_{1}(i)|+\sup_{j\in\llbracket 1,2T\rrbracket}|L_{2}(j)|\geq M\sqrt{T}\bigg)\leq\varepsilon,

where the bottom free law
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{p}}^{\vec{x},(-\infty)^{2T};2,T}
is defined in Definition
2.4
.

Proof.

For clarity we divide the proof into two steps.

Step 1.
Fix any
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
and consider
M_{0}(\varepsilon)
from Lemma
5.3
. In this step we prove (
5.5
). From Proposition
3.9
choose
M_{1}(\varepsilon)>0
such that for all large enough
T
we have

(5.7)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{1}}^{0,(-\infty)^{T};1,T}\big(|L_{1}(1)|\geq M_{1}\sqrt{T}\big)\geq\varepsilon,\quad\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{2}}^{(0,-\sqrt{T}),(-\infty)^{T};2,T}\big(|L_{1}(1)|+|L_{2}(2)|\geq M_{1}\sqrt{T}\big)\geq\varepsilon.

We will use the first bound immediately, and the second a bit later.
Set
M_{3}:=2M_{0}+M_{1}+1
, and

\displaystyle\mathsf{A}_{1}:=\left\{\sup_{i\in\llbracket 1,2T-1\rrbracket}L_{1}(i)\geq(M_{3}+M_{0})\sqrt{T}\right\},\qquad\mathsf{A}_{2}:=\left\{\sup_{i\in\llbracket 2,2T\rrbracket}L_{2}(i)\geq(M_{0}+M_{1})\sqrt{T}\right\}.

Finally, introduce shorthand notation
\mathbb{P}_{1}
for
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{1}}^{(0,-\sqrt{T}),(-\infty)^{T};1,T}
and
\mathbb{P}_{2}
for
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{2}}^{(0,-\sqrt{T}),(-\infty)^{T};2,T}
(and likewise for
\mathbb{E}
).
In view of (
5.7
), by a union bound we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{1}(\mathsf{A}_{1})\leq\varepsilon+\mathbb{E}_{1}\left[\mathbf{1}_{L_{1}(1)\leq M_{1}\sqrt{T}}\mathbb{E}_{1}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}_{1}}\mid\sigma(L_{1}(1))\right]\right].

As
\mathsf{A}_{1}
is an increasing event with respect to the boundary data, due to stochastic monotonicity (Proposition
2.6
), increasing the boundaries will only increase the conditional probability. Thus to get an upper bound, we may assume
L_{1}(1)=L_{1}(2T-1)=M_{1}\sqrt{T}
. But note that under this boundary condition we have
(L_{1}(i)-M_{1}\sqrt{T})_{i=1}^{2T-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}(X(i))_{i=1}^{2T-1}
, where
X(\cdot)
is defined in Lemma
5.3
. Thus, owing to (
5.3
), almost surely we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{L_{1}(1)\leq M_{1}\sqrt{T}}\mathbb{E}_{1}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}_{1}}\mid\sigma(L_{1}(1))\right]\leq\mathbb{P}\bigg(\sup_{i\in\llbracket 1,2T-1\rrbracket}|X(i)|\geq(2M_{0}+M)\sqrt{T}\bigg)\leq\varepsilon.

This implies
\mathbb{P}_{1}(\mathsf{A}_{1})\leq 2\varepsilon
. Following similar calculations one can show

\mathbb{P}_{1}\bigg(\inf_{i\in\llbracket 1,2T-1\rrbracket}L_{1}(i)\leq-(M_{3}+M_{0})\sqrt{T}\bigg)\leq 2\varepsilon.

This proves (
5.5
) with
M=M_{3}
for
\varepsilon\mapsto 2\varepsilon
.

Step 2.
In this step we prove (
5.6
). At this point we encourage the readers to look at Figure
18
and its caption for an overview of the proof idea.

\begin{overpic}[ty1.pdf]
