Figure 19
.
(A) A possible domain
\Lambda
includes all the vertices in the shaded region.
w_{i}
’s are the vertices of
\Lambda
enumerated in lexicographic order. Directed edges
e_{r}
going are shown above for
r=5
and
r=8
. These are the blue edges with
w_{r}
as the left point of
e_{r}
. (B) The domain
\Lambda_{5}
includes the vertices in the shaded region.
Q_{5}
is the set of all red and black edges that have one vertex as
w_{6}
and one vertex in
\partial\Lambda_{6}
. In the above figure,
Q_{5}
is composed of two black edges that points toward
w_{6}
.

Define

\displaystyle h_{r}\big(x;(u_{v})_{v\in\partial\Lambda_{r}}\big):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{|\Lambda_{r-1}|}}\prod_{e=\{v_{1}\to v_{2}\}\in E_{r}\setminus\{e_{r}\}}W_{e}(u_{v_{1}}-u_{v_{2}})\prod_{v\in\Lambda_{r-1}}du_{v}

with the convention
u_{w_{r}}=x
. Observe that the difference between
H_{r}
from (
A.1
) and
h_{r}
above is that the weight of the directed blue edge
e_{r}
is included in the former but not in the latter. Note that the vertices of
e_{r}
are not in
\Lambda_{r-1}
. Thus in the definition of
H_{r}
, the edge weight function corresponding to
e_{r}
can be pulled out of the integrand leading to

(A.5)

\displaystyle H_{r}\big(x;(u_{v})_{v\in\partial\Lambda_{r}}\big)=h_{r}\big(x;(u_{v})_{v\in\partial\Lambda_{r}}\big)\cdot F_{r}(u_{w_{r}+(0,1)}-x)

where
F_{r}(y)
is the directed blue edge weight corresponding to
e_{r}
, i.e.,
F_{r}(y):=e^{\vartheta_{r}y-e^{y}}
or
F_{r}(y)=e^{-\vartheta_{r}y-e^{-y}}
depending on the direction of the
e_{r}
edge between
w_{r}
and
w_{r}+(0,1)
. Here
\vartheta_{r}
is the parameter linked to the blue edge
e_{r}
.

With the above introduced notation, we now turn towards the proof of (
A.2
). Note that given a function
P(x)=e^{-R(x)}
with
R
being convex, we have

(A.6)

\displaystyle P(\delta-\beta)P(\gamma-\alpha)\geq P(\delta-\alpha)P(\gamma-\beta)

for all
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\in\mathbb{R}
with
\alpha\leq\beta
and
\gamma\leq\delta
. All our weight functions in (
1.5
) are of this type. In particular, this implies that (
A.6
) holds for
P=F_{r}
. In view of this and the relation (
A.5
), to show (
A.2
) it suffices to show the same holds for
h_{r}
replacing
H_{r}
, i.e.,

(A.7)

\displaystyle h_{r}\big(s;(u_{v})_{v\in\partial\Lambda_{r}}\big)h_{r}\big(t;(u_{v}^{\prime})_{v\in\partial\Lambda_{r}}\big)\leq h_{r}\big(s;(u_{v}^{\prime})_{v\in\partial\Lambda_{r}}\big)h_{r}\big(t;(u_{v})_{v\in\partial\Lambda_{r}}\big).

We shall prove (
A.7
) via induction. Note that

\displaystyle h_{1}(x;(u_{v})_{v\in\partial\{w_{1}\}})=\prod_{e=\{v_{1}\to v_{2}\}\in E_{1}\setminus\{e_{1}\}}W_{e}(u_{v_{1}}-u_{v_{2}})

is the product of edge weights without any integration and with the convention
u_{w_{1}}=x
. Applying (
A.6
) to each such weight function yields (
A.7
) for
r=1
.
Observe the recursion relation for
h_{r}
:

\displaystyle h_{r+1}\big(x;(u_{v})_{v\in\partial\Lambda_{r+1}}\big)=d_{r}\big(x;(u_{v})_{v\in\partial\Lambda_{r+1}}\big)\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}}h_{r}\big(y;(u_{v})_{v\in\partial\Lambda_{r}}\big)F_{r}(x-y)dy

where by convention we set
u_{w_{r+1}}=x
and where we define

\displaystyle d_{r}(x;(u_{v})_{v\in\partial\Lambda_{r+1}})=\prod_{e=\{v_{1}\to v_{2}\}\in Q_{r}}W_{e}(u_{v_{1}}-u_{v_{2}})

with
Q_{r}
being the set of all red and black edges that have one vertex as
w_{r+1}
and another vertex in
\partial\Lambda_{r+1}
, see Figure
19
(B). Note that the blue edge
e_{r+1}
between
w_{r+1}
and
w_{r+1}+(0,1)
is excluded from
Q_{r}
. Appealing to (
A.6
) again, we have

(A.8)

\displaystyle d_{r}(s;(u_{v})_{v\in\partial\Lambda_{r+1}})d_{r}(t;(u_{v}^{\prime})_{v\in\partial\Lambda_{r+1}})\leq d_{r}(s;(u_{v}^{\prime})_{v\in\partial\Lambda_{r+1}})d_{r}(t;(u_{v})_{v\in\partial\Lambda_{r+1}})

for all
s\geq t
and for all
u_{v}^{\prime}\geq u_{v}
with
v\in\partial\Lambda_{r+1}
. Under same conditions we claim that

(A.9)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}h_{r}(y;(u_{v})_{v\in\partial\Lambda_{r}})F_{r}(s-y)h_{r}(x;(u_{v}^{\prime})_{v\in\partial\Lambda_{r}})F_{r}(t-x)dxdy

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}h_{r}(y;(u_{v}^{\prime})_{v\in\partial\Lambda_{r}})F_{r}(s-y)h_{r}(x;(u_{v})_{v\in\partial\Lambda_{r}})F_{r}(t-x)dxdy.

Combining the above inequality with (
A.8
) we have (
A.7
) completing the proof. To see why (
A.9
) holds, we split the integrals in (
A.9
) over
\{x<y\}
and
\{y<x\}
and swap the
x,y
labels in the region
\{y<x\}
to get that (
A.9
) is equivalent to

\int_{x<y}A(y)Y(y)B(x)X(x)+C(x)Z(x)D(y)W(y)\leq\int_{x<y}D(y)Y(y)C(x)X(x)+B(x)Z(x)A(y)Z(y)

where we let

A(y)=h_{r}(y;(u_{v})_{v\in\partial\Lambda_{r}}),B(x)=h_{r}(x;(u_{v}^{\prime})_{v\in\partial\Lambda_{r}}),C(x)=h_{r}(x;(u_{v})_{v\in\partial\Lambda_{r}}),D(y)=h_{r}(y;(u_{v}^{\prime})_{v\in\partial\Lambda_{r}}),

X(x)=F_{r}(t-x),Y(y)=F_{r}(s-y),W(y)=F_{r}(t-y),Z(x)=F_{r}(s-x).

The integral above can be rewritten as
\int_{x<y}\big(A(y)B(x)-C(x)D(y)\big)\big(X(x)Y(y)-W(y)Z(x)\big)
and thus it suffices to show for each
x\leq y
the integrand is non-positive.
By induction hypothesis,
A(y)B(x)\leq C(x)D(y)
for all
x\leq y
and since the weight function
F_{r}
satisfies (
A.6
) (with
P=F_{r}
), we also have
X(x)Y(y)\geq W(y)Z(x)
. This proves (
A.9
), completing the proof of the lemma.
∎

Appendix B
Basic properties of log-gamma type random variables

In this section we collect some basic facts about log-gamma type random variables. Towards this end, for each
\theta,\kappa>0
, and
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
we consider the following function:

\displaystyle H_{\theta,(-1)^{m},\kappa}(y):=\frac{\kappa^{\theta}}{\Gamma(\theta)}\exp(\theta(-1)^{m}y-\kappa e^{(-1)^{m}y}).

It is plain to check
H
is a valid probability density function. Observe that
H_{\theta,(-1)^{m},1}\equiv G_{\theta,(-1)^{m}}
where
G
is defined in (
2.2
). The following lemma collects some useful properties of
H
. Its proof follows via straightforward computations and is hence omitted.

Lemma B.1
.

Suppose
X\sim H_{\theta,1,\kappa}
. Then
-X\sim H_{\theta,-1,\kappa}
. For all
\alpha>-\theta
we have
\mathbb{E}[e^{\alpha X}]=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+\theta)}{\kappa^{\alpha}\Gamma(\theta)}
.

We next define generalized
\mathpzc{HSLG}

\Theta
-Gibbs measures in the same vein as
\mathpzc{HSLG}

\Theta
-Gibbs measures (see Definition
1.2
) but by considering the weight function

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}_{e}(x)=\begin{cases}\exp(\vartheta x-\kappa e^{x})&\mbox{ if $e$ is {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{blue}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}($\vartheta$)}}\\
\exp(-\gamma e^{x})&\mbox{ if $e$ is {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{black}}}\\
\exp(-\alpha x)&\mbox{ if $e$ is {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{red}}.}\end{cases}

instead of
W
defined in (
1.5
).
\kappa=\gamma=1
in above weights lead to the usual Gibbs measures. The following result ensures that generalized
\mathpzc{HSLG}

\Theta
-Gibbs measures (and hence the usual ones from Definition
1.2
) are well-defined.

Lemma B.2
.

Fix any
\gamma,\kappa>0,

\Theta:=\{\vartheta_{m,n}>0:(m,n)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{2}\}
and
\alpha>-\min\{\vartheta_{m,n}:(m,n)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{2}\}
. Recall the graph
G
from Section
1.3.1
used in defining
\mathpzc{HSLG}

\Theta
-Gibbs measures. Given a domain
\Lambda
and a boundary condition
\{u_{i,j}:(i,j)\in\partial\Lambda\}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{|\Lambda|}}\prod_{e=\{v_{1}\to v_{2}\}\in E(\Lambda\cup\partial\Lambda)}\widetilde{W}_{e}(u_{v_{1}}-u_{v_{2}})\prod_{v\in\Lambda}du_{v}<\infty.

Let us suppose
|u_{i,j}|\leq R
for all
(i,j)\in\partial\Lambda
. Let us assume
\Lambda=\mathcal{K}_{k,T}
or
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{k,T}
defined in (
2.4
). There exists a constant
\mathrm{C}
that depends only on
\gamma,\kappa,\theta,
and
\alpha
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{|\Lambda|}}\prod_{e=\{v_{1}\to v_{2}\}\in E(\Lambda\cup\partial\Lambda)}\widetilde{W}_{e}(u_{v_{1}}-u_{v_{2}})\prod_{v\in\Lambda}du_{v}\leq\mathrm{C}^{kT+R}.

Proof.

We shall prove this lemma only for the homogeneous case, i.e.
\vartheta_{m,n}\equiv\theta>0
. The general case is notationally more cumbersome but follows in an exact same manner as the homogeneous case. First note that for red edges
\{v_{1}\to v_{2}\}
the corresponding weight function
W_{e}(u_{v_{1}}-u_{v_{2}})
factors out as
e^{-\alpha u_{v_{1}}}\cdot e^{\alpha u_{v_{2}}}
. Hence they can be viewed as vertex weight functions. More specifically, at each vertex
(k,1)
we can associate the vertex weight function
V_{k}(u):=e^{(-1)^{k}\alpha u}
. They replace the role of red edge weights. We denote this vertex weights as red circles in Figure
20
. We now divide our analysis into two cases based on the value of
\alpha
.

(a)

(b)

(c)

u_{0}
u_{k}
u_{0}
u_{k}

(d)

u_{k}

(e)

c
u

(f)

Figure 20
.
(A) A possible domain
\Lambda
. (B) Reduction in the case of
\alpha\in(-\theta,\theta)
. (C) Reduction in the case of
\alpha>0
. (D) Type I Gibbs measures. The figure shows two of them of even length. It may also have odd length with one edge at either of the end removed. (E) Type II Gibbs measures. It may also have odd length with one edge at right end removed. (F) Few examples of Type III Gibbs measures.

Suppose
\alpha\in(-\theta,\theta)
. As black edge weights are less than
1
, we may drop all of them to get a Gibbs measure based on the blue and red edge weights only, see Figure
20
(B). The integral of the reduced Gibbs measure can be viewed as a product of integrals of several smaller Gibbs measures that are two types: Type I and Type II as in Figure
20
(D) and (E) respectively. Type I Gibbs measures are those for which red vertex weights do not appear. The integral corresponding to Type I takes the following form:

\displaystyle\left(\kappa^{\theta}(\Gamma(\theta))^{-1}\right)^{k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k-1}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}H_{\theta,\kappa,(-1)^{i+m}}(u_{i-1}-u_{i})\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}du_{i}

where
u_{0}
and
u_{k}
are in
\partial\Lambda
. In this case, we may use
H_{\theta,\kappa,(-1)^{i+m}}(u_{k-1}-u_{k})\leq\mathrm{C}
and the fact that
H
is a probability density function to get that the integral is bounded by
\mathrm{C}\cdot\left(\kappa^{\theta}(\Gamma(\theta))^{-1}\right)^{k}
. Type II Gibbs measures are the ones where red vertex weights are present. The integral corresponding to the Type II Gibbs measures takes the form

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}e^{(-1)^{m}\alpha u_{0}}\cdot e^{(-1)^{i+m}\theta(u_{i-1}-u_{i})-\kappa e^{(-1)^{i+m}(u_{i-1}-u_{i})}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}du_{i}.

The integrand can be manipulated to show that the above integral is equal to

\displaystyle e^{(-1)^{m+k-1}\alpha u_{k}}\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}(\Gamma(\theta+(-1)^{m+i+1}\alpha))\kappa^{-\theta+(-1)^{m+i}\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}H_{\theta+(-1)^{m+i+1}\alpha,\kappa,(-1)^{i-1}}(x_{i})\prod_{i=1}^{k}dx_{i}

\displaystyle=e^{(-1)^{m+k-1}\alpha u_{k}}\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}(\Gamma(\theta+(-1)^{m+i+1}\alpha))\kappa^{-\theta+(-1)^{m+i}\alpha}.

Since we factor out the measure into these independent pieces and all integrals are finite, the claim follows for
\alpha\in(-\theta,\theta)
.

For
\alpha>0
, we remove all the black edges except the ones connecting
(2i-1,1)
to
(2i,1)
(again since weights of black edges are atmost
1
, removal of them only increases the integrand). This leads to a reduced Gibbs measures shown in Figure
20
(C). The reduced Gibbs measure decomposes into several Type I Gibbs measures and Type III Gibbs measures. Type III Gibbs measures are those for which red vertex weights do appear. Because of the presence of black edge in this case, Type III Gibbs measures are different from Type II. A few of the possible Type III Gibbs measures are shown in Figure
20
(F).

•

If a Type III Gibbs measure has two red vertices in its domain or boundary, we may use the fact that the weight of the figure

a
b
c

is
e^{(\theta+\alpha)(b-c)-\kappa e^{b-c}}\cdot e^{\alpha(c-a)-\gamma e^{c-a}}\leq e^{(\theta+\alpha)(b-c)-\kappa e^{b-c}}\cdot\left(\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}e^{\alpha x-\gamma e^{x}}\right)\leq\mathrm{C}\cdot e^{(\theta+\alpha)(b-c)-\kappa e^{b-c}}
.

•

If a Type III Gibbs measure has only one red vertex in its domain or boundary, then it must contain either of the two following figures

b
c
a
c

with
c\in\partial\Lambda
. The corresponding weights are
e^{\alpha c}\cdot e^{(\theta+\alpha)(b-c)-\kappa e^{b-c}}\leq\mathrm{C}^{\prime}e^{\alpha c}
and
e^{-\alpha c}\cdot e^{\alpha(c-a)-\gamma e^{c-a}}\leq\mathrm{C}^{\prime}e^{-\alpha c}
respectively where
\mathrm{C}^{\prime}:=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}e^{\alpha x-\gamma e^{x}}
.

Based on the kind of Type III Gibbs measures, we may insert the above bounds on the Gibbs weights in the integrand of this type of Gibbs measures. The resulting integral can then be computed explicitly to yield a bound of the form
\mathrm{C}^{V}e^{|\alpha c|}
where
V
is the number of vertices in the Gibbs measures. For example, for the middle figure in Figure
20
(F) we have (with
u_{4}:=u
)

\displaystyle\left(\kappa^{-\theta}\Gamma(\theta)\right)^{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}e^{-\alpha u_{0}}e^{-\gamma e^{c-u_{0}}}\prod_{i=0}^{3}H_{\theta,\kappa,(-1)^{i}}(u_{i}-u_{i+1})du_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\kappa^{-\theta}\Gamma(\theta)\right)^{4}\cdot\mathrm{C}e^{-\alpha c}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\prod_{i=0}^{3}H_{\theta,\kappa,(-1)^{i}}(u_{i}-u_{i+1})du_{i}\leq\left(\kappa^{-\theta}\Gamma(\theta)\right)^{4}\cdot\mathrm{C}e^{|\alpha c|}.

This establishes the lemma for
\alpha>0
. ∎

We end this section we two lemmas concerning with the tail properties of
\mathsf{f}_{\theta}
and
\xi
-distributions defined in (
2.3
) and (
4.5
) respectively.

Lemma B.3
.

For all
x\in\mathbb{R}
we have

e^{-2e}\leq(\Gamma(\theta))^{2}\mathsf{f}_{\theta}(x)e^{\theta|x|}\leq\Gamma(2\theta),

where
\mathsf{f}_{\theta}
is defined in (
2.3
).

Proof.

Since
\mathsf{f}_{\theta}
is symmetric, it suffices to show the lemma for
x>0
. We have

\displaystyle\big(\Gamma(\theta)\big)^{2}\mathsf{f}_{\theta}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{\theta y-e^{y}+\theta(y-x)-e^{y-x}}dy=e^{-\theta x}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{2\theta y-e^{y}-e^{y-x}}dy.

Now for the lower bound we observe

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{2\theta y-e^{y}-e^{y-x}}dy\geq\int_{0}^{1}e^{2\theta y-e^{y}-e^{y-x}}dy\geq e^{-2e},

whereas for the upper bound we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{2\theta y-e^{y}-e^{y-x}}dy\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{2\theta y-e^{y}}dy=\Gamma(2\theta).

∎

Lemma B.4
.

Fix any
\theta_{0}>1
. For any
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in[\theta_{0}^{-1},\theta_{0}]
and
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, define the random variable
X_{\theta_{1},\theta_{2};\pm 1}^{(a,b)}\sim\xi_{\theta_{1},\theta_{2};\pm 1}^{(a,b)}
where
\xi_{\theta_{1},\theta_{2};\pm 1}^{(a,b)}
is defined in (
4.5
). There exists a constant
\mathrm{C}>0
depending only
\theta_{0}
such that for all
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in[\theta_{0}^{-1},\theta_{0}]
, for all
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
and for all
r\geq|a-b|
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(X_{\theta_{1},\theta_{2};\pm 1}^{(a,b)}\notin\big[\min\{a,b\}-2r,\max\{a,b\}+2r]\Big)\leq\mathrm{C}e^{-\frac{1}{\mathrm{C}}r}.

Proof.

Fix any
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in[\theta_{0}^{-1},\theta_{0}]
. We shall prove the bound for
X_{\theta_{1},\theta_{2};1}^{(a,b)}
. The proof for the case
X_{\theta_{1},\theta_{2};-1}^{(a,b)}
is analogous. Without loss of generality assume
b\leq a
. Observe that

(B.1)

\mathbb{P}\Big(X_{\theta_{1},\theta_{2};\pm 1}^{(a,b)}\notin\big[\min\{a,b\}-2r,\max\{a,b\}+2r\big]\Big)\leq\frac{\int_{(-\infty,b-2r]\cup[a+2r,\infty)}G_{\theta_{1},1}(a-x)G_{\theta_{2},1}(b-x)}{\int_{a}^{a+1}G_{\theta_{1},1}(a-x)G_{\theta_{2},1}(b-x)}.

Note that

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{a+1}G_{\theta_{1},1}(a-x)G_{\theta_{2},1}(b-x)\geq\tfrac{1}{\mathrm{C}}\cdot e^{-\max\{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\}\cdot(a-b)},

where we have used the fact that
G_{\beta,1}(-y)\geq\mathrm{C}^{-1}e^{-\beta y}
(recall
G
from (
2.2
)). Similarly

\displaystyle\int_{x\leq b-2r}G_{\theta_{1},1}(a-x)G_{\theta_{2},1}(b-x)dx+\int_{x\geq a+2r}G_{\theta_{1},1}(a-x)G_{\theta_{2},1}(b-x)dx\leq\mathrm{C}\cdot e^{-2(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2})r}.

Thus as long as
r\geq a-b
, inserting the above two bounds back in (
B.1
) and adjusting the constant
\mathrm{C}
we get the desired result.
∎

Appendix C
Estimates for non-intersection probability

In this section, we study non-intersection probability of random walks and random bridges (defined in Definition
3.6
), and modified random bridges (defined in Definition
4.9
). Throughout this section we shall assume the increments are drawn from a density
\mathsf{f}
that satisfies the following assumptions. It is worth recalling that due to Corollary
4.6
,
\mathsf{f}_{\theta}
defined in (
2.3
) satisfies the conditions of Assumption
C.1
and hence all results of this section can be applied to random walks with that increment law.

Assumption C.1

(Assumption on the increments)
.

The density
\mathsf{f}
satisfies the following properties.

(1)

The density
\mathsf{f}
is symmetric and
\log\mathsf{f}
is concave.

(2)

Let
\psi
denote the characteristic function corresponding to
\mathsf{f}
.
|\psi|
is integrable. Given any
\delta>0
, there exists
\eta
such that
\sup_{t\geq\delta}|\psi(t)|=\eta<1
.

(3)

There exists a constant
\mathrm{C}>0
such that
\mathsf{f}(x)\leq\mathrm{C}e^{-|x|/\mathrm{C}}
. In particular, this implies that if
X\sim\mathsf{f}
, there exists
v>0
such that and

\displaystyle\sup_{|t|\leq v}\big[\mathbb{E}[e^{tX}]\big]<\infty.

In other words
X
is an subexponential random variable.

The following lemma concerns with sharp rate of convergence of the probability density function of
(X(1)+X(2)+\cdots+X(n))/\sqrt{n}
, where
X(i)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\mathsf{f}
, to the Gaussian density with appropriate variance.

Lemma C.2
.

Let
\mathsf{f}^{*n}
be the
n
-fold convolution of
\mathsf{f}
. There exists a constant
\mathrm{C}>0
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{|x|\leq(\log n)^{2}}\left|\frac{\sqrt{n}\mathsf{f}^{*n}(x\sqrt{n})}{\phi_{\sigma}(x)}-1\right|\leq\mathrm{C}\cdot n^{-3/4}.

where
\phi_{\sigma}(x):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}}
and
\sigma^{2}:=\int x^{2}\mathsf{f}(x)dx
.

Proof.

This proof is adapted from Theorem 5 in Chapter XV in
[
Fel08
]
. In what follows we shall use the big
O
-notation and write
a_{n}=O(b_{n})
if
a_{n}/b_{n}
is uniformly bounded above by some universal constant. Let
\psi
denote the characteristic function of
\mathsf{f}_{\theta}
. The explicit form of
\psi
was given in (
4.13
). In particular,
|\psi|
is integrable. In what follows, for simplicity we will assume
\sigma^{2}=1
. Set
f_{n}(x):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{itx}\psi^{n}(t/\sqrt{n})dt
. By the Fourier inversion formula,
f_{n}(x)
is the density of
(X(1)+X(2)+\cdots+X(n))/\sqrt{n}
where
X(i)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\mathsf{f}
. Hence we have
\sqrt{n}\mathsf{f}^{*n}(x/\sqrt{n})=f_{n}(x)
. Since
\mathsf{f}
is symmetric and has all finite moments, by Taylor expansion we have

\displaystyle\psi(t/\sqrt{n})=1-\tfrac{t^{2}}{2n}+O(\tfrac{t^{4}}{n^{2}}).

Set
\alpha=1/16
. Thus for
|t|\leq n^{\alpha}
, we have
\psi(t/\sqrt{n})=1-\frac{t^{2}}{2n}+O(n^{4\alpha-2})=e^{-t^{2}/2n+O(n^{4\alpha-2})}
. Thus
\psi^{n}(t/\sqrt{n})=e^{-t^{2}/2}(1+O(n^{-3/4}))
, where the
O
term is free of
t
in that specified range. Thus,

\displaystyle f_{n}(x)

\displaystyle=(1+O(n^{-3/4}))\int_{|t|\leq n^{\alpha}}e^{itx}e^{-t^{2}/2}dt+\int_{|t|\geq n^{\alpha}}e^{itx}\psi^{n}(t/\sqrt{n})dt

\displaystyle=(1+O(n^{-3/4}))\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{itx}e^{-t^{2}/2}dt+\int_{|t|\geq n^{\alpha}}e^{itx}\psi^{n}(t/\sqrt{n})dt-(1+O(n^{-3/4}))\int_{|t|\geq n^{\alpha}}e^{itx}e^{-t^{2}/2}dt.

We next compute the order of the last two integrals above. Clearly
\int_{|t|\geq n^{\alpha}}e^{-t^{2}/2}dt\leq\mathrm{C}e^{-cn^{2\alpha}}
. For the second one, we choose
\delta>0
small enough such that
|\psi(t)|\leq e^{-t^{2}/4}
for all
|t|\leq\delta
. This implies

\displaystyle\int_{n^{\alpha}\leq t\leq\sqrt{n}\delta}|\psi^{n}(t/\sqrt{n})|dt\leq\mathrm{C}e^{-cn^{2\alpha}}.

For
|t|\geq\sqrt{n}\delta
, we know
\sup_{t\geq\delta}|\psi(t)|=\eta<1
by part (2) of Lemma
4.7
. This forces

\displaystyle\int_{|t|\geq\sqrt{n}\delta}|\psi^{n}(t/\sqrt{n})|dt\leq\eta^{n-1}\sqrt{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi(t)dt.

Thus the error integrals are at most
\mathrm{C}e^{-\frac{1}{\mathrm{C}}n^{1/8}}
in absolute value uniform in
x
. Furthermore if we assume
|x|\leq(\log n)^{2}
,
\phi_{1}(x)\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-(\log n)^{2}/2}
, which dominates the error coming from the integrals. Hence we may divide
\phi_{1}(x)
and still obtain that the errors are going to zero.
∎

For any
p\in[0,\infty)
,
s,t\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
, we set

\displaystyle\mathsf{NI}_{p}\llbracket s,t\rrbracket:=\{S_{1}(k)-S_{2}(k)\geq-p,\mbox{ for all }k\in\llbracket s,t\rrbracket\}.

When
s=2,t=n-1
we write
\mathsf{NI}_{p}:=\mathsf{NI}_{p}\llbracket 2,n-1\rrbracket
so that it coincides with the
\mathsf{NI}_{p}
event defined in (
1.18
). When
a_{1}-a_{2}=O(1)
, it is well known that
\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}(\mathsf{NI})=O(n^{-1/2})
. We record this classical fact in the following lemma.

Lemma C.3
.

For all
(a_{1},a_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
we have
\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}(\mathsf{NI})\leq\mathrm{C}\frac{\max\{a_{1}-a_{2},1\}}{\sqrt{n}}
for some absolute constant
\mathrm{C}>0
. If in addition
a_{1}\geq a_{2}
, we have
\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}(\mathsf{NI})\geq\frac{\mathrm{C}^{-1}}{\sqrt{n}}
.

Proof.

The first part is
[
Koz77
, Theorem A]
and the second part is
[
Spi60
, Theorem 3.5]
.
∎

We again remind the readers that the above result, as well as all the results stated below within this section, the random walks/bridges or the modified random bridges are assumed to have increments drawn from a density
\mathsf{f}
satisfying Assumption
C.1
. In many of our arguments below, we shall often appeal to stochastic monotonicity of non-intersecting random walks or bridges with respect to boundary data. We record this result below.

Proposition C.4

(Stochastic monotonicity of random bridges and random walks)
.

Fix
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.
et
a_{i}^{(j)},b_{i}^{(j)}\in[-\infty,\infty]
for
i,j\in\{1,2\}
. Suppose
a_{i}^{(1)}\geq a_{i}^{(2)}
and
b_{i}^{(1)}\geq b_{i}^{(2)}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
.

(a)

There exists a probability space that supports a collection of random variables

\displaystyle\big(S_{1}^{(j)}(k),S_{2}^{(j)}(k):j\in\{1,2\},k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\big)

such that
S_{i}^{(1)}(k)\geq S_{i}^{(2)}(k)
for all
i\in\{1,2\}
and
k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
, and marginally
(S_{1}^{(j)}(\cdot),S_{2}^{(j)}(\cdot))\sim\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1}^{(j)},a_{2}^{(j)}),(b_{1}^{(j)},b_{2}^{(j)})}(\cdot\mid\mathsf{NI})
for each
j\in\{1,2\}
.

(b)

There exists a probability space that supports a collection of random variables

\displaystyle\big(S_{1}^{(j)}(k),S_{2}^{(j)}(k):j\in\{1,2\},k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\big)

such that
S_{i}^{(1)}(k)\geq S_{i}^{(2)}(k)
for all
i\in\{1,2\}
and
k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
, and marginally
(S_{1}^{(j)}(\cdot),S_{2}^{(j)}(\cdot))\sim\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1}^{(j)},a_{2}^{(j)})}(\cdot\mid\mathsf{NI})
for each
j\in\{1,2\}
.

Proof.

This proposition of as a discrete analogue of Lemma 2.6 in
[
CH14
]
and is true under log-concavity assumption on
\mathsf{f}
. Instead of giving the full details, we explain the two possible ways in proving this proposition. One is via Markov Chain arguments as done, for example, in
[
CH14
,
Wu20
,
Ser23
]
previously. In
[
Ser23
]
for example, the stochastic monotonicity was proved for non-intersecting random bridges under discrete bounded increment assumption. One can take a discrete to continuous limit of the increments to obtain the above result. The second route is via direct construction argument is the style of
[
BCD23
,
DW21b
]
. We have, in fact, already adapted that technique in proving stochastic monotonicity for
\mathpzc{HSLG}
Gibbs measures in Appendix
A
and a similar argument can be carried out to prove Proposition
C.4
.
∎

Next we study diffusive properties of the random walks under the non-intersecting event.

Lemma C.5
.

Given any
\varepsilon>0
there exists a constant
\delta(\varepsilon)>0
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
(a_{1},a_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
we have

(C.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}\bigg(S_{1}(n)-S_{2}(n)\geq\delta\sqrt{n}\mid\mathsf{NI}\bigg)\geq 1-\varepsilon,

(C.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}\bigg(\sup_{k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket}(S_{1}(k)-S_{2}(k))\leq\delta^{-1}\sqrt{n}+\max\{a_{1}-a_{2},0\}\mid\mathsf{NI}\bigg)\geq 1-\varepsilon,

(C.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}\bigg(\inf_{k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket}S_{1}(k)-a_{1}\geq-\delta^{-1}\sqrt{n}\mid\mathsf{NI}\bigg)\geq 1-\varepsilon,

(C.4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}\bigg(\sup_{k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket}S_{2}(k)-a_{2}\leq\delta^{-1}\sqrt{n}\mid\mathsf{NI}\bigg)\geq 1-\varepsilon.

We remark that Lemma
C.5
holds if
\mathsf{NI}=\mathsf{NI}_{0}\llbracket 2,n-1\rrbracket
is replaced by
\mathsf{NI}_{0}\llbracket 2,n\rrbracket
. The same argument presented below essentially works when the conditional event is the latter one instead.

Proof.

Proof of Eq. (
C.1
).
Set
U(k):=S_{1}(k)-S_{2}(k)
. Under
\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}
,
(U(k))_{k=1}^{n}
is a random walk starting from
a_{1}-a_{2}
with increments drawn from
\mathsf{f}\ast\mathsf{f}
. The non-intersection condition for
(S_{1}(k),S_{2}(k))_{k=1}^{n}
translates to
(U(k))_{k=1}^{n}
staying non-negative. If
a_{1}\geq a_{2}
, since
\{U(n)\geq\delta\sqrt{n}\}
is an increasing event with respect to the boundary conditions, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}\bigg(S_{1}(n)-S_{2}(n)\geq\delta\sqrt{n}\mid\mathsf{NI}\bigg)\geq\mathbb{P}^{n;(0,0)}\bigg(S_{1}(n)-S_{2}(n)\geq\delta\sqrt{n}\mid\mathsf{NI}\bigg)

But under
\mathbb{P}^{n;(0,0)}
, it is known from
[
Igl74
]
that the random walk
(U(k))_{k=1}^{n}
, conditioned to stay non-negative converges weakly to a Brownian meander under diffusive scaling. Since the endpoint of a Brownian meander is a strictly positive continuous random variable, we thus have (
C.1
). If
a_{1}\leq a_{2}
, the argument is a bit more involved. We first write the (complement of the) conditional probability as a ratio:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}\big(S_{1}(n)-S_{2}(n)\leq\delta\sqrt{n}\mid\mathsf{NI}\big)=\frac{\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}\big(\big\{S_{1}(n)-S_{2}(n)\leq\delta\sqrt{n}\big\}\cap\mathsf{NI}\big)}{\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}\big(\mathsf{NI}\big)}.

For the denominator we condition on
(S_{1}(2),S_{2}(2))
and use the lower bound from Lemma
C.3
:

(C.5)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}(\mathsf{NI})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}\left[\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(2)\geq S_{2}(2)}\mathbb{E}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{NI}_{0}\llbracket 3,n-1\rrbracket}\mid\sigma(S_{1}(2),S_{2}(2))\right]\right]

(C.6)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\mathrm{C}^{-1}}{\sqrt{n}}\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}({S_{1}(2)\geq S_{2}(2)}).

For the numerator, we again condition on
(S_{1}(2),S_{2}(2))
to get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}\big(\big\{S_{1}(n)-S_{2}(n)\leq\delta\sqrt{n}\big\}\cap\mathsf{NI}\big)

(C.7)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(2)\geq S_{2}(2)}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\{S_{1}(n)-S_{2}(n)\leq\delta\sqrt{n}\}\cap\mathsf{NI}_{0}\llbracket 3,n-1\rrbracket}\mid\sigma(S_{1}(2),S_{2}(2))\right]\right].

Upon conditioning on
(S_{1}(2),S_{2}(2))
, the random walks starts at
(S_{1}(2),S_{2}(2))
. For any
b_{1}\geq b_{2}
, utilizing the upper bound from Lemma
C.3
and stochastic monotonicity we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n-1;(b_{1},b_{2})}\left(\{S_{1}(n-1)-S_{2}(n-1)\leq\delta\sqrt{n}\}\cap\mathsf{NI}_{0}\llbracket 2,n-2\rrbracket\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}^{n-1;(b_{1},b_{2})}(\mathsf{NI}_{0}\llbracket 2,n-2\rrbracket)\mathbb{P}^{n-1;(b_{1},b_{2})}\left(\{S_{1}(n-1)-S_{2}(n-1)\leq\delta\sqrt{n}\}\mid\mathsf{NI}_{0}\llbracket 2,n-2\rrbracket\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathrm{C}}{\sqrt{n}}\max\{b_{1}-b_{2},1\}\cdot\mathbb{P}^{n-1;(0,0)}\left({\{S_{1}(n-1)-S_{2}(n-1)\leq\delta\sqrt{n}\}\mid\mathsf{NI}_{0}\llbracket 2,n-2\rrbracket}\right)

Taking
b_{1}=S_{1}(2)
and
b_{2}=S_{2}(2)
, we insert the above bound back in (
C.7
) to get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}\big(\big\{S_{1}(n)-S_{2}(n)\leq\delta\sqrt{n}\big\}\cap\mathsf{NI}\big)

(C.8)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\frac{\mathrm{C}}{\sqrt{n}}\mathbb{E}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}\left[\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(2)\geq S_{2}(2)}\max\{S_{1}(2)-S_{2}(2),1\}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\cdot\mathbb{P}^{n-1;(0,0)}\left({\{S_{1}(n-1)-S_{2}(n-1)\leq\delta\sqrt{n}\}\mid\mathsf{NI}_{0}\llbracket 2,n-2\rrbracket}\right)

Note that under
\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}
,
S_{1}(2)-S_{2}(2)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}Z-b
where
Z\sim\mathsf{f}\ast\mathsf{f}
and
b=a_{2}-a_{1}\geq 0
. We claim that there exists a constant
\mathrm{C}>0
such that for all
b\geq 0
we have

(C.9)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}\left[\max\{Z-b,1\}\mathbf{1}_{Z\geq b}\right]}\leq\mathrm{C}\cdot\mathbb{P}(Z\geq b).

Plugging this bound in the expectation in (
C.8
) we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}\big(\big\{S_{1}(n)-S_{2}(n)\leq\delta\sqrt{n}\big\}\cap\mathsf{NI}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathrm{C}}{\sqrt{n}}\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}({S_{1}(2)\geq S_{2}(2)})\!\cdot\!\mathbb{P}^{n-1;(0,0)}\left({\{S_{1}(n-1)-S_{2}(n-1)\leq\delta\sqrt{n}\}\mid\mathsf{NI}_{0}\llbracket 2,n-2\rrbracket}\right)

Combining this with the lower bound on the denominator from (
C.6
) we get that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}\big(S_{1}(n)-S_{2}(n)\leq\delta\sqrt{n}\mid\mathsf{NI}\big)\leq\mathrm{C}\!\cdot\!\mathbb{P}^{n-1;(0,0)}\left({\{S_{1}(n-1)-S_{2}(n-1)\leq\delta\sqrt{n}\}\mid\mathsf{NI}_{0}\llbracket 2,n-2\rrbracket}\right)\!.\!

From here, we can again appeal to
[
Igl74
]
and Brownian meander properties to show that the above bound can be made arbitrarily small by choosing
\delta
small enough. Thus we are left to (
C.9
).

Suppose
Z\sim\mathsf{f}\ast\mathsf{f}
. Observe that for any
b\in\mathbb{R}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\max\{Z-b,1\}\mathbf{1}_{Z\geq b}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\max\{Z-b,1\}\mathbf{1}_{Z\in[b+k-1,b+k]}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(Z\geq b)\left[1+\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}k\cdot\frac{\mathbb{P}(Z\geq b+k-1)}{\mathbb{P}(Z\geq b)}\right]

If we assume
b\geq 0
additionally, using exponential tail bounds for
\mathsf{f}
(and hence
\mathsf{f}\ast\mathsf{f}
), we may get a constant
\mathrm{C}>0
free of
b
, such that
\frac{\mathbb{P}(Z\geq b+k-1)}{\mathbb{P}(Z\geq b)}\leq\mathrm{C}e^{-k/\mathrm{C}}
for all
k\geq 2
. This ensure the infinite sum above can be bounded uniformly over
b\in[0,\infty)
. This proves (
C.9
).

Proof of Eq. (
C.2
).
Set
U(k):=S_{1}(k)-S_{2}(k)
. To obtain (
C.2
), observe the following inequalities

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}\bigg(\sup_{k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket}U(k)\leq\delta^{-1}\sqrt{n}+\max\{a_{1}-a_{2},0\}\mid\bigcap_{k=2}^{n}\{U(k)\geq 0\}\bigg)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}^{n;(\max\{a_{1},a_{2}\},a_{2})}\bigg(\sup_{k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket}U(k)\leq\delta^{-1}\sqrt{n}+\max\{a_{1}-a_{2},0\}\mid\bigcap_{k=2}^{n}\{U(k)\geq 0\}\bigg)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}^{n;(\max\{a_{1},a_{2}\},a_{2})}\bigg(\sup_{k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket}U(k)\leq\delta^{-1}\sqrt{n}+\max\{a_{1}-a_{2},0\}\mid\bigcap_{k=2}^{n}\{U(k)\geq\max\{a_{1}-a_{2},0\}\}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{2},a_{2})}\bigg(\sup_{k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket}U(k)\leq\delta^{-1}\sqrt{n}\mid\bigcap_{k=2}^{n}\{U(k)\geq 0\}\bigg)\geq 1-\varepsilon.

Let us briefly explain the above inequalities that imply (
C.2
). The first inequality follows from stochastic monotonicity applied to the boundary point. We are conditioning on the event that requires the random walk
(U(k))_{k=1}^{n}
to stay above the barrier zero. By stochastic monotonicity, increasing this barrier will only decrease the conditional probability. This implies the second inequality. The equality in the last line follows by translating the random walk. The final inequality follows by taking
\delta
small enough due to the tightness of the random walk paths conditioned to stay positive (when scaled by diffusively)
[
Igl74
]
.

Proof of Eq. (
C.3
) and Eq. (
C.4
)
Note that due to stochastic monotonicity (Proposition
C.4
), taking
a_{2}\downarrow-\infty
we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}\bigg(\inf_{k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket}S_{1}(k)-a_{1}\geq-\delta^{-1}\sqrt{n}\mid\mathsf{NI}\bigg)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},-\infty)}\bigg(\inf_{k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket}S_{1}(k)-a_{1}\geq-\delta^{-1}\sqrt{n}\mid\mathsf{NI}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},-\infty)}\bigg(\inf_{k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket}S_{1}(k)-a_{1}\geq-\delta^{-1}\sqrt{n}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}\bigg(\inf_{k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket}S_{1}(k)-a_{1}\geq-\delta^{-1}\sqrt{n}\bigg).

The first equality above is due to the fact that
\mathsf{NI}
happens almost surely when the second walk starts at
-\infty
. The second equality follows from noting that
S_{1}(\cdot)
and
S_{2}(\cdot)
are independent and hence the probability is independent of the starting point of the second walk. Thus the non-intersecting condition makes
S_{1}(\cdot)
stochastically larger than a usual random walk. By diffusive behavior of random walks one can choose
\delta
small enough so that the above quantity is at least
1-\varepsilon
. Similarly the non-intersecting condition makes
S_{2}(\cdot)
stochastically smaller than a usual random walk. Combining this with the diffusive behavior of random walks leads to (
C.4
).
∎

Corollary C.6
.

Fix any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
. Suppose
a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
with
|a_{1}-a_{2}|\leq n/\log n
. Given any
\varepsilon,\gamma>0
there exists a constant
\rho(\varepsilon,\gamma)\in(0,\frac{1}{4}]
such that for all large enough
n
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}\bigg(\sup_{k\in\llbracket 1,n\rho\rrbracket,i=1,2}\big|S_{i}(k)-a_{i}\big|\geq\gamma\sqrt{n}\mid\mathsf{NI}\bigg)\geq 1-\varepsilon.

Proof.

Let us focus only on
S_{1}(\cdot)
. We may control lower drift of
S_{1}(\cdot)
around
a_{1}
, i.e.,
\inf_{k\in\llbracket 1,n\rho\rrbracket}[S_{1}(k)-a_{1}]
by an argument similar to the proof of (
C.3
). For upper drift we use

\displaystyle\sup_{k\in\llbracket 1,n\rho\rrbracket}[S_{1}(k)-a_{1}]\leq a_{2}-a_{1}+\sup_{k\in\llbracket 1,n\rho\rrbracket}[S_{1}(k)-S_{2}(k)]+\sup_{k\in\llbracket 1,n\rho\rrbracket}[S_{2}(k)-a_{2}]

The second and third term can be controlled by an argument similar to the proof of (
C.2
) and (
C.4
) respectively. Note that by diffusive properties all the fluctuations are of the order
\sqrt{n\rho}
. Hence one can choose
\rho
small enough so that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}\bigg(\sup_{k\in\llbracket 1,n\rho\rrbracket}\big|S_{1}(k)-a_{1}\big|\geq\gamma\sqrt{n}\mid\mathsf{NI}\bigg)\geq 1-\varepsilon.

∎

We now study non-intersecting probabilities for random bridges
\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}
defined in Definition
3.6
(increments drawn from
\mathsf{f}
).
The following lemma shows that when the starting points and endpoints are far apart in the diffusive scale, non-intersection probability is bounded from zero.

Lemma C.7
.

Fix
\delta>0
. For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 4}
, consider the set

(C.10)

\displaystyle R_{n,\delta}:=\{(x_{1},x_{2}):|x_{i}|\leq 2\sqrt{n}(\log n)^{3/2},x_{1}-x_{2}\geq\delta\sqrt{n}\}

There exists
\phi=\phi(\delta)>0
such that for all
n
large enough and all
(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})\in R_{n,\delta}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}\left(\inf_{k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket}\big[S_{1}(k)-S_{2}(k)\big]\geq\tfrac{1}{4}\delta\sqrt{n}\right)\geq\phi.

Proof.

Fix any
(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})\in R_{n,\delta}
. For simplicity let us write
\mathbb{P}
for
\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}
. Note that
|b_{i}-a_{i}|\leq 4\sqrt{n}(\log n)^{3/2}
. By the KMT coupling for Brownian bridges (Theorem 2.3 in
[
DW21a
]
with
z=b_{i}-a_{i}
and
p=0
), there exists a constant
\mathrm{C}>0
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
we have

(C.11)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}\bigg(\neg\mathsf{SC}_{(a_{1},a_{2})}^{(b_{1},b_{2})}\bigg)\leq\tfrac{1}{n},

\displaystyle\mbox{where }\mathsf{SC}_{(a_{1},a_{2})}^{(b_{1},b_{2})}:=\bigg\{\sup_{k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket,i=1,2}\left|S_{i}(k)-\sqrt{n}B_{i}(k/n)-a_{i}-\tfrac{k}{n}(b_{i}-a_{i})\right|\leq\mathrm{C}\log^{3}n\bigg\},

and where
B_{1},B_{2}
are Brownian bridges on the same probability space with variance
\int x^{2}\mathsf{f}(x)dx
(
\mathsf{SC}
stands for ‘strong coupling’). By Brownian bridge properties, there exists
\phi=\phi(\delta)>0
so that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}\bigg(\sup_{x\in[0,1]}(|B_{1}(x)|+|B_{2}(x)|)\leq\tfrac{1}{8}\delta\bigg)\geq 2\phi.

Combining the previous two math displays we see that with probability
2\phi-\frac{1}{n}
we have

\displaystyle S_{1}(k)-S_{2}(k)

\displaystyle\geq a_{1}-a_{2}+\tfrac{k}{n}(b_{1}-a_{1}-b_{2}+a_{2})-2\mathrm{C}(\log n)^{3}-\tfrac{1}{4}\delta\sqrt{n}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{n-k}{n}a_{1}-a_{2}+\tfrac{k}{n}(b_{1}-b_{2})-2\mathrm{C}(\log n)^{3}-\tfrac{1}{4}\delta\sqrt{n}

\displaystyle\geq-2\mathrm{C}(\log n)^{3}+\tfrac{1}{2}\delta\sqrt{n}>\tfrac{1}{4}\delta\sqrt{n}

for all large enough
n
. Taking
n
large enough ensures
2\phi-\frac{1}{n}\geq\phi
completing the proof.
∎

Our next lemma gives a crude bound for the weak non-intersection probability in terms of true non-intersection probability.

Lemma C.8
.

There exists
\mathrm{C}>0
such that for all
p\in[0,\infty)
,
(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}(\mathsf{NI}_{p})\leq e^{\mathrm{C}p}\cdot\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}(\mathsf{NI}).

Proof.

By lifting the first random bridge by
p
units we see that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}(\mathsf{NI}_{p})=\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1}+p,a_{2}),(b_{1}+p,b_{2})}(\mathsf{NI}).

Conditioning on the second point and the penultimate point of both the random bridges we get

(C.12)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1}+p,a_{2}),(b_{1}+p,b_{2})}(\mathsf{NI})=\frac{\int_{x_{1}\geq x_{2},y_{1}\geq y_{2}}\Lambda_{x_{1},x_{2}}^{n;(y_{1},y_{2})}(\mathsf{NI})\Upsilon_{p}(x_{1},x_{2};y_{1},y_{2})dx_{1}dx_{2}dy_{1}dy_{2}}{\mathsf{f}^{\ast(n-1)}(a_{1}-b_{1})\mathsf{f}^{\ast(n-1)}(a_{2}-b_{2})}.

where

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{p}(x_{1},x_{2};y_{1},y_{2}):=\mathsf{f}(a_{1}+p-x_{1})\mathsf{f}(a_{2}-x_{2})\mathsf{f}(y_{1}-b_{1}-p)\mathsf{f}(y_{2}-b_{2}),

\displaystyle\Lambda_{x_{1},x_{2}}^{n;(y_{1},y_{2})}(\mathsf{NI}):=\int_{x_{j,1}\geq x_{j,2},j\in\llbracket 3,n-2\rrbracket}\prod_{j=2}^{n-2}\mathsf{f}(x_{j,1}-x_{j+1,1})\mathsf{f}(x_{j,2}-x_{j+1,2})\prod_{j=3}^{n-2}dx_{j,1}dx_{j,2}.

Here in the above integration we set
x_{2,1}:=x_{1}
,
x_{2,2}:=x_{2}
,
x_{n-1,1}:=y_{1}
,
x_{n-1,2}:=y_{2}
. From Lemma
B.3
, we have that
\Upsilon_{p}(x_{1},x_{2};y_{1},y_{2})\leq e^{\mathrm{C}p}\Upsilon_{0}(x_{1},x_{2};y_{1},y_{2})
, where the
\mathrm{C}>0
depends only on
\theta
. Plugging this bound back in (
C.12
) we get the desired result.
∎

The following technical lemma, which can be thought of as the bridge analog of Lemma
C.3
, studies the non-intersection probability for random bridges when the starting points are close.

Lemma C.9
.

Fix
M>0
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
. There exist a constant
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}(M)>0
such that for all
|a_{i}|\leq\sqrt{n}(\log n)^{3/2}
with
|a_{1}-a_{2}|\leq(\log n)^{3/2}
, and
|b_{i}|\leq M\sqrt{n}
with
b_{1}\geq b_{2}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}(\mathsf{NI})\leq\mathrm{C}\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}{\max\{a_{1}-a_{2},1\}\cdot\max\left\{\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}|a_{1}-b_{1}|,2\right\}^{3/2}}.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the lemma only for large enough
n
(since we can always choose the
\mathrm{C}
large enough). Set
r=\max\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}|a_{1}-b_{1}|,2\}
and
p=\lfloor nr^{-3}\rfloor
. We first claim that there exists
m(M)>0
such that

(C.13)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}(\mathsf{NI})\leq 2\cdot\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}\left(\{|S_{i}(p)-a_{i}|\leq m\sqrt{n}r^{-1}\mbox{ for }i=1,2\}\cap\mathsf{NI}\right).

Let us first complete the proof of the lemma assuming (
C.13
).
Note that the density of
S_{i}(p)
at
x
is given by
\frac{\mathsf{f}^{*(p-1)}(x-a_{i})\mathsf{f}^{*(n-p)}(b_{i}-x)}{\mathsf{f}^{*(n-1)}(b_{i}-a_{i})}
. By Lemma
C.2
, we may replace the
n
-fold convolution with Gaussian densities at the expensive of a multiplicative factor close to
1
. In particular for large enough
n
we have

\displaystyle\sup_{|x-a_{i}|\leq m\sqrt{n}r^{-1}}\frac{\mathsf{f}^{*(n-p)}(b_{i}-x)}{\mathsf{f}^{*(n-1)}(b_{i}-a_{i})}

\displaystyle=2\exp\left(\tfrac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\left(r^{2}-\tfrac{(r-mr^{-1})^{2}}{1-r^{-3}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=2\exp\left(\tfrac{1}{2\sigma^{2}(1-r^{-3})}\left(-r^{-1}-m^{2}r^{-2}+2m\right)\right)\leq 2e^{2m/\sigma^{2}}.

Thus
\frac{\mathsf{f}^{*(p-1)}(x-a_{i})f^{*(n-p)}(b_{i}-x)}{\mathsf{f}^{*(n-1)}(b_{i}-a_{i})}\leq 2e^{2m/\sigma^{2}}\cdot\mathsf{f}^{*(p-1)}(x-a_{i})
whenever
|x-a_{i}|\leq m\sqrt{n}r^{-1}
. This allows us to go from random bridge laws to random walk laws. We thus have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}\left(\{|S_{i}(p)-a_{i}|\leq m\sqrt{n}r^{-1}\mbox{ for }i=1,2\}\cap\mathsf{NI}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}\left(\{|S_{i}(p)-a_{i}|\leq m\sqrt{n}r^{-1}\mbox{ for }i=1,2\}\cap\bigcap_{k=1}^{p}\{S_{1}(k)\geq S_{2}(k)\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2e^{2m/\sigma^{2}}\cdot\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}\left(\{|S_{i}(p)-a_{i}|\leq m\sqrt{n}r^{-1}\mbox{ for }i=1,2\}\cap\bigcap_{k=1}^{p}\{S_{1}(k)\geq S_{2}(k)\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2e^{2m/\sigma^{2}}\cdot\mathbb{P}^{p;(a_{1},a_{2})}\left(\bigcap_{k=1}^{p}\{S_{1}(k)\geq S_{2}(k)\}\right)\leq\tfrac{\mathrm{C}}{\sqrt{n}}r^{3/2}\cdot\max\{a_{1}-a_{2},1\}.

where the last inequality uses Lemma
C.3
. This completes the proof modulo (
C.13
). The rest of the proof is devoted to showing (
C.13
).

We claim that

(C.14)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}(S_{1}(p)-a_{1}\leq-m\sqrt{n}r^{-1}\mid\mathsf{NI})\leq\tfrac{1}{8},

(C.15)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}(S_{1}(p)-a_{1}\geq m\sqrt{n}r^{-1}\mid\mathsf{NI})\leq\tfrac{1}{8},

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}(S_{2}(p)-a_{2}\leq-m\sqrt{n}r^{-1}\mid\mathsf{NI})\leq\tfrac{1}{8},

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}(S_{2}(p)-a_{2}\geq m\sqrt{n}r^{-1}\mid\mathsf{NI})\leq\tfrac{1}{8},

for all large enough
n
. Applying an union bound, leads to (
C.13
). We shall prove the first two inequalities: (
C.14
) and (
C.15
), the remaining two follows in a similar fashion.

Proof of Eq. (
C.15
).
Similar to the proof of (
C.3
) and (
C.4
), by stochastic monotonicity for random bridges (Proposition
C.4
) we have

(C.16)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}(S_{1}(p)-a_{1}\leq-m\sqrt{n}r^{-1}\mid\mathsf{NI})\leq\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}(S_{1}(p)-a_{1}\leq-m\sqrt{n}r^{-1})

We invoke the KMT coupling for random bridges
[
DW21a
]
to define Brownian bridge
B_{1},B_{2}
on
[0,1]
on a common probability space such that (
C.11
) holds. By (
C.11
), with probability
1-\frac{1}{n}
,

\displaystyle S_{1}(p)-a_{1}

\displaystyle\geq\sqrt{n}B_{1}(p/n)+\tfrac{p}{n}(b_{1}-a_{1})-\mathrm{C}\log^{3}n

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n}B_{1}(p/n)-\sqrt{n}r^{-2}-\mathrm{C}\log^{3}n\geq\sqrt{n}B_{1}(p/n)-2\sqrt{n}r^{-1}.

for large enough
n
. Since
p/n
is of the order
r^{-3}
,
B_{1}(p/n)
fluctuates of the order
r^{-3/2}
. By Brownian bridge one point tail estimates, there exists a constant
c>0
such that for all
m\geq 3

\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}(B_{1}(p/n)>-(m-2)r^{-1})\geq 1-e^{-cm^{2}r}.

Thus by an union bound we have

(C.17)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}(S_{1}(p)-a_{1}>-m\sqrt{n}r^{-1})\geq 1-\tfrac{1}{n}-e^{-cm^{2}r}.

Taking
m,n
are large enough, ensure that
1-\frac{1}{n}-e^{-cm^{2}r}\geq\frac{7}{8}
. This verifies (
C.14
).

Proof of Eq. (
C.15
).
By stochastic monotonicity (Proposition
C.4
) at the starting points,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}(S_{1}(p)-a_{1}\geq m\sqrt{n}r^{-1}\mid\mathsf{NI})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1}+\sqrt{n}r^{-1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}(S_{1}(p)-a_{1}\geq m\sqrt{n}r^{-1}\mid\mathsf{NI})

(C.18)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1}+\sqrt{n}r^{-1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}(S_{1}(p)-a_{1}\geq m\sqrt{n}r^{-1})}{\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1}+\sqrt{n}r^{-1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}(\mathsf{NI})}.

Using an argument similar to the derivation of (
C.17
), we find that

(C.19)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1}+\sqrt{n}r^{-1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}(S_{1}(p)-a_{1}\geq m\sqrt{n}r^{-1})\leq\tfrac{1}{n}+e^{-cm^{2}r}.

This gives an upper bound for the numerator of (
C.18
). For the denominator, recall the event
\mathsf{SC}_{(a_{1},a_{2})}^{(b_{1},b_{2})}
and the Brownian bridges
B_{1},B_{2}
from (
C.11
). Note that on the event

\mathsf{SC}_{(a_{1}+\sqrt{n}r^{-1},a_{2})}^{(b_{1},b_{2})}\cap\{\inf_{x\in[0,1]}(B_{1}(x)-B_{2}(x))\geq-\frac{1}{2}r^{-1}\},

for large enough
n
we have

\displaystyle S_{1}(k)

\displaystyle\geq\sqrt{n}B_{1}(k/n)+a_{1}+\sqrt{n}r^{-1}+\tfrac{k}{n}(b_{1}-a_{1})-\mathrm{C}(\log n)^{3}

\displaystyle\geq\sqrt{n}B_{2}(k/n)+\tfrac{1}{2}\sqrt{n}r^{-1}+a_{2}+\tfrac{k}{n}(b_{2}-a_{2})-2\mathrm{C}(\log n)^{3}

\displaystyle\geq S_{2}(k)+\tfrac{1}{2}\sqrt{n}r^{-1}-3\mathrm{C}(\log n)^{3}\geq S_{2}(k).

where we used that
|a_{1}-a_{2}|\leq(\log n)^{3/2}
,
b_{1}\geq b_{2}
, and
r\leq(\log n)^{3/2}
. Thus for large enough
n
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1}+\sqrt{n}r^{-1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}(\mathsf{NI})

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1}+\sqrt{n}r^{-1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}\bigg(\inf_{x\in[0,1]}(B_{1}(x)-B_{2}(x))\geq-\tfrac{1}{2}r^{-1}\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad-\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1}+\sqrt{n}r^{-1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}\bigg(\neg\mathsf{SC}_{(a_{1}+\sqrt{n}r^{-1},a_{2})}^{(b_{1},b_{2})}\bigg)

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{C}r^{-2}-\tfrac{1}{n}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\mathrm{C}r^{-2},

where the penultimate inequality follows from (
C.11
) and Brownian bridge calculations (see Lemma 2.11 in
[
CH16
]
for example). Combining (
C.19
) and the above lower bound we have

\displaystyle\mbox{r.h.s.~\eqref{eeb0}}\leq\tfrac{2}{\mathrm{C}}\left(\tfrac{r^{2}}{n}+r^{2}e^{-cm^{2}r}\right)\leq\tfrac{1}{8},

for all large enough
n
and
m
(as
r\leq(\log n)^{3/2}
).
∎

Corollary C.10
.

Fix any
M>0
and
n\geq 1
. Suppose
|a_{i}|,|b_{i}|\leq M\sqrt{n}
for
i=1,2
. There exists a constant
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}(M)>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}(\mathsf{NI})\leq\mathrm{C}\!\cdot\!\mathbb{P}^{\lfloor n/4\rfloor;(a_{1},a_{2})}(\widetilde{\mathsf{NI}})\mathbb{P}^{\lfloor n/4\rfloor;(b_{1},b_{2})}(\widetilde{\mathsf{NI}})

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}(\mathsf{NI})\geq\tfrac{1}{\mathrm{C}}\!\cdot\!\mathbb{P}^{\lfloor n/4\rfloor;(a_{1},a_{2})}(\widetilde{\mathsf{NI}})\mathbb{P}^{\lfloor n/4\rfloor;(b_{1},b_{2})}(\widetilde{\mathsf{NI}}).

where
\widetilde{\mathsf{NI}}:=\{S_{1}(k)\geq S_{1}(k)\mbox{ for all }k\in\llbracket 2,n/4\rrbracket\}.

Proof.

The upper bound follows by applying (
4.23
) with
\delta_{i}=\frac{1}{4}
and integrating over the non-intersection event. Let us focus on the lower bound. For simplicity we will drop the floor functions from
\lfloor n/4\rfloor
. By Lemma
C.5
, we can choose a constant
\widetilde{M}
depending only on
M
such that for all
|c_{i}|\leq M\sqrt{n}
with
|x_{i}|\leq M\sqrt{n}
, we have

(C.20)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n/4;(c_{1},c_{2})}\bigg(\bigcap_{i=1}^{2}\{|S_{i}(n/4)|\leq\widetilde{M}\sqrt{n}\}\mid\widetilde{\mathsf{NI}}\bigg)\geq\tfrac{3}{4}.

By Lemma
C.5
we can next choose a
\delta=\delta(M)>0
small enough such that

(C.21)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n/4;(c_{1},c_{2})}\bigg((S_{1}(n/4),S_{2}(n/4))\in R_{n,\delta}\mid\widetilde{\mathsf{NI}}\bigg)\geq\tfrac{3}{4}.

where
R_{n,\delta}
is from (
C.10
). By Lemma
C.7
,
there exists
\phi(\delta)>0
so that for all
(x_{1},x_{2}),(y_{1},y_{2})\in R_{n,\delta}

(C.22)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n/2;(x_{1},x_{2}),(y_{1},y_{2})}\bigg(\bigcap_{k=1}^{n/2}\{S_{1}(k)\geq S_{2}(k)\}\bigg)\geq\phi.

We next consider the events

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{1}:=\left\{|S_{i}(n/4)|\leq\widetilde{M}\sqrt{n}\mbox{ for }i=1,2\right\},\quad\mathsf{E}_{2}:=\left\{|S_{i}(3n/4)|\leq\widetilde{M}\sqrt{n}\mbox{ for }i=1,2\right\}.

Using (
4.24
) with
\delta=\frac{1}{4}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}(\mathsf{NI})\geq\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}(\mathsf{E}_{1}\cap\mathsf{E}_{2}\cap\mathsf{NI})

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{C}^{-1}\!\cdot\!\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(\mathsf{E}_{1}\cap\mathsf{E}_{2}\cap\mathsf{NI})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{C}^{-1}\!\cdot\!\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(\mathsf{NI})\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(\mathsf{E}_{1}\cap\mathsf{E}_{2}\mid{\mathsf{NI}}).

for some
\mathrm{C}>0
depending on
\widetilde{M},M
. Here
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}:=\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{(n;n/4,n/4);(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}
denotes the joint law of two independent
(n;n/4,n/4)
-modified random bridges of length
n
starting at
(a_{1},a_{2})
and ending at
(b_{1},b_{2})
(see Definition
4.9
). In view of our
\widetilde{M}
choice and by the definition of modified random bridges, we have

\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(\mathsf{E}_{1}\cap\mathsf{E}_{2}\mid{\mathsf{NI}})=\mathbb{P}^{n/4;(a_{1},a_{2})}\bigg(\bigcap_{i=1}^{2}\{|S_{i}(n/4)|\leq\widetilde{M}\sqrt{n}\}\mid\widetilde{\mathsf{NI}}\bigg)\mathbb{P}^{n/4;(b_{1},b_{2})}\bigg(\bigcap_{i=1}^{2}\{|S_{i}(n/4)|\leq\widetilde{M}\sqrt{n}\}\mid\widetilde{\mathsf{NI}}\bigg)

which is lower bounded by
(3/4)^{2}
from (
C.20
) and (
C.21
). Furthermore, in view of (
C.22
), we have

\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(\mathsf{NI})\geq\phi\cdot\mathbb{P}^{n/4;(a_{1},a_{2})}(\widetilde{\mathsf{NI}})\mathbb{P}^{n/4;(b_{1},b_{2})}(\widetilde{\mathsf{NI}}).

We thus have the desired lower bound.
∎

We now analyze the
\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta}
event defined in (
4.22
) under modified random bridge law. Fix any
M>0
,
n\geq 1
, and
(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Suppose
|a_{i}|,|b_{i}|\leq M\sqrt{n}
and
a_{1}\geq a_{2}
. Take
p,q\in\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket
with
p+q\leq n/2
and
p\neq 0
. Suppose further that there exists
\rho\in(0,1)
such that either
q\geq n\rho
or
b_{1}-b_{2}\geq\rho\sqrt{n}
. Consider two independent
(n;p,q)
- modified random bridges
(S_{i}(k))_{k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket,i=1,2}
starting and ending at
(a_{1},a_{2})
and
(b_{1},b_{2})
respectively. We denote its law by
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{(n;p,q);(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}
. The following lemma asserts
\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta}
event is very likely under non-intersection.

Lemma C.11
.

Fix
\varepsilon,\rho\in(0,1)
and
M>0
. There exist
\beta(\varepsilon,\rho,M)>0
,
n_{0}(\varepsilon,\rho,M)>0
, such that for all
n\geq n_{0}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{(n;p,q);(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}\left(\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta}\mid\mathsf{NI}\right)\geq 1-\varepsilon.

Proof.

Recall that
\mathsf{Gap}_{\beta}
event is intersection of six smaller ‘Gap’ events:
\mathsf{Gap}_{i,\beta}
defined around (
4.22
). For simplicity write
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}
for
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{(n;p,q);(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}
. We now analyze each ‘Gap’ event separately.

\mathsf{Gap}_{1,\beta}
and
\mathsf{Gap}_{2,\beta}
.
Note that for
k\in\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket
,
S_{1}(k)-S_{2}(k)
is itself a random walk. The
\mathsf{NI}
event corresponds to the event of this random walk being non-negative. By classical result about growth of random walks conditioned to stay non-negative (see
[
Rit81
, Theorem 2]
) it follows that one can choose
\beta
small enough such that
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(\mathsf{Gap}_{1,\beta}\mid\mathsf{NI})\geq 1-\frac{\varepsilon}{6}
. By the same argument one has
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(\mathsf{Gap}_{2,\beta}\mid\mathsf{NI})\geq 1-\frac{\varepsilon}{6}
for all large enough
n
by choosing
\beta
small enough.

\mathsf{Gap}_{3,\beta}
.

Note that combining (
C.2
), (
C.3
), and (
C.4
) from Lemma
C.5
we have tightness of the endpoint of random walks conditioned on non-intersection. Combining this with (
C.1
), one can choose
\gamma
small enough such that

(C.23)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}\big((S_{1}(p),S_{2}(p)),(S_{1}(n-q),S_{2}(n-q)\mid\mathsf{NI})\in\mathcal{P}_{n,\gamma}\big)\geq 1-\tfrac{\varepsilon}{12},

where

(C.24)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{n,\gamma}:=\{(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:|z_{i}|\leq\gamma^{-1}\sqrt{n},z_{1}-z_{2}\geq\gamma\sqrt{n}\}.

In other words, with probability
1-\frac{\varepsilon}{12}
, the endpoints of the middle portions of the modified random bridges are in
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}
when conditioned upon non-intersection . Thus,

(C.25)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(\mathsf{Gap}_{3,\beta}\mid\mathsf{NI})\geq(1-\tfrac{\varepsilon}{12})\cdot\inf_{(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})\in\mathcal{P}_{n,\gamma}}\mathbb{P}^{n-p-q+1;(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}(\mathsf{Gap}_{3,\beta}\mid\mathsf{NI}).

Since the increments are drawn from a smooth density, for each fixed
n
, the probability

\mathbb{P}^{n-p-q+1;(a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2})}(\mathsf{Gap}_{3,\beta}\mid\mathsf{NI})

is jointly continuous with respect to the starting and ending points of the random bridge. As
\mathcal{P}_{n,\gamma}
is closed, the infimum in (
C.25
) is attained at some point
(a_{1}^{*},a_{2}^{*}),(b_{1}^{*},b_{2}^{*})\in\mathcal{P}_{n,\gamma}
. Take any subsequential limit of
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}(a_{1}^{*},a_{2}^{*}),\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}(b_{1}^{*},b_{2}^{*})
say
(u_{1},u_{2}),(v_{1},v_{2})
. Then
|u_{i}|,|v_{i}|\leq\gamma^{-1}
and
u_{1}-u_{2},v_{1}-v_{2}\geq\gamma
. By invariance principle for Brownian bridges, this conditional law under diffusive scaling converges to non-intersecting Brownian bridges (with variance
\int x^{2}\mathsf{f}(x)dx
)
(B_{1},B_{2})
starting at
(u_{1},u_{2})
ending at
(v_{1},v_{2})
. We have
\mathbb{P}(\inf_{x\in[0,1]}(B_{1}(x)-B_{2}(x))>0)=1
. This implies along this subsequence the limit of
\mathbb{P}^{n-p-q+1;(a_{1}^{*},a_{2}^{*}),(b_{1}^{*},b_{2}^{*})}(\mathsf{Gap}_{3,\beta}\mid\mathsf{NI})
is
1
. Since this holds for all subsequences, we thus see that for all large enough
n
, r.h.s. (
C.25
) can be made at least
1-\tfrac{\varepsilon}{6}
.

\mathsf{Gap}_{4,\beta}
and
\mathsf{Gap}_{5,\beta}
.
We shall first show
\mathsf{Gap}_{4,\beta}
happens with high probability under non-intersection. Note that this event only depends on the first part of the modified random bridge (which is just two independent pure random walk) independent of the other two parts. Hence

(C.26)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(\neg\mathsf{Gap}_{4,\beta}\mid\mathsf{NI})

(C.27)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}\bigg(\bigcap_{k=2}^{p}\{S_{1}(k)-S_{1}(k-1)\geq\beta^{-1}k^{1/8}\}\mid\bigcap_{k=2}^{p}\{S_{1}(k)\geq S_{2}(k)\}\bigg)

(C.28)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=2}^{p}\frac{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}\bigg(\{S_{1}(k)-S_{1}(k-1)\geq\beta^{-1}k^{1/8}\}\cap\bigcap_{j\in\{2\}\cup\llbracket k+2,p\rrbracket}\{S_{1}(k)\geq S_{2}(k)\}\bigg)}{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}\bigg(\bigcap_{k=2}^{p}\{S_{1}(k)\geq S_{2}(k)\}\bigg)},

where the above inequality follows via an union bound.
Since under
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}
,
(S_{1}(\ell),S_{2}(\ell))_{\ell\in\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket}
are two independent random walks starting from
(a_{1},a_{2})
. From (
C.6
) we get that

(C.29)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}\bigg(\bigcap_{k=2}^{p}\{S_{1}(k)\geq S_{2}(k)\}\bigg)\geq\frac{\mathrm{C}^{-1}}{\sqrt{p}}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(S_{1}(2)\geq S_{2}(2)).

(C.30)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}\bigg(\{S_{1}(k)-S_{1}(k-1)\geq\beta^{-1}k^{1/8}\}\cap\bigcap_{j\in\{2\}\cup\llbracket k+2,p\rrbracket}\{S_{1}(k)\geq S_{2}(k)\}\bigg)

(C.31)

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(2)\geq S_{2}(2)}\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(k)-S_{1}(k-1)\geq\beta^{-1}k^{\frac{1}{8}}}\!\cdot\!\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\prod_{j=k+2}^{p}\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(j)\geq S_{2}(j)}\mid\sigma\big((S_{1}(\ell),S_{2}(\ell))_{\ell\in\llbracket 1,k+1\rrbracket}\big)\bigg]\bigg]

By Lemma
C.3
, we have the following bound for the interior conditional expectation above:

(C.32)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\prod_{j=k+2}^{p}\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(j)\geq S_{2}(j)}\mid\sigma\big((S_{1}(\ell),S_{2}(\ell))_{\ell\in\llbracket 1,k+1\rrbracket}\big)\bigg]\leq\frac{\mathrm{C}\!\cdot\!\max\{S_{1}(k+1)-S_{2}(k+1),1\}}{\sqrt{p-k+1}}.

Under
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}
, the increments of
S_{1}(\cdot)
and
S_{2}(\cdot)
are independent and distributed as
\mathsf{f}
which has exponential tails by assumption. We now claim that

(C.33)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(2)\geq S_{2}(2)}\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(k)-S_{1}(k-1)\geq\beta^{-1}k^{1/8}}\!\cdot\!\max\{S_{1}(k+1)-S_{2}(k+1),1\}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{C}\cdot k\cdot e^{-\frac{1}{\mathrm{C}}\beta^{-1}k^{1/8}}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(S_{1}(2)\geq S_{2}(2))

We shall prove (
C.33
) later. Assuming it, combining the estimates from (
C.31
), (
C.32
), and (
C.33
) we get

\displaystyle\eqref{b31}\leq\mathrm{C}^{2}\sum_{k=2}^{p}\sqrt{\tfrac{p}{p-k+1}}\cdot k\cdot e^{-\frac{1}{\mathrm{C}}\beta^{-1}k^{1/8}}.

Taking
\beta
small enough, the right-hand side can be made smaller than
\varepsilon/6
. Thus,
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(\mathsf{Gap}_{4,\beta}\mid\mathsf{NI})\geq 1-\frac{\varepsilon}{6}
for all small enough
\beta
. An exact same argument leads to
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(\mathsf{Gap}_{5,\beta}\mid\mathsf{NI})\geq 1-\frac{\varepsilon}{6}
for all small enough
\beta
as well.

To prove Eq. (
C.33
), we start by writing
X(k):=S_{1}(k)-S_{1}(k-1)
and
Y(k):=S_{2}(k)-S_{2}(k-1)
. For
k=2
, observe that

\displaystyle\mbox{l.h.s.~\eqref{b34}}

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(2)\geq S_{2}(2)}\!\cdot\!\max\{S_{1}(3)-S_{2}(3),1\}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(2)\geq S_{2}(2)}\!\cdot\!\max\{S_{1}(2)-S_{2}(2),1\}\bigg]+\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(2)\geq S_{2}(2)}\!\cdot\!\max\{X(3)-Y(3),1\}\bigg].

By (
C.9
), the first expectation above is less than
\mathrm{C}^{\prime}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(S_{1}(2)\geq S_{2}(2))
. For the second expectation by independence we get

\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(2)\geq S_{2}(2)}\cdot\max\{X(3)-Y(3),1\}\bigg]=\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(S_{1}(2)\geq S_{2}(2))\cdot\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}[\max\{X(3)-Y(3),1\}].

Since
X(3)
and
Y(3)
have exponential tails, by adjusting the constant
\mathrm{C}^{\prime}
we get
\mbox{l.h.s.~\eqref{b34}}\leq\mathrm{C}^{\prime}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(S_{1}(2)\geq S_{2}(2))
. This proves (
C.33
) for
k=2
upon adjusting
\mathrm{C}
. For
k\geq 3
, using the fact that
\max\{\sum_{i}A_{i},1\}\leq\sum_{i}\max\{A_{i},1\}
, we get

(C.34)

\displaystyle\mbox{l.h.s.~\eqref{b34}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=3}^{k+1}\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(2)\geq S_{2}(2)}\mathbf{1}_{X(k)\geq\beta^{-1}k^{1/8}}\!\cdot\!\max\{X(i)-Y(i),1\}\bigg]

\displaystyle+\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(2)\geq S_{2}(2)}\mathbf{1}_{X(k)\geq\beta^{-1}k^{1/8}}\!\cdot\!\max\{S_{1}(2)-S_{2}(2),1\}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=3}^{k+1}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(S_{1}(2)\geq S_{2}(2))\cdot\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\mathbf{1}_{X(k)\geq\beta^{-1}k^{1/8}}\!\cdot\!\max\{X(i)-Y(i),1\}\bigg]

\displaystyle+\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(2)\geq S_{2}(2)}\!\cdot\!\max\{S_{1}(2)-S_{2}(2),1\}\bigg]\cdot\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}\big(X(k)\geq\beta^{-1}k^{1/8}\big).

Using (
C.9
) again, we have
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\mathbf{1}_{S_{1}(2)\geq S_{2}(2)}\!\cdot\!\max\{S_{1}(2)-S_{2}(2),1\}\bigg]\leq\mathrm{C}^{\prime}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(S_{1}(2)\geq S_{2}(2))
. Using exponential tail estimates for
X(\ell),Y(\ell)
we obtain that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\mathbf{1}_{X(k)\geq\beta^{-1}k^{1/8}}\!\cdot\!\max\{X(i)-Y(i),1\}\bigg]\leq\mathrm{C}\exp\big(-\tfrac{1}{\mathrm{C}}\beta^{-1}k^{1/8}\big),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}\big(X(k)\geq\beta^{-1}k^{1/8}\big)\leq\mathrm{C}\exp\big(-\tfrac{1}{\mathrm{C}}\beta^{-1}k^{1/8}\big).

Putting this estimates back in r.h.s. (
C.34
) we arrive at (
C.33
).

\mathsf{Gap}_{6,\beta}
.
From (
C.23
), we get that the endpoints of the middle part of the modified random walk are in
\mathcal{P}_{n,\gamma}
(defined in (
C.24
)) with probability
1-\frac{\varepsilon}{12}
. Whenever the endpoints are in
\mathcal{P}_{n,\gamma}
, by Lemma
C.7
, the probability of non-intersection of the middle portion of the walk is lower bounded by some constant
\phi>0
. Under this event, we may use the KMT coupling
[
DW21a
]
on the middle portion bridge of the modified random bridge to deduce that

\mathbb{P}^{n-p-q+1;(c_{1},c_{2}),(d_{1},d_{2})}(|S_{1}(k)-S_{1}(k-1)|\geq\beta^{-1}\log n)\leq\tfrac{1}{n^{2}}.

for all small enough
\beta
and for all
(c_{1},c_{2}),(d_{1},d_{2})\in\mathcal{P}_{n,\gamma}
. Combining all these estimates, by a union bound we have the desired result.
∎

We end this section with a modulus of continuity estimate for non-intersecting random walks.

Lemma C.12
.

Fix
M,\gamma>0
. There exists
n_{0}(M,\gamma)>0
and
\delta(M,\gamma)>0
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
and for all
0\leq a_{1}-a_{2}\leq M+2\log\log n
we have (recall the modulus of continuity
\omega_{\delta}
from (
5.1
))

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}\bigg(\omega_{\delta}(S_{i}(\cdot),\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket)\geq\gamma\sqrt{n}\mid\mathsf{NI}_{0}\llbracket 2,n\rrbracket\bigg)\leq\varepsilon,

Proof.

Fix
\gamma>0
. We write
\mathbb{P}
for
\mathbb{P}^{n;(a_{1},a_{2})}
. By Corollary
C.6
one can choose
\rho
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg(\sup_{i=1,2}\omega_{\delta}(S_{i}(\cdot),\llbracket 1,n\rho\rrbracket)\geq\gamma\sqrt{n}\mid\mathsf{NI}_{0}\llbracket 2,n\rrbracket\bigg)\leq\varepsilon.

Thus it suffices to control the modulus of continuity away from zero: on the interval
\llbracket n\rho/2,n\rrbracket
(assuming
\delta<\rho/2
). Towards this end let
I_{v}:=\{(x_{1},x_{2}):|x_{i}|\leq v^{-1}\sqrt{n},x_{1}-x_{2}\geq v\sqrt{n}\}
. By Lemma
C.5
, one can choose
v
small enough to get
\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{A}_{v}\mid\mathsf{NI}_{0}\llbracket 2,n\rrbracket)\geq 1-\varepsilon
where
\mathsf{A}_{v}:=\{(S_{1}(n\rho/2),S_{2}(n\rho/2))\in I_{v}\}
.
Let
\mathcal{F}:=\sigma\big(S_{1}(n\rho/2),S_{2}(n\rho/2)\big)
. Note that

(C.35)

\mathbb{P}\Big(\omega_{\delta}(S_{i}(\cdot),\llbracket n\rho/2,n\rrbracket)\geq\gamma\sqrt{n}\mid\mathsf{NI}_{0}\llbracket 2,n\rrbracket\Big)\leq\varepsilon+\frac{\mathbb{P}\big(\mathsf{A}_{v}\cap\{\omega_{\delta}(S_{i}(\cdot),\llbracket n\rho/2,n\rrbracket)\geq\gamma\sqrt{n}\}\cap\mathsf{NI}_{0}\llbracket 2,n\rho/2\rrbracket\big)}{\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{NI}_{0}\llbracket 2,n\rrbracket)}.

Note that
\{\omega_{\delta}(S_{i}(\cdot),\llbracket n\rho/2,n\rrbracket)\geq\gamma\sqrt{n}\}
is independent of
\mathcal{F}
. By Lemma
C.7
, we have
\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}_{v}}\cdot\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{NI}_{0}\llbracket n\rho/2,n\rrbracket}\mid\mathcal{F}]\geq\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}_{v}}\phi
for some
\phi>0
. Combining these two facts we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{A}_{v}\cap\{\omega_{\delta}(S_{i}(\cdot),\llbracket n\rho/2,n\rrbracket)\geq\gamma\sqrt{n}\}\cap\mathsf{NI}_{0}\llbracket 2,n\rho/2\rrbracket\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{A}_{v}\cap\mathsf{NI}_{0}\llbracket 2,n\rho/2\rrbracket\right)\mathbb{P}\left(\omega_{\delta}(S_{i}(\cdot),\llbracket n\rho/2,n\rrbracket)\geq\gamma\sqrt{n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\phi^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{A}_{v}\cap\mathsf{NI}_{0}\llbracket 2,n\rho/2\rrbracket}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{NI}_{0}\llbracket n\rho/2,n\rrbracket}\mid\mathcal{F}]\right]\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\omega_{\delta}(S_{i}(\cdot),\llbracket n\rho/2,n\rrbracket)\geq\gamma\sqrt{n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\phi^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{A}_{v}\cap\mathsf{NI}_{0}\llbracket 2,n\rrbracket)\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\omega_{\delta}(S_{i}(\cdot),\llbracket n\rho/2,n\rrbracket)\geq\gamma\sqrt{n}\right)

(C.36)

\displaystyle\leq\phi^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{NI}_{0}\llbracket 2,n\rrbracket)\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\omega_{\delta}(S_{i}(\cdot),\llbracket n\rho/2,n\rrbracket)\geq\gamma\sqrt{n}\right).

Invoking the modulus of continuity of random walks we can choose
\delta
small enough such that
\mathbb{P}\left(\omega_{\delta}(S_{i}(\cdot),\llbracket n\rho/2,n\rrbracket)\geq\gamma\sqrt{n}\right)
is at most
\varepsilon\phi
for all large enough
n
. This, implies

\eqref{b55}\leq\varepsilon\cdot\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{NI}_{0}\llbracket 2,n\rrbracket).

Using this inequality we see that r.h.s. (
C.35
) is at most
2\varepsilon
. Hence combining the near zero and away zero modulus of continuity we get the desired result by adjusting
\gamma
and
\varepsilon
.
∎

Appendix D
Supporting calculations

In this section we provide a detailed verification of various tedious calculations. We first show how to go from (
2.11
) to (
2.12
)-(
2.14
) under the change of variables
u_{i,j}=\log\big(t_{N+\lfloor j/2\rfloor-i+1,N-\lceil j/2\rceil-i+2}\big)
for
(i,j)\in K_{N}
. This follows from the fact that the factor
\prod t_{i,j}^{-1}
in (
2.11
) is absorbed as the Jacobian of the change of variables, as well as the following four relations:

(D.1)

\displaystyle\quad\prod_{j=1}^{N}\left(\frac{\tau_{2N-2j+2}\tau_{2N-2j}}{\tau_{2N-2j+1}^{2}}\right)^{\theta_{j}}=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\left(e^{-\theta_{i}u_{i,2N-2i+2}}\prod_{j=1}^{N-i+1}e^{\theta_{N-j+1}(u_{i,2j-1}-u_{i,2j})}\prod_{j=1}^{N-i}e^{\theta_{N-j+1}(u_{i,2j+1}-u_{i,2j})}\right)

(D.2)

\displaystyle\quad\sum_{i>j}\frac{t_{i-1,j}}{t_{i,j}}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N-i+1}e^{u_{i,2j-1}-u_{i,2j}}+\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\sum_{j=1}^{N-i}e^{u_{i+1,2j}-u_{i,2j+1}}

(D.3)

\displaystyle\quad\sum_{i\geq j>1}\frac{t_{i,j-1}}{t_{i,j}}=\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\sum_{j=1}^{N-i}e^{u_{i,2j+1}-u_{i,2j}}+\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\sum_{j=1}^{N-i}e^{u_{i+1,2j}-u_{i,2j-1}}

(D.4)

\displaystyle\quad\prod_{j=1}^{N}t_{j,j}^{(-1)^{N-j+1}\alpha}=\prod_{j=1}^{N}t_{N-j+1,N-j+1}^{(-1)^{j}\alpha}=\prod_{i=1}^{N}e^{(-1)^{i}u_{i,1}\alpha}.

While (
D.4
) is obvious, (
D.1
), (
D.2
) and (
D.3
) are shown below. We continue with the same notations as in the proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Verification of (
D.1
).
Note that from the transformation we have

\displaystyle e^{u_{j-i+1,2N-2j+1}}=t_{i+2N-2j,i},\qquad e^{u_{j-i+1,2N-2j+2}}=t_{i+2N-2j+1,i}.

This yields

\displaystyle\tau_{2N-2j}^{\theta_{j}}=\prod_{i=1}^{j}t_{i+2N-2j,i}^{\theta_{j}}=\prod_{i=1}^{j}e^{\theta_{j}u_{j-i+1,2N-2j+1}}=\prod_{i=1}^{j}e^{\theta_{j}u_{i,2N-2j+1}}.

Similarly we have

\displaystyle\tau_{2N-2j+2}^{\theta_{j}}=e^{-\theta_{j}u_{j,2N-2j+3}}\prod_{i=1}^{j}e^{\theta_{j}u_{i,2N-2j+3}},\quad\tau_{2N-2j+1}^{\theta_{j}}=\prod_{i=1}^{j}e^{\theta_{j}u_{i,2N-2j+2}}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{N}\left(\frac{\tau_{2N-2j+2}\tau_{2N-2j}}{\tau_{2N-2j+1}^{2}}\right)^{\theta_{j}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{N}\left(e^{-\theta_{j}u_{j,2N-2j+3}}\prod_{i=1}^{j}e^{\theta_{j}(u_{i,2N-2j+1}+u_{i,2N-2j+3}-2u_{i,2N-2j+2})}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{N}e^{-\theta_{i}u_{i,2N-2i+3}}\right)\cdot\left(\prod_{i=1}^{N}\prod_{j=i}^{N}e^{\theta_{j}(u_{i,2N-2j+1}+u_{i,2N-2j+3}-2u_{i,2N-2j+2})}\right)

\displaystyle{=}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{N}e^{-\theta_{i}u_{i,2N-2i+3}}\right)\cdot\left(\prod_{i=1}^{N}\prod_{j=1}^{N-i+1}e^{\theta_{N-j+1}(u_{i,2j-1}+u_{i,2j+1}-2u_{i,2j})}\right),

where the last equality follows by changing the dummy variable
j
has been changed to
N-j+1
. The last term above is clearly equal to the right-hand side of (
D.1
).

Verification of (
D.2
).
Let us write

\displaystyle\sum_{i>j}\frac{t_{i-1,j}}{t_{i,j}}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{i=j+1}^{2N-j+1}\frac{t_{i-1,j}}{t_{i,j}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{2N-2j+1}\frac{t_{j+r-1,j}}{t_{j+r,j}}

(D.5)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{N-j}\frac{t_{j+2r-1,j}}{t_{j+2r,j}}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{N-j+1}\frac{t_{j+2r-2,j}}{t_{j+2r-1,j}}.

Observe that

(D.6)

\displaystyle e^{u_{N-r-j+1,2r+1}}=t_{j+2r,j},\quad e^{u_{N-r-j+2,2r}}=t_{j+2r-1,j}.

Thus we have

(
D.5
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{N-j}e^{u_{N-r-j+2,2r}-u_{N-r-j+1,2r+1}}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{N-j+1}e^{u_{N-r-j+2,2r-1}-u_{N-r-j+2,2r}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{j-1}e^{u_{j-r+1,2r}-u_{j-r,2r+1}}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{j}e^{u_{j-r+1,2r-1}-u_{j-r+1,2r}}\quad(j\mapsto N-j+1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{N-1}\sum_{j=r+1}^{N}e^{u_{j-r+1,2r}-u_{j-r,2r+1}}+\sum_{r=1}^{N}\sum_{j=r}^{N}e^{u_{j-r+1,2r-1}-u_{j-r+1,2r}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{N-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N-r}e^{u_{i+1,2r}-u_{i,2r+1}}+\sum_{r=1}^{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N-r+1}e^{u_{i,2r-1}-u_{i,2r}},

where
(j\mapsto N-j+1)
means the dummy variable
j
has been changed to
N-j+1
to obtain the equality in the second step. The last equality follows by setting
j-r\mapsto i
and
j-r\mapsto i-1
in the first and second sum respectively. A final interchange of sum in each of the two terms leads to the right hand side of (
D.2
).

Verification of (
D.3
).
We follow the same above strategy and write

\displaystyle\sum_{i\geq j>1}\frac{t_{i,j-1}}{t_{i,j}}=\sum_{j=2}^{N}\sum_{i=j}^{2N-j+1}\frac{t_{i,j-1}}{t_{i,j}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=2}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{2N-2j+2}\frac{t_{j+r-1,j-1}}{t_{j+r-1,j}}

(D.7)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=2}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{N-j+1}\frac{t_{j+2r-1,j-1}}{t_{j+2r-1,j}}+\sum_{j=2}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{N-j+1}\frac{t_{j+2r-2,j-1}}{t_{j+2r-2,j}}

Due to (
D.6
) we have

(
D.7
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=2}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{N-j+1}e^{u_{N-r-j+2,2r+1}-u_{N-r-j+2,2r}}+\sum_{j=2}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{N-j+1}e^{u_{N-r-j+3,2r}-u_{N-r-j+2,2r-1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N-1}\sum_{r=1}^{j}e^{u_{j-r+1,2r+1}-u_{j-r+1,2r}}+\sum_{j=1}^{N-1}\sum_{r=1}^{j}e^{u_{j-r+2,2r}-u_{j-r+1,2r-1}}\quad(j\mapsto N-j+1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{N-1}\sum_{j=r}^{N-1}e^{u_{j-r+1,2r+1}-u_{j-r+1,2r}}+\sum_{r=1}^{N-1}\sum_{j=r}^{N-1}e^{u_{j-r+2,2r}-u_{j-r+1,2r-1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{N-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N-r}e^{u_{i,2r+1}-u_{i,2r}}+\sum_{r=1}^{N-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N-r}e^{u_{i+1,2r}-u_{i,2r-1}}\quad(j-r\mapsto i-1).

A final interchange of sum in each of the two terms leads to the right hand side of (
D.3
). This completes the verification of all three equalities.

Verification of (
3.22
).
Note that
\theta_{c}
is a function of
p
defined as a solution of the equation
\Psi^{\prime}(\theta_{c})-p\Psi^{\prime}(2\theta-\theta_{c})=0
. Set
g(p)=\theta_{c}
. Note that
g(1)=\theta
. By differentiating the equation with respect to
p
we get

g^{\prime}(p)\Psi^{\prime\prime}(g(p))-\Psi^{\prime}(2\theta-g(p))+p\Psi^{\prime\prime}(2\theta-g(p))g^{\prime}(p)=0

This implies
g^{\prime}(1)=\Psi^{\prime}(\theta)/2\Psi^{\prime\prime}(\theta)
. Since
p-1=O(N^{-1/3})
. By Taylor expansion around
1
to first three terms we get

\displaystyle(N-k)f_{\theta,p}

\displaystyle=-(N-k)\big(\Psi(g(p))+p\Psi(2\theta-g(p))\big)

\displaystyle=-(N-k)\Big(2\Psi(\theta)+(p-1)\Psi(\theta)+(p-1)^{2}\big(\Psi^{\prime\prime}(\theta)(g^{\prime}(1))^{2}-g^{\prime}(1)\Psi^{\prime}(\theta)\big)+O(N^{-1})\Big)

\displaystyle=-2N\Psi(\theta)+\frac{k^{2}(\Psi^{\prime}(\theta))^{2}}{\Psi^{\prime\prime}(\theta)(N-k)}+O(1),

where in the final line we used the fact that
p-1=2k/(N-k)
and the formula for
g^{\prime}(1)
derived above. Taking
k=MN^{2/3}
we arrive at the leading orders claimed in the first part of (
3.22
). The second part follows by observing that by Taylor expansion up to first order we have

\log\sigma_{\theta,p}=\log\sigma_{\theta,1}+O(p-1)=\log\sigma_{\theta,1}+O(N^{-1/3}).

Thus,
\sigma_{\theta,p}/\sigma_{\theta,1}=\sigma_{\theta,p}/(-\Psi^{\prime\prime}(\theta))^{1/3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N\to\infty}}{{\to}}1,
proving the second claim in (
3.22
).
